# Myne — Threat Model v0.1

> **Status: draft.** This document defines the adversaries Myne defends against, the assets it protects, the trust boundaries it draws, and — critically — what it does **not** protect against. Marketing copy must not exceed the guarantees in this document. When the architecture changes, this document changes in the same PR.

---

## 1. Adversary Classes

Each adversary class is assigned one of four status levels:

- **🛡️ Strong guarantee** — The architecture makes the attack technically impossible. Even with full server compromise, source code, and operator cooperation, the attacker cannot succeed.
- **🔒 Mitigated** — The architecture makes the attack significantly harder, but does not provide an absolute guarantee. Specific failure modes are documented.
- **⚠️ Best-effort** — The attack is acknowledged as feasible. We minimize damage and surface area, but defending fully is outside the scope of cryptography.
- **❌ Out of scope** — We do not claim protection against this adversary in v1. The user owns this risk.

**2026-09-28 A4 / TB4 transport amendment (myne#786).** The native certificate-pin
claims in the A4 and TB4 rows below, and in their repeated §4/§6 explanations,
describe the retired mechanism. They are superseded by this rule: desktop and
mobile verify public-CA chains and the requested hostname using bundled Mozilla
roots on every TLS connection, including enrollment through a Tor exit. No native
client accepts an arbitrary first certificate, uses a saved leaf pin as a trust
anchor, or asks for its fingerprint. The browser still uses its browser trust
store; the separate web-bundle delivery exposure A15/TB11 remains. A4 is
**Mitigated, not Strong** for native clients, and **four** residuals are owned by
that rating rather than rounded away:

1. **A compromised or compelled public CA** can issue an attacker a certificate
   these clients accept, which TOFU continuity after a legitimate first connect
   would have refused.
2. **A root distrusted after a mis-issuance stays trusted until Myne ships a
   release.** The bundle is a `const` array compiled into the binary, so a CA a
   browser has already stopped trusting keeps authenticating sync servers here
   for as long as this `webpki-roots` version is pinned. This is not the same
   residual as (1): nothing needs to be compromised or compelled, and nothing
   fails loudly.
3. **Revocation is never checked.** rustls builds a
   `WebPkiServerVerifier` with an empty CRL list, `UnknownStatusPolicy::Allow`,
   and a stapled OCSP response that is traced and not enforced. A leaf its issuer
   has revoked is still accepted until it expires.
4. **A private-CA or self-signed certificate is refused on the first
   connection.** A self-hoster serving on a private network, an internal hostname
   or a bare IP has no publicly trusted certificate available, and the
   previously retired claim that "every endpoint, hosted or self-hosted, is
   TOFU-pinned" no longer holds in either direction. Hosted and self-host are no
   longer symmetric in what they require of the operator, which §3 TB5's
   "treated identically" and §8's "first-class" now have to be read against; see
   `self-host-policy.md` §6 for what such an operator is to do.

Invalid chains, expired certificates and wrong hostnames fail closed, and a
refusal is reported as a TLS failure with its own copy rather than as a generic
transport error. The TLS-exporter binding remains inert behind a
TLS-terminating proxy, as before. This supersession does not affect the
vault-head bootstrap fingerprint used for device approval, and it does not reach
§2's asset roster, which is amended separately below.

| ID | Adversary | Status | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| A1 | Passive cloud provider (the operator of the sync server, whoever runs it) | 🛡️ Strong | Server stores only opaque ciphertext blobs and key-wrapping material. Plaintext, decryption keys, and master passwords never leave the client. |
| A2 | Compelled lawful access (subpoena, court order, national security letter) | 🛡️ Strong | The server cannot disclose what it does not possess. Account number, ciphertext blobs, sync timestamps, and IP addresses at connection time may be disclosed; note plaintext, titles, and structure cannot. |
| A3 | Passive network attacker (ISP, Wi-Fi sniffer) | 🛡️ Strong | TLS in transit plus client-side E2EE before transit. The attacker sees only encrypted bytes between encrypted bytes. |
| A4 | Active network attacker (MitM, TLS downgrade, BGP hijack) | 🔒 Mitigated (native clients) · ⚠️ Best-effort (web client) | TLS with a strict configuration plus public-CA chain and server-name verification (myne#786). A successful active MitM still cannot decrypt notes. It cannot deliver an unsigned client update: a client now fetches one (TB7, since 2026-10-07), and that fetch is refused unless the artifact's signature verifies against the offline release key. What it can attempt is A9's territory: serving a malicious artifact to someone downloading at that moment, and, on the web client, a substituted bundle on any page load (A15). **Per client:** a native client verifies the chain against bundled Mozilla roots on EVERY connection, the first one included, so the MitM resistance in this row is the CA set's and not a leaf pin's. **That is a real weakening, and it is the deliberate cost of `decisions/sync/0620`:** a compromised or compelled public CA can now issue a certificate these clients accept, where a TOFU pin refused any second certificate until the user replaced the pin out of band. Invalid chains, expired certificates and wrong hostnames fail closed, and a refusal is reported as a TLS failure with its own copy rather than as a generic transport error. **A pin mismatch is no longer a failure class at all:** it was one from 2026-08-24 (entry 0531), and a class named for a mechanism that no longer exists would send a user to a remedy that cannot work. **Channel binding is wired but inert:** the SCRAM `-PLUS` `tls-exporter` binding engages only when the server itself terminates TLS 1.3, and behind a terminating reverse proxy both ends bind to empty, so the deployed posture is SCRAM-SHA-256 over verified TLS (`decisions.md` 2026-07-13). Neither has had its primitive-level security review, so no surface presents certificate trust as *verified*. **The web client can do neither, permanently:** a browser exposes neither TLS-exporter keying material nor the raw certificate to page JavaScript, so a web session rests on the browser's own trust store and nothing more. A MitM against the *sync* origin still sees only ciphertext; a MitM against the *bundle* origin is A15, not this row. **The vault-head bootstrap fingerprint is a different mechanism and is untouched:** it is a device-approval SAS over a content hash, not a certificate, and it survives this change.
| A5 | Lost or stolen device (locked) | 🛡️ Strong | Master key is derived via Argon2id from a password the device does not store. With the password absent, ciphertext on disk is opaque. **One exception, PIN quick-unlock (see §7):** enabling the PIN provider writes a third wrapped copy of `root_key` to the same disk (`vault.quickunlock`, `spec/protocol.md` §9.14), guarded by a ≥6-digit PIN instead of by the master password, so a copied disk is open to offline brute-force at PIN entropy × one Argon2id evaluation per candidate. The failed-attempt counter bounds online guessing only. This rating describes a vault with PIN quick-unlock off (the default) or using the hardware-bound biometric provider, whose wrap key is non-exportable. **A second, much narrower exception (the cached vault name; see §7, myne-md/myne#411):** for every vault this device has unlocked at least once, that vault's **name** is cached at rest in `vault.label` (`spec/protocol.md` §9.22), sealed under an OS-keychain item (`md.myne.vaultlabel`, `AccessibleAfterFirstUnlockThisDeviceOnly`, no user-presence flag). Whoever can satisfy the **OS account credential** on the recovered device can therefore read those names, which the vault's master password does not guard, exactly as the `vault.quickunlock` carve-out above describes for its own key. **The rating does not move**, and the reason is the asset rather than the mechanism: what this yields is a name the user chose for a vault, not `root_key`, not a note, not a title, and it opens no ciphertext. Turning off the cache is the device-wide hide-vault-names option (§7 records its four limits). **A third exception, member vaults only (see §7, `decisions/protocol/0653`):** a B2B member who sets a device-local shared-vault password writes a copy of that vault's `group_key`, `items_key` and their person keypair to the same disk (`vault.member-unlock`), guarded by the member-chosen password rather than the recovery phrase, so that vault's disk is offline-brute-forceable at password entropy × one Argon2id evaluation per candidate. As with PIN quick-unlock, the rating describes a member vault whose device password is unset, which is no longer the default: since `decisions/ui/0668` accepting an invite on either client requires a device password, so a member vault's disk carries this copy unless the member clears it afterwards in Settings (`sync_person_clear_member_unlock`, §7). The biometric provider is not a member-vault option yet. **Amended 2026-10-30 (myne-md/myne#938 task 12, `decisions/protocol/0763`), and this one moves the rating in the safe direction.** A member vault used to carry a SECOND wrapped copy of its keys, `vault.member-key`, sealed under a KEK derived from the recovery phrase, so a copied disk was also open to anyone holding 24 words. That store is retired rather than kept beside the password gate: two credentials for one vault means the weaker gate decides, and the weaker gate was the phrase, which is also the member's identity root. A member now has exactly one way in and it is the password, PIN or biometric above, and a stale `vault.member-key` left by an older build is **deleted** at the next member unlock rather than ignored, because leaving it keeps a second, unrotated copy of `group_key` and `itemsKey` on disk with nothing that reads it. The consequence is stated where it belongs rather than here: a member's second device now needs a fresh invite, never a phrase. That sentence narrowed on 2026-10-31, when the second door named in `0763` (the device-admission ceremony) was withdrawn from both clients by `decisions/protocol/0764`; it reads as a narrowing rather than as a description of how things always were, because `0763` was written the day before with two doors open. |
| A6 | Lost or stolen device (unlocked, app open) | 🔒 Mitigated | Auto-lock on idle bounds the exposure window: after a configurable period of machine inactivity, Myne deterministically zeroizes the in-memory keys (no further notes decryptable) and tears the currently-open plaintext out of the application (a synchronous OS-level window hide plus a best-effort WebView reload). An attacker who takes the device *during* the active window, before idle-lock fires, still sees an unlocked vault — auto-lock narrows the window, it does not close it. Residual freed-page memory, swap, and hibernation are **not** scrubbed and remain A7/A10 territory; this is deliberately not a 🛡️ Strong guarantee against forensic RAM capture. See §6 and `spec/recovery-flow.md` §3.5. **Quick-unlock after a process restart (MYND-641):** a **PIN** quick-unlock credential is suppressed until one full master-password unlock has happened in the current process, so a restart cannot hand the vault back on six digits guarded only by our own five-attempt counter. A **biometric** credential is not so gated: its wrap key is held by the OS under `kSecAccessControlBiometryCurrentSet` + `kSecAttrAccessibleWhenUnlockedThisDeviceOnly` (`protocol.md` §9.14), so reading it already requires a Secure-Enclave-backed biometric prompt, it is non-exportable, and the OS invalidates it outright when the enrolled biometric set changes — a gate that survives a restart on its own. The honest consequence is that on a device whose owner's biometric an attacker can satisfy (coercion, a co-enrolled face/finger), a restart is no longer a second barrier; that was already true while the process lived, and it is the same trade every biometric-unlock app makes. It does **not** widen A6 for a lost device with no usable biometric. **One bounded exception — a running import (myne-md/myne#223):** while the importer is actively ingesting, it takes a short, renewable lease that **defers** the idle lock. Importing a large vault takes far longer than the default timeout and the machine is genuinely idle throughout — the user is watching a progress bar — so locking mid-run destroyed the import rather than protecting anything the user had not already left on screen. The lease **does not touch the idle clock**: idle keeps accumulating underneath, so the tick after the lease lapses locks a vault whose owner really did walk away, and the deferral is bounded to a 60-second ceiling per renewal (a dead WebView stops renewing and the vault locks within a minute). It is desktop-only, plugin-scoped, and visible — an import modal is on screen the whole time. The residual is that a **compromised WebView** could renew the lease indefinitely and so outlive the idle window; that is A7, already out of scope, and such an attacker can already read every note through the unlocked slot. This narrows nothing about a stolen device that is not mid-import. **Per client:** the mitigation above describes the native clients, which observe OS-level idle. The **web client is weaker**: a page can observe only its own input events and visibility, so "idle" there means "no interaction with this tab" — a user who leaves the machine while typing in another window is not idle to it. Browsers also throttle timers in background tabs, so the timer that fires the lock may fire late or not until the tab is foregrounded again. The web client therefore narrows a *smaller* window than the desktop does, and says so rather than presenting the two as equivalent. **Amended 2026-08-21 (myne-md/myne#540) — what the web lock can fail at, because two documents had it backwards.** `src/App.tsx`'s idle handler and `decisions/clients/0519` both recorded the web lock as *best-effort*, on the reasoning that a rejected lock IPC leaves the slot unlocked and routes the user to a screen still holding live keys. That failure is **not reachable**: `lock_with_state` takes the slot and returns `Ok(())` with no fallible step after the take, and `lock_from_host`'s only `?` is on that call, so the command cannot report failure while leaving the slot unlocked. On web the reachable rejections are a **fatal Worker** — which is the side holding the keys, so its death is the event the lock existed to force — plus `unsupported` (held closed by `scripts/check-web-command-coverage.mjs`) and a structured-clone throw while posting (this command takes no payload). Desktop's auto-lock does not come through that path at all: it is the Rust timer in `auto_lock.rs`, which drops the slot backend-side. So **no per-client best-effort qualifier is owed**, and understating a guarantee is recorded here as the same defect as overstating one. **The residual that is real is narrower and is now smaller.** `root_key` and `items_key` live in the Worker's wasm linear memory, and a wasm panic **aborts without unwinding**, so no `Zeroizing` `Drop` runs. `declareFatal` (`web/src/worker/host.ts`) used to mark the instance dead and reject every caller **without terminating the Worker**, and `fatal` is never cleared, so that memory stayed allocated in the page — reachable by nothing, scrubbed by nothing — until the user reloaded or closed the tab, which every broken UI path was pushing them toward without anything making it happen. It terminates now, on all four death routes, with a per-route test. **State exactly what that buys and no more:** terminating releases the allocation, it does not zeroize it, so the bytes move from a live allocation into freed memory, which is the freed-page case this row already places in A7/A10 and does not claim to scrub. **A vault import widens this row's window, and the widening is stated rather than implied (myne-md/myne#464).** During an import from another local Myne vault, TWO vaults' key material is resident: the target's in its `Slot::Unlocked` entry, and the source's in a separate, non-`Unlocked` `Slot::ImportSource` entry keyed by the source's own `VaultId`. That is not an unlock and cannot become one (every editor command matches `Slot::Unlocked` and rejects the variant at compile time, and SYNC-1's at-most-one-`Unlocked` invariant is untouched), but for the purposes of THIS row it is a second set of keys in RAM. The window is not bounded by the idle timer that bounds everything else here: `import_set_hold` (myne-md/myne#223) deliberately DEFERS idle auto-lock for the length of an import, precisely so a long import is not interrupted by a machine the user is not touching. So during exactly the interval when a second vault is exposed, the reclaimer this row rests on is switched off. Three bounds replace it, and none substitutes for another: the source slot is dropped and zeroized on the target vault's lock path, immediately after the target's own keys; a 30-minute TTL, INDEPENDENT of the import hold, is swept unconditionally on every auto-lock tick, so an import that hung or was abandoned cannot hold a second vault's keys open indefinitely; and the count is **at most one source slot**, enforced at the write rather than assumed. That last bound is stated separately because it was not held when this row was first drafted: the caller's precondition was keyed on the SOURCE id, so it refused re-opening the same source and permitted a different one, and N distinct ids gave N key-holding slots against a row that says TWO vaults' key material. It was also checked at a different program point from the write, with a `read_meta` and a full 256 MiB Argon2id in between and no lock held, so a lock or an idle auto-lock landing in that window cleared a slot that did not exist yet and the write then minted one into a process the user was looking at as locked. Both are now checked inside `set_import_source`, under the same write guard that inserts, so the check and the write are one operation and the sentence above is a bound rather than an intention. **A7 is unchanged by this feature** and is not re-argued here: an attacker with live process-memory access already defeats client-side encryption for one vault, and reading two is not a new capability. |
| A7 | Malware on the user's device | ⚠️ Best-effort | Live malware running with user privileges defeats any client-side encryption — keyloggers capture passwords, screen scrapers capture decrypted plaintext. At-rest encryption protects against *dormant* malware that finds disk artifacts but does not run actively. We do not claim protection against active compromise. Some defense-in-depth via native screen-capture exclusion — see §6. |
| A8 | Compromised dependency / supply chain (npm, Go modules) | ⚠️ Best-effort | Direct-dependency hash-pinning, lockfiles checked in, minimal dependency tree, and dependency-update reviews. Cannot fully defend against a compromised transitive dependency that ships malicious code. |
| A9 | Compromised build or distribution channel | ⚠️ Best-effort | **This row said "Reproducible builds, code-signed releases" until 2026-08-25 and had neither on desktop** (entry 0534, completing the sweep 0496 began in §4 and §6). What holds: a minisign-signed `SHA256SUMS` per release whose key is held offline, the same hashes cross-published on a second origin, and, since 2026-10-07, a signature-gated auto-update channel whose artifacts must verify against that offline key before they install. The macOS `.dmg` is now signed with a Developer ID and notarized (since 2026-10-07) and the Android `.apk` is OS-verified; Windows and the Linux packages carry no signature any OS checks. Verification is manual and opt-in, so a user who downloads and runs without checking is protected by none of it. See §4 A9 and TB7. |
| A10 | State-level actor (zero-day capabilities, supply-chain access, coercion of operators) | ⚠️ Best-effort | We use boring, well-vetted primitives and publish the spec. We do not claim protection against nation-state adversaries with zero-day capabilities, hardware implants, or the legal power to compel operator cooperation beyond what A2 covers. |
| A11 | Insider threat (Myne team member acting maliciously) | 🛡️ Strong (native clients) · ⚠️ Best-effort (web client) | **Native clients — 🛡️ Strong.** The architecture grants the team no more access than any external attacker. Insiders cannot decrypt notes because the keys never leave the client. The release-signing key is the one privileged asset, meaning the offline minisign key over each release's hash list plus the Android release keystore; see A9 and TB8. (This row said "code-signing keys" until 2026-08-25 and there is no desktop code-signing key: entry 0534.) **Web client — ⚠️ Best-effort.** A served bundle grants exactly the extra access the Strong claim denies: whoever operates the bundle origin can serve modified JavaScript to one chosen user and the honest bundle to everyone else, and that code runs with the master password typed into it. This is the whole of A15; the qualification is stated here so the Strong guarantee is not read across a client where it is false. |
| A12 | Coerced user ("rubber-hose" / give-me-your-password) | ❌ Out of scope | No plausibly-deniable encryption in v1. If a user is compelled to enter their password, the attacker gets the data. |
| A13 | Traffic analysis (server or network observer correlates sync timing, blob sizes, account activity) | ⚠️ Best-effort | Sync metadata, timing, blob count, blob size, IP at connection, is visible to the server and to anyone observing the network. Padding, cover traffic, and timing randomization are deferred beyond v1. Since the next release a live-awareness stream (`/sync/awareness`, §7, asset AS24) additionally exposes active-note presence and active-edit timing, a sharper signal in the same class; the rating does not move. |
| A14 | Compromised or malicious browser-extension build (auto-pushed malicious update, or a co-resident malicious page/extension) | 🔒 Mitigated | The clipper extension is a separately-distributed artifact with its own web-store auto-update supply chain (distinct from the desktop's TB7/TB8; cf. the Cyberhaven / Trust Wallet 2024–2025 extension-compromise incidents). A compromised build is keyless — it holds no root_key/item_key and cannot read or decrypt the vault — and its bridge token is append-only create-clip, so it cannot modify or delete existing notes. Worst case: it writes attacker-chosen or spam clip notes, which the desktop renders safe-by-construction (DOMPurify allowlist, no script execution, no core-side fetch) and surfaces visibly. Browser extension isolation prevents co-resident pages/other extensions from reading the clipper's data. Residual (not defended): a compromised build writing misleading content the user then trusts — bounded (no exfiltration, no deletion, no decryption). |
| A15 | Hostile delivery of the client code itself — targeted substitution of a served web bundle | ⚠️ Best-effort — **web client only** | The web client is re-delivered from an origin on every page load. Whoever operates that origin can serve one identified account a modified bundle and every other account the honest one, with nothing in the browser that checks a signature and nothing left on disk to compare afterwards. The modified bundle runs in the same page the user types the master password into, so this is a plaintext-and-key compromise, not a denial of service. **This is not A9.** A9 covers a build/distribution channel whose artifacts are hash-published and *installed* — the user's copy persists across the attack and can be inspected. Here there is no signature the browser verifies, no artifact the user keeps, and the copy is discarded at every reload. It is a separate boundary (TB11), not a variant of TB7. **What we do about it is detection, not prevention:** the bundle is reproducible from source, every released bundle's hashes go to an append-only record (**not a public one**, which this row said until 2026-08-25: it is in a private repository, so the third party it exists for cannot read it, entry 0534), and a service worker **will** pin the code after first load, accepting a replacement only when its hash is signed by the release key and present in that record (policy fixed in `decisions.md` 2026-07-26; **not yet implemented** — no service worker ships today, so nothing currently pins a loaded bundle). That will make the web *update* channel structurally equivalent to TB7 — **once it ships, every load after the first is as strong as desktop; only the first load is exposed** — with one limit that does not go away even then: browsers fetch the service-worker script itself from the network rather than from its own cache, so an operator controlling the origin can serve a replacement worker that omits the verification. It will raise the cost and leave an artifact trail; it does not make substitution impossible. None of that closes *targeted first delivery* — the case where the very first bundle a user receives is the malicious one — and the product ships with no in-app disclosure of the weakened posture. **Self-hosting removes this adversary for the self-hoster's own use**: someone who builds and serves their own bundle is their own code-delivery operator, so there is no third party in that path at all. It does **not** extend to other people using that instance — for them the self-hoster is the code-delivery operator, exactly as any operator is for the instance they serve. Native desktop and mobile clients are unaffected (TB7). |
| A16 | Unprivileged same-machine peer — another local process, or a different local user, reaching a user-installed AI runtime's unauthenticated loopback listener | ❌ Out of scope | Ollama binds `127.0.0.1:11434` with no authentication by default, so any process on the machine can query it, and on a multi-user machine that includes a different uid. Once the user enables the local-provider path (TB12), note plaintext transits a socket Myne neither opens nor guards. **Why this is its own row rather than A7.** A7 is *malware that already controls the process layer* — an attacker who has that does not need this door. A16 is the weaker and more ordinary case: an unprivileged peer with no special access, which A7's "we do not defend against attackers who already control the process" sentence does not cover. This shape was previously absent from the roster entirely (myne#380/#343 record the gap); the local-provider work is what makes it reachable, so it is named here. **What Myne does about it: nothing, and it says so.** The listener's bind address and auth policy belong to a daemon Myne does not ship, so there is no control to apply — only disclosure, an opt-in default of off, and the loopback-literal endpoint validation that at least keeps Myne from widening the door itself. A user on a shared machine should not enable the local-provider path. |
| A17 | The AI provider operator itself — an honest-but-curious or compelled party that legitimately receives the plaintext it was sent | ❌ Out of scope | Distinct from A4 (network attacker) and from A1/A2 (the sync server): this party is *supposed* to receive the data, so no transport or storage control touches it. Whatever a remote provider does with a request after receiving it — retain it, log it, train on it, forward it to a model vendor, hand it to a court — is outside anything Myne can verify or constrain, and OpenRouter's brokered shape means even "who receives this" is a set Myne can bound only at the first hop. **The mitigation is that this adversary only exists if the user summons it:** TB13 is opt-in, default off, requires naming a provider and passing a consent screen, is barred from every surface that fires without the user asking, and is limited to user-initiated work. `ai_excluded_folders` remains the content-scoped control. **Myne makes no claim on the provider's behalf** — see §7 and `decisions.md` 2026-08-09; a claim about a third party's retention would be a claim we cannot check. |
| A18 | Credential phishing of the recovery phrase, a lookalike origin that asks the user for their 24 words and account number | ⚠️ Best-effort, **web client only**, and now disclosure-only (see the containment clause below) | Until 2026-08-15 this adversary had no move, and the roster had no row for it, because of a **structural** property rather than a control: the recovery phrase was never generated by, displayed by or typed into served code (`decisions.md` 2026-07-26), so any web page asking for it was self-evidently fake. That inference needed no judgement, no address-bar inspection and no security literacy, and it was the strongest anti-phishing property in this design. `decisions.md` **2026-08-15, entry 0514** ships phrase display and phrase entry on the web client and therefore **spends it, permanently**; **2026-10-18, entry 0704** retires the one restriction 0514 kept back, so the phrase-credential enrolment grid ships too and no phrase path is absent from served code. What an attacker gains is total. **On `MYV1`/`NTV5`/`NTV6`**: `GET /vault.meta` is a pre-auth route, so the account number alone fetches the header, and the phrase decrypts `enc_root_key_recovery` offline into `root_key`, which yields `scram_secret`. **On `NTV7`** there is no recovery wrap to decrypt and the header is addressed by a random namespace id, so the chain is the identity one: the phrase derives the person keypair, opens the person wrap of every vault the account owns, and `GET /b2b/vaults` enumerates them. Either way a recovery-path device **self-approves** (`server-protocol.md` §3.1a), and the honest client's pending gate is a posture the server cannot enforce. Phrase plus account number is immediate, complete and permanent account takeover, and `protocol.md` §7 gives it no in-app remedy: the phrase survives every password change and no client has a rotation command. **The content half of that chain is now closed for vaults this version writes, and the identity half is not (amended 2026-10-30, myne-md/myne#938, `decisions/protocol/0758`).** On an `NTV8` vault there is no recovery wrap AND no person wrap: `root_key` is sealed under the password wrap alone, so a phished phrase no longer reaches a note on any vault the client writes, and neither does the person keypair it derives. What a phished phrase still buys is the identity: register a device as that person (self-approved), enumerate every vault the account owns by name, sign as them, and receive future shares. It does not open one of them, because each still needs its own password, which the phrase does not derive and the server does not hold. **This narrows the row's impact and does not move its rating.** The rating is about the credential class and the containment, and the containment is unchanged: there is still no situation a user can name as the one Myne asks in, still no in-app remedy, still no rotation. What changed is the blast radius, from "everything that person owns" to "who that person is", and a reader who took the older sentence as a description of today's client would overstate it in the alarming direction, which is the one direction this document must not be wrong in. **Amended 2026-11-03 (myne-md/myne#1003): which radius applies to a vault is now user-publishable.** The diagnostics block a user copies after a sync failure carries the vault's `vault.meta` generation, and that is the field this row's own radius turns on: `MYV1`, `NTV5` and `NTV6` carry a recovery wrap an offline phrase attack reaches, and `NTV8` carries neither wrap. A paste therefore names, in whatever public issue it lands in, which of the two radii that user faces. It carries no identifier, no path, no key material and no content, and it is user-initiated per press, so no rating moves; the disclosure is stated because a reader of this row is the one who would act on it. **This adversary needs no bundle compromise**, so it is not A15 and not A4, and every control built for those, reproducible builds, the published bundle-hash record, the strict CSP, certificate pinning, is irrelevant to it. **The identity phrase joined this row on 2026-10-22 (myne-md/myne#911 task 13), and the cost is stated rather than implied.** The web client now creates, registers and unlocks a PERSON identity, so the phrase a lookalike origin asks for is no longer only a vault's recovery credential: it is the account's, the one no password change rotates, and the one that opens every vault the person owns. Two things narrow it, and neither is containment. The phrase is asked for on the web only where the user is deliberately doing an identity action (creation, its type-back, the recovery door, registration, or a device join), and the ceremony's phrase step carries the platform caveats. And a browser keeps **no person credential at rest**: the sealed keypair and the device token live in the Worker's memory for the session (`web/wasm/src/person_io.rs`), so there is no phrase-encrypted file in an origin's storage for an attacker to crack offline and no token to steal from disk, at the cost of the 24 words being re-entered after every reload, which the phrase step says out loud (`personCredentialPersisted` is absent on this client and is what the copy keys on). That absence is a decision, not a limitation: the alternative stored a phrase-derived keypair in origin storage, which is the offline cracking target this row's sibling `GET /vault.meta` already is, in a place any script on the origin can read. **What remains is disclosure, not containment**, and the containment this row used to claim is **retired** (`decisions.md` 2026-10-18, entry 0704). It read: the phrase is asked for in one situation a routine flow never stages, a recovery the user started themselves, plus the recovery drill (`decisions/security/0640`), which on web is a second entry point bounded by the unlock gate (it answers nothing while the vault is locked) and by the shared single-permit auth budget, while phrase-credential *device enrolment* remained a decided absence kept out of the bundle by build-time substitution and an artefact grep. Entry 0747 (myne-md/myne#935) has since withdrawn the drill, so that half of the clause is history rather than description. Entry 0704 retires the enrolment absence, so there is no longer a situation a user can name as the one Myne asks in: the rule this row stated, **never to add a device and never to sign in**, is gone, and with it the only control here a user could apply without judging an address bar. What is left is the disclosure each surface carries (the flows state what the phrase is for) plus the origin's HSTS preload, which binds the HONEST origin to TLS and says nothing about a lookalike, so it belongs with the controls listed above as irrelevant to this adversary rather than with anything that bears on it. **The rating does not move**, and the reason is that it was already this roster's weakest tier rather than that anything was replaced: an adversary at best-effort does not become a different tier by losing a control, and writing a stronger rating here would be the overstatement this row exists to avoid. **The rating's meaning does narrow**: read it as disclosure-only, with no rule a user can apply. None of that stops a user who types 24 words into a homoglyph domain. Native clients are unaffected, and the reason is not a signature: they display and accept the phrase inside an application the user installed, so there is no origin to impersonate and no address bar to read correctly. **The mechanism this sentence claimed until 2026-08-25 does not exist** (entry 0534): no desktop artifact carries a signature any OS checks and nothing verifies one at launch (§1 A9, TB7). What defeats this adversary on a native client is that the client is not fetched from an origin at all. |
| A19 | A browser extension holding host permissions on the web client's origin | ❌ Out of scope, **web client only** | An extension the user has installed with access to the origin reads the page's DOM, including the recovery-phrase grid at creation, the 24 cells at recovery, and the master-password field at every unlock. No content-security policy addresses this: CSP governs what the *page* may load, not what an extension with host permissions may read out of it. **This is not A14**, which is about Myne's own clipper extension being compromised and is bounded by that extension being keyless; here the extension is a third party's, it is not part of Myne's design, and it reads plaintext directly. **It predates the 2026-08-15 change**, the master password has always been typed into this DOM, and that change raises the stakes rather than creating the exposure, because the phrase is the one credential a password change does not rotate. **There is no control to apply.** A page cannot detect, refuse or shield itself from an extension the user has granted access to. What ships is disclosure at the CREATION phrase step only, and the standing answer that a native client has no equivalent surface. **Amended 2026-10-22 (myne-md/myne#911 task 13): the identity ceremony's phrase step carries the same caveats**, because the person account's phrase is the only recovery credential the product still issues and the step that shows it owes exactly what the vault kit owes (the clipboard notice, the extension notice, and, new with this task, the session-only notice below). That closes the gap this row named for the one phrase surface the row's own list did not have yet; the recovery entry screen and the unlock password field still carry none, and closing those is still owed. Stated that narrowly on purpose: the row names three reads and the notice reaches one of them. The recovery entry screen carries none, and its users are by definition people who did not create their account in this browser, so they never saw the creation step; the unlock password field carries none either. Closing that gap is owed and is not claimed here: nothing can inject into the desktop WebView. A user who does not trust every extension installed in a browser profile should create and recover in a native client, or use a clean profile for those steps. |

---

## 2. Assets

Each asset is classified by sensitivity and by where it is visible (client only, client + server-as-ciphertext, server-as-plaintext, or transient).

| ID | Asset | Sensitivity | Where it lives | Server visibility |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| AS1 | Note plaintext | Critical | Client only (decrypted in RAM) | Ciphertext blob only |
| AS2 | Note title | Critical | Inside encrypted blob | Ciphertext blob only — never extracted as a separate field |
| AS3 | Folder / hierarchy structure | Sensitive | Inside encrypted blob | Not visible. Server stores notes as a flat keyed set; structure is reconstructed client-side. |
| AS4 | Wikilinks / backlinks graph | Sensitive | Inside encrypted blob | Not visible. Graph is reconstructed client-side after decryption. |
| AS5 | Tag list | Sensitive | Inside encrypted blob | Not visible. |
| AS6 | User-meaningful timestamps (created_at, updated_at) | Sensitive | Inside encrypted blob | **Split, and the two halves differ.** `updated_at` is **not visible**: it lives inside the AEAD envelope, and the server uses an opaque per-note version counter that increments on each push — sync-internal state, not a user timestamp, revealing that *something* changed, not when. **`created_at` is visible.** It has no encrypted carrier: a note's `created_at` is derived from the timestamp embedded in its UUIDv7 (`protocol.md` §9.1 — the `.myn` filename IS the note id, and `markdown-dialect.md` deliberately keeps a `created:` field out of frontmatter for exactly that reason). That same UUID is the sync wire key (`sync-protocol.md` §4.2), so the server holds **note creation time at millisecond resolution, durably, in its per-vault index** — not merely observed in passing. Accepted for v0.1; see §7 and the A2 row. The two code-side alternatives (UUIDv4 ids, or a keyed per-note sync-id) are both major-version changes, not doc patches — §7 records why. **Amended 2026-09-05 (myne-md/myne#675):** conversation ids are a second UUIDv7 family with the identical property. `chats/<id-hex>` is the conversation id, and the per-file sync blob-id is the conversation id's own bytes (`sync-protocol.md` §10.2.5, `protocol/src/subkey.rs`; no separate sync-id role, following the attachment-id pattern), so the server holds per-conversation creation time at millisecond resolution, durably, as a second series beside the note series. Same acceptance as notes (accepted for v0.1); same alternatives-are-major-version consequence. Stored and compellable like the note series (A2/A13): a request for "when did conversations start" is answerable off the index without touching ciphertext. **Amended 2026-09-17 (myne-md/myne#697):** memory entry ids are a third UUIDv7 family with the identical property. `memories/<id-hex>` is the entry id, and the per-file sync blob-id is the entry id's own bytes (`sync-protocol.md` §10.2.6), so the server holds per-entry creation time at millisecond resolution, durably, as a third series beside the note and conversation series. Same acceptance as notes (accepted for v0.1); same alternatives-are-major-version consequence. |
| AS7 | Attachment content (images, SVG, PDFs, audio) | Critical | Encrypted attachment blob (`MYA1`, `protocol.md` §9.9), separate from notes; per-attachment key wrapped by `itemsKey`. **Audio** (voice notes, MYND-259) is captured natively in Rust into a RAM-only `Zeroizing` buffer (never a plaintext temp file) and encrypted through this same `MYA1` path before any disk write; the **microphone is a new ingress** — unlock-gated, explicit-gesture, visible-recording-UI (no covert activation), aborted on lock / auto-lock / vault-delete — and the in-RAM capture buffer plus the PCM that playback necessarily decrypts into the WebView are AS1-class in-RAM plaintext (the A6/A7/A10 forensic-RAM limitation is inherited, not widened). | Ciphertext blob only |
| AS8 | Attachment filename | Sensitive | Authoritatively in the encrypted attachments catalog (`vault.attachments`, `MYC1`, `protocol.md` §9.10), referenced from notes by a `root_key`-keyed content-addressed attachment-id; the in-note alias is a cosmetic copy | Not visible. Attachment blob size is visible (cannot avoid) — v0.1 does not pad; size-indistinguishability is deferred (A13). |
| AS9 | Account number (Mullvad-style identifier) | Pseudonymous | Server stores a hash; an identifier, NOT an authenticator (access is the SCRAM session; a person number is derived from the phrase, so it cannot be a shared secret, `decisions/protocol/0718`). Possession does select the pre-authenticated `GET /vault.meta` for a legacy namespace, which is the offline-cracking handle §5 describes; it opens nothing by itself | Hash only, **structurally enforced** (2026-07-13): the raw value never crosses the sync wire at all. The client derives its SCRAM identity as the account-hash hex inside the enrollment module (no API accepts a raw-number username), and the server rejects any register/init whose username ≠ `account_hash_hex` (`server-protocol.md` §3.1), so the persisted auth state cannot contain a raw account number even from an outdated client. Stronger than the earlier "verified once at registration and discarded": the server never sees the raw form. |
| AS10 | Master password | Critical secret | User's memory only | Never. Used only as Argon2id input on the client. |
| AS11 | Recovery phrase (BIP39) | Critical secret | User's offline storage (paper, password manager), and, when the user takes the save-to-file affordance `recovery-flow.md` §2.2 requires the creation flow to offer, a plain-text file: at a location of their choosing on desktop and on the web, and in the app's `Documents` directory on mobile, from which the platform chooser is the only route to it, because no platform offers the WebView a save dialog of its own (TB3). On Android the same save also lands wherever the user points the picker, which is outside both that directory and its backup exclusion (TB3, myne-md/myne#505). Written owner-only where the platform has file modes, and outside Myne's protection from the moment it lands. On mobile that directory is declared excluded from the platform's automatic cloud backup (TB3, myne-md/myne#491); phrases saved before that landed are already in existing iCloud or Google backups and nothing in the app can retract them. | Never. Server has no representation of the phrase. **Also the OS clipboard**, wherever the creation flow's Copy is taken, and for how long differs by client: desktop schedules a compare-and-clear at 30 s that dies with the process, iOS writes an item the OS expires on its own timer, Android is offered no Copy at all, and **the web client writes it with no clear scheduled** and says so beside the control (`recovery-flow.md` §2.2). On web that means an unrotatable Critical secret sits in a facility other applications read, that clipboard managers persist to disk, and that Windows Cloud Clipboard / macOS Universal Clipboard may copy to another device, until the user copies something else. The affordance ships there anyway because removing it does not remove copying, only makes it manual over a numbered grid, and web is also the client with no screen-capture protection to fall back on; the reasoning per platform is in `recovery-flow.md` §2.2 and the split is deliberate rather than an oversight. |
| AS12 | Root encryption key | Critical secret | Client RAM only | Never. Derived locally from the vault password via Argon2id + HKDF. **Amended 2026-10-30 (myne-md/myne#938, `decisions/protocol/0758`): the parenthetical "(or recovery phrase)" is retired.** An `NTV8` vault seals `root_key` under the password wrap alone, with no recovery wrap and no person wrap in the frame, so no phrase derives it, and an `NTV8` vault keeps **exactly one** wrapped copy of this asset on disk: the password wrap. The only other copies anywhere are the two optional device-local ones the user may create (the PIN or biometric quick-unlock copy in `vault.quickunlock`, and a member vault's password-sealed copy in `vault.member-unlock`), and neither exists on a vault the user has not opted into. The phrase-sealed member store that used to hold a third (`vault.member-key`) is deleted (`decisions/protocol/0763`). Legacy generations keep their copies and stay readable forever; the phrase path they carry is history rather than a guarantee. |
| AS13 | Server-auth secret | Sensitive | Derived client-side via HKDF; server stores Argon2id verifier | Verifier hash only — raw secret never persisted server-side |
| AS14 | IP address at sync time | Metadata | Transient — server connection only | **Not logged.** No persistent storage of source IPs. TLS termination sees the IP for the duration of the request and discards it. **When the desktop's opt-in Tor routing mode is on** (§7), the address the server observes for the duration of the request is a Tor exit node's rather than the user's — a client-side reduction of what is exposed here, not a change to what the server does with it. |
| AS15 | Sync timing (when device X synced) | Metadata | Transient on the wire; two hourly-coarse epoch-ms fields in that device's own `sync-state.json` | Visible during the request; **not persistently logged on the server or on the wire**. The version counter (AS6) reveals that *some* sync happened, but not when. **Amended 2026-11-05 (myne-md/myne#1027, `decisions/sync/0787`): the "not persistently logged" clause was already untrue on the device, not only by this change.** Two device-local clocks are kept in that device's own plaintext `sync-state.json`: `last_success_at_ms` (myne#583) and `last_upload_at_ms` (myne-md/myne#1027). Both are **hourly-coalesced**, so each STORED value moves at most once an hour. That bounds the two values and nothing else, and an earlier draft of this amendment claimed more: **the file's own mtime already advances once per successful cycle**, because the same cycle persists its advanced cursor on success (`sync/orchestrator.rs`, "Persist the advanced cursor ... ONLY on success"), and the write is an atomic temp-and-rename with no compare. So a device running the 30-second poll leaves a once-per-cycle at-rest trace of when it was awake and online, and no coalescing of these two fields changes that. It is recorded here as a residual rather than described as bounded. Neither is ever synced, but **both ride the OS platform backup on mobile and desktop, because the vault root is deliberately inside it (TB3)**, so "not backed up" would be false. Nothing renders either clock to the user today: `last_upload_at_ms` is carried on the blocked status for the surface that will say "your work has not left this machine, and the last time it did was three hours ago", and that surface is not built yet (`0787` "Residuals"). |
| AS16 | Device count per account | Metadata | Server-side registration table | Visible — each registered device gets a server-side record (necessary for revocation). |
| AS17 | Total ciphertext storage size per account | Metadata | Server | Visible — required for quota enforcement on the paid tier. |
| AS18 | Pending watch capture (not-yet-committed note text) | Sensitive (AS1-class) | Device-local encrypted queue (`MYK1`, `protocol.md` §9.18) sealed under a device-local keychain key, `root_key`-independent; two OS-Data-Protection-only transients precede sealing (watch WatchConnectivity outbox + phone staging file). **Extended 2026-10-03:** watch meeting audio is the same asset class, held in a device-local pending-meeting manifest + sealed chunks (the `MYF1` codec at §9.20 under the share-import device key, `decisions/clients/0647`); its watch-side transient is the meeting audio held between capture and transfer, and its phone-side transient is the OS-written WatchConnectivity inbox file, held under OS Data Protection from delivery until the seal consumes it (and re-sealed by the boot / unlock inbox re-scan if the on-arrival seal fails or never runs). | Never — device-local, never synced or backed up. The server never sees a capture. |
| AS19 | Pending share-import file (not-yet-imported shared file) | Sensitive (AS1/AS7-class) | Device-local encrypted queue (`MYF1`, `protocol.md` §9.20) sealed under a device-local keychain/Keystore key, `root_key`-independent. An OS-Data-Protection-only plaintext window precedes sealing: **on iOS a persistent plaintext staging file** (bytes + `.meta` sidecar written by the Share Extension into the App-Group container, `.completeFileProtection`, sealed + deleted by the app on next open — the §9.18.4 watch-staging pattern); **on Android a RAM + WebView-transit transient** (the `content://` stream read into RAM, base64'd across the WebView, then sealed — no persistent plaintext file). The **OS share sheet is a new ingress** of attacker-influenceable bytes from an arbitrary sending app; the imported file is re-sniffed authoritatively before it enters the `MYA1` library. | Never — device-local, never synced or backed up. The server never sees a pending share. |
| AS20 | The bundled public CA root set (the certificate trust anchors every native client verifies a sync server against) | Security-critical configuration | Compiled into the binary from the `webpki-roots` crate's `const` array, pinned by the shared `Cargo.lock` (TB9). **Not** the OS trust store: a private or corporate CA installed on the user's machine is not consulted, and a root removed from a later bundle does not stop being trusted here until Myne ships a release that bumps the pin. rustls verifies with an empty CRL list and does not enforce stapled OCSP, so revocation is never checked (A4 amendment residuals 2 and 3). | Disclosed to nobody. The bundle is public data and the list of issuers a client will accept is not a secret; the *cost* is in what it is not refreshed from, which is §7's release-responsibility note rather than a disclosure one. |
| AS20 | Account labels + vault co-membership (grouping) | Sensitive | Device-local sealed record (`<app_config_dir>/account.json`) under a device-local OS-keychain wrap key, `root_key`-independent (shares the vault-label keychain service under a reserved name; `protocol.md` §9.3.1). Readable while every vault is locked, by design: the locked picker groups under it. | The record never syncs. The sealed membership field inside each vault's `vault.meta` travels as opaque bytes with the vault (it is vault content, not a second copy of the record); the server sees only the coarse consequence: `vault.meta` growing 345 to 417 bytes on first stamp plus a per-device-varying sealed field afterwards (opaque bytes, blob-size class). |
| AS21 | Person keypair (B2B member identity) | Critical (key material) | Sealed at rest in one file (`<app_config_dir>/person_keypair.sealed`, `salt(16) || kdf_params(11) || nonce(24) || ct`) under an Argon2id wrap key derived from the person's OWN recovery phrase (salt domain `"myne.v0.2 argon2id-person-keypair|"`, vault-independent; the phrase also yields the `person_id_hash` the salt binds). Vault-independent: the phrase-sealed primary store is NOT `root_key`-derived, so no vault credential can unwrap THAT copy (the `NTV6` carrier copy below is the deliberate exception). The keypair material itself is DETERMINISTICALLY derived from the phrase since `decisions/protocol/0604` (an Argon2id seed under the salt domain `"myne.v0.2 argon2id-person-identity|"`, HKDF-expanded per key type), so the sealed file is a cache of the derived key rather than its only carrier: the same phrase yields the same keypair and fingerprint on every device, and a LOST device is recoverable from the phrase alone (the identity need not be re-invited from scratch). The private seeds are held in process RAM from `sync_person_unlock` until the next vault lock: they are dropped by the manual lock, the idle auto-lock, the change-password force-relock, a vault switch through password or quick unlock, a direct create-vault switch, and vault delete. The person identity is vault-independent in scope (one person, many vaults), but session-scoped in RAM: a vault switch does not carry the private seeds into the next vault session. The residency bound is therefore "until the next vault lock, explicit person lock, or process end", never in the WebView. The KEM half is ML-KEM-768, the signature half a side-by-side Ed25519 + ML-DSA-65 hybrid. Since `decisions/protocol/0622`, an `NTV6` owner vault ALSO caches the keypair in a device-local carrier (`<app_config_dir>/person-vault-carriers/<vault_id_hex>.json`) sealed under the vault's `root_key` (`SubkeyRole::PersonVaultCarrier`), so a vault unlock installs the identity without the phrase; that carrier makes the vault credential (the password, or a quick-unlock PIN/biometric that yields `root_key`) open the person identity, and because the identity is vault-independent a leaked vault credential exposes it in the person's other vaults too (to be decoupled, myne-md/myne#793). A B2B **member** ALSO caches the keypair under their device password (`vault.member-unlock`, `decisions/protocol/0653`), which `decisions/ui/0668` makes required at accept, so a leaked member-vault password exposes the identity in the person's other vaults as well. **Amended 2026-10-30 (myne-md/myne#938 task 12, `decisions/protocol/0763`):** that is now the member's ONLY carrier of this asset besides the phrase itself. The phrase-sealed `vault.member-key` store, which also carried the keypair, is retired, so the member surface has one credential rather than two and the "leaked member-vault password exposes the identity" clause is unchanged in scope while the second door beside it is gone. **The `NTV7` owner carrier clause above is unaffected by `decisions/protocol/0758`**: the device-local `person-vault-carriers/` file is sealed under the vault's `root_key` and has nothing to do with the frame's person wrap, so dropping that wrap neither creates nor removes a copy of this asset. | Never synced. The sealed invite payload travels through the relay as opaque bytes: the outer routing frame is only the recipient person id and the vault id, and the fingerprints, the owner public half and the group-key grant ride INSIDE the AEAD-sealed payload, not in the clear. The owner's public half reaches the invitee out of band in the person identity code. Since `decisions/protocol/0675` that code ALSO crosses the server boundary on a member's second-device path: the member's own first device pins it inside that member's phrase-sealed `MYY5` profile (row AS23), and the second device fetches it back to verify the group package against it. There it rides as ciphertext under a key derived from the member's own phrase, so the server stores bytes it can neither read nor serve, and the invite relay itself still never carries it. The grant is a group package (`group_key` wrapped to the recipient), not an `itemsKey` wrap. The server stores an invite row (recipient person id, vault id, claimed flag) and, after claim, a person↔vault membership binding; it reads no payload. The private seeds never leave the device. Because the keypair is phrase-derived (`decisions/protocol/0604`), device loss IS recoverable from the phrase: the same fingerprint returns on a new device. |
| AS22 | previousKeySet rotation-visibility chain (B2B) | Low | The `previous_fingerprint_hex` field on each vault-roster member row, inside the AEAD membership envelope. Its intent is to record WHICH identity fingerprint a member's row superseded at the last rotation, so a key change after the invite-time SAS is user-visible, but NOTHING WRITES IT in this change: member removal rotates the vault's GROUP key (epoch+1) and its `itemsKey`, not any member identity, so there is no identity rotation to record yet. The field is on the schema for the later identity-re-roll feature; treat the rotation-visibility property as roadmap, not shipped. The group-key epoch itself IS user-visible (the roster carries `group_key_epoch` and each row a `continuity` state). | Travels as vault-roster content inside the synced membership envelope (opaque ciphertext to the server). The server also sees the coarse consequence: `vault.meta` growing with member count (each row carries the member's public halves, so the blob-size class already recorded for the membership stamp applies, now scaling with team size), and the `reserved 0x02` byte distinguishing a shared vault from a single-user one to anyone holding the blob. The continuous Key Transparency log that would make every change verifiable stays on the roadmap; this chain is the user-visible half. |
| AS23 | Per-person private AI profile (`MYY5`) | Critical (content) | One sealed profile per `(person, vault)` at `profiles/<profile-id>` holding that person's private AI state (chat history, agent memory, personality directive) and, since `decisions/protocol/0675`, the owner identity code a member's device pinned there at join (`protocol.md` §9.28.4), which is what lets that member's second device verify a group package against an owner it already knows. Sealed under a KEK derived from the person's OWN recovery phrase under the salt domain `"myne.v0.2 argon2id-person-vault-profile|"`, with a fixed (non-secret) salt randomizer so the profile opens on every client from the phrase alone; the `profile_id` is `SHA-256("myne.person-profile-id v1|" || person_id_hash || account_number_hash)[:16]` and is bound into the AAD together with `account_number_hash`. **Not group state:** neither the shared `group_key` nor the owner `root_key` can open it, and a different person's phrase or a different vault derives a different id AND a different key. The provider choice, the provider credential and the egress-consent record are NOT in this blob: they stay device-local (the credential in `vault.prefs` today, the consent in device-local state). The derived private profile key is held only for the active vault session and is dropped on lock, vault switch, direct create-vault switch, delete, or process end; a later member operation rehydrates it from the person's phrase. | **The plane and the AI wiring are built.** The codec, the per-`(person, vault)` key/id derivation and the sync plane ship (myne-md/myne#711 task 16): `PUT|GET /b2b/vaults/{account_hash}/profiles/{profile_id}`, authorized by the person's own account session plus a membership binding, in a vault-scoped namespace separate from the shared group manifest. A **member's** memory, conversations and personality directive are serialized into the profile rather than the shared families (design A, tasks 27-29): the local sealed container is cached at `vault.person-ai`, the profile route carries it, and a pull merges the remote container at the family layer (the catalogs' causal join with remove-wins tombstones, append-merged conversation turns, decision 0610) so a concurrent edit on another device is not dropped. An owner or single-user vault keeps the shared families and writes no profile. The server sees an opaque `profiles/<id>` record with no plaintext and no person link beyond the id it cannot invert, and records the writing account so removal can schedule deletion after the retention grace. **What DOES ship is the key**: the phrase-derived profile KEK is held in process RAM from `sync_person_unlock` until the person locks, a vault switch, vault delete or the change-password force-relock (the same residency AS21 records for its sibling key), and it is dropped on each of those paths. **Removal and deletion make no recall guarantee:** a removed member's already-copied profile is not erasable, and a scheduled deletion is a policy input (retention owned by `example-org/operations#48`), never a cryptographic recall. |
| AS24 | Live awareness state (transient collaboration presence: active-note, cursor/selection, typing) | Transient (RAM-only) | Held in process RAM only, for the duration of a live collaboration session; never persisted, never synced, never backed up. Carried as opaque E2E-sealed awareness frames (sealed under a per-vault awareness key, group-derived for `NTV6`) over the `/sync/awareness` relay. The server sees only ephemeral relay traffic scoped to a note-unlinkable per-note room id, and reads no note id, cursor or content. | Disclosed to the server only as ephemeral presence metadata: that a vault has live editing activity, and roughly when, per room (A13), never which note, where the caret is, or what is typed. Dropped on vault lock, app backgrounding, or stream teardown. |

| AS25 | Member display name (`vault.member-names`, `MYO5`) | Sensitive (member-authored, may be personal data) | Inside the group-tier-sealed `MYO5` blob (`spec/protocol.md` §9.29), one row per `person_id_hash` with each row signed by the person it NAMES; encrypted, synced Class-1, backed up. No device-local cache. | Ciphertext only. A display name is free text a member chose, so it may carry personal data the vault holds nowhere else, which is why it is enumerated as its own asset rather than folded into AS20's vault labels. A row whose signature does not verify is never rendered as a name: the unverified state and the absent state are distinguished (§9.29.4). |
| AS26 | Device-to-person bindings (`vault.device-bindings`, `MYU5`) | Sensitive (metadata: which person uses which device) | Inside the group-tier-sealed `MYU5` blob (`protocol.md` §9.30), one row per device id carrying the claiming person's fingerprint, a monotonic version and a tombstone, each row signed by the person it claims; encrypted, synced Class-1, backed up. No device-local cache of its own. | Ciphertext only. The store is addressed by the group-derived `device-binding-sync-id`, so the whole family rotates with the group key when a member is removed. A fingerprint is a stable pseudonym for a person within one vault, so the family is the vault's own device-to-member ledger rather than a new field for the server: the server sees an opaque blob whose size class scales with the device count, the same class AS16 already records. A row is NEVER rendered as a person without partition verification against the roster first (§9.30.4): an unverified row and an absent row render identically. **A verified row is a claim and not an ownership**: the signature binds the row to the person who signed it and binds nothing to the device, so any member can sign a row naming any device id, a co-member's included, under their own fingerprint, and the family's writer-chosen version lets that row outbid the real one (§9.30.3, §9.30.4). A surface that shows a person beside a device therefore renders "claimed by", never "belongs to", and the device list is exactly the surface a reader would otherwise take as an authority on who runs which machine. **Amended 2026-11-02 (myne-md/myne#976, `decisions/sync/0772`):** a refused merge now makes the client WRITE with no user act, so the bound is stated here rather than inferred. `ConflictingBinding` refuses the whole map because two copies tied one device at one version, and the device PARTY to the pair re-signs its own row at `version + 1` and lets the next merge settle it (§9.30.3); a reader whose device is not in the pair writes nothing. The write is therefore bounded on four axes and all four are properties of the code rather than of the caller: one row, this device's own, once per cycle, and only from a session that is unlocked WITH a live person identity (no session, no signature, no write). The re-sealed blob is never pushed on that path, so the server learns nothing new from a repair. What the party that triggers it can do is unchanged from the paragraph above: any group-key holder can still seal a row naming any device, and this repair does not make such a row verify. The capability this amendment adds is availability, not authority: one forged row used to freeze the store for every member, and now it costs the device inside the pair one extra write. |
| AS27 | Device names (`device_names` inside `vault.prefs`, `MYP1`) | Sensitive (a human-chosen label; may be a person's own name) | One entry per device id inside the AEAD-sealed `vault.prefs` (`spec/protocol.md` §9.5.2), a set-union map that merges **per key** on a namespaced `device_names/<key>` clock and has no deletion (`spec/sync-protocol.md` §10.2; `decisions/sync/0348` clause 3). Encrypted, synced Class-1, backed up. No device-local cache. | Ciphertext only: no name field exists in the enrollment request or the device roster, so nothing about a label reaches the server outside the blob. On a shared vault every co-member reads it, inside the group tier the notes already use. **An automatic write can now create the entry** (`decisions/sync/0770`): an approved, previously-nameless device names itself with a platform word. That value is not bounded by the backend: `set_device_name` accepts any non-blank string on both write paths and the automatic one adds only the plane, absent-blob and insert-only bounds, so `device_names` holds free text whoever writes it and is rated where AS25 is. What keeps the automatic entry non-personal is the shipped client, which sends the platform suggestion and nothing else; a client that sent something else would be a rename by another name. There is **no delete**, so a removed device's label outlives the device. |

### No new asset row for the AI provider boundaries (TB12 / TB13)

The local-runtime and remote-provider boundaries were reviewed for an asset row of their own and deliberately do not get one. What crosses them is **AS1 note plaintext** — the same asset in the same state (decrypted, in RAM, in transit to a process), differing only in *which* process receives it. Sensitivity, where it lives and server visibility are all unchanged, and those are what this table's columns record. "Which non-Myne process may receive this, and under what conditions" is a property of the boundary, and TB12/TB13 carry it. An AS20 would have been AS1's row restated with one sentence changed, and would have split every future edit across two places.

### Key consequence of an encrypted `updated_at` (AS6)

> Scope, because the old heading over-promised: this subsection is about `updated_at` only. Note **creation** time is server-visible via the UUIDv7 blob id — see the AS6 row and §7.

Standard sync architectures use the user's `updated_at` timestamp for last-write-wins conflict resolution. Because that is encrypted here, the sync server cannot make conflict decisions. Instead:

- Each note has a **sync-internal version counter** (an integer, not a wall-clock time) that the server increments when a client successfully pushes a new ciphertext blob.
- Conflicts are detected via version-vector mismatch and resolved on the **client** after decryption — typically duplicate-on-conflict, surfacing both versions to the user.
- This pattern follows the Standard Notes 004 model. It costs sync-protocol simplicity for a meaningful metadata-leak reduction.

### Key consequence of "no IP logging" (AS14)

- No traditional access logs. Operational logs (errors, rate-limit triggers, abuse heuristics) must be designed to function on **counts and timestamps without IPs** — e.g., counter-based per-account rate limits, not per-IP.
- This is technically harder for abuse mitigation (no easy IP banning) and **must** be reflected in the sync-protocol design, not retrofitted.
- The "no IP logs" claim is a hard architectural commitment. Any deviation requires this document to be amended in the same change.

## 3. Trust Boundaries

A trust boundary is a line in the system where the security model changes — what is trusted on one side is not trusted on the other. Myne's boundaries, from innermost to outermost:

| ID | Boundary | Trusted with | Crossing requires |
|---|---|---|---|
| TB1 | User's memory | Master password, identity phrase, decision to authenticate | The user's mind. If this boundary breaks (the user reveals or forgets the password and phrase), no other defense applies. **Amended 2026-10-30 (myne-md/myne#938, `decisions/protocol/0758`): the two credentials no longer fail the same way, and the difference is the point of the split.** Forgetting the **phrase** now costs the identity's convenience rather than any content: an `NTV8` vault opens with its password, and the phrase is not a second way in. Forgetting the **password** costs the vault, permanently, with no support remedy and no partial path, because the password wrap is the only copy of `root_key` on that disk. Revealing the phrase still costs the identity (every vault listed, none opened); revealing the password costs every note in that vault. The boundary is unchanged in strength and sharper in shape: it used to be "lose either and you are fine, lose both and you are not", and it is now "the password is the whole barrier for content, the phrase for who you are". |
| TB2 | User's device (kernel + user-space process) | Decrypted plaintext, root key, item keys, transient session state | OS-level isolation. We do not defend against attackers who already control the process at this layer (A6, A7). |
| TB3 | Local on-disk storage | Ciphertext blobs, salt, KDF parameters, version counters, public metadata | Argon2id derivation from the master password to obtain the root key. Disk in isolation is opaque. **Deliberate exception, user-initiated plaintext egress:** an export (plain-markdown note, vault markdown tree, PDF, attachment files), the diagnostics block a user copies after a failure (five bounded vault-state tokens plus, on desktop since myne-md/myne#991, the vault directory's entry count and total size, all enumerated in the §7 bullet that owns them, myne-md/myne#1003), or a user-initiated credential save (the plain-text recovery-kit file `recovery-flow.md` §2.2 requires the vault-creation flow to offer, which carries the recovery phrase AND the account number in ONE document (`decisions/ui/0642`)), writes plaintext across this boundary to a destination the user explicitly chose via the OS file dialog (dialog-plugin user authority). The exported artifact is outside Myne's protection from the moment it is written; export is one-shot and user-initiated, there is no plaintext mode for the vault and no automatic egress. The credential half of the exception differs from the note half in what lands, not in who authorized it: a saved phrase is a standalone credential that no password reset rotates (`recovery-flow.md` §4.4) and that has no revocation (`protocol.md` §7.5). **Amended 2026-10-30 (myne-md/myne#938, `decisions/protocol/0758`): the credential a kit carries no longer opens a vault, and the kit itself is legacy-only.** An `NTV8` vault is born without a vault phrase, so its creation shows no kit at all (`recovery-flow.md` §2 scope note); the plaintext credential save a modern flow offers is the **identity** phrase, and what it buys whoever reads the file is the person (their keypair, their account number, every vault they own by name) rather than any note. The sentence this replaces called a saved phrase "a standalone unlock path", which was true of a kit written by a vault that carries a recovery wrap or a person wrap and is false of anything this version writes. The exposure class is unchanged: it is a plaintext file at a user-chosen destination, outside every protection in this document, and the at-rest adversary (A5) reads it if they get the disk. Myne writes it owner-only, `0600` from the moment the file exists on Unix, with no equivalent on platforms that have no file modes, through a single narrow command that refuses any destination inside the vault tree, and retains no path to the file afterwards, so `recovery-flow.md` §2.4's no-re-display commitment is unaffected. **Two qualifications this exception carried implicitly and now states (myne-md/myne#491).** (1) *"The destination the user explicitly chose via the OS file dialog"* describes the desktop and the web clients. **On mobile there is no dialog inside the WebView, and where the file goes is still decided in two steps.** Neither platform offers the WebView a save dialog of its own, so the app writes into one directory it picks (the bundle's `Documents`, which is `getExternalFilesDir(DIRECTORY_DOCUMENTS)` on Android) for each of its plaintext writers (recovery-phrase / account-number `.txt`, identity-phrase `.txt`, vault markdown tree, note PDF, exported attachment), and that write is what the user authorizes by pressing the button. What the user does NOT authorize is the directory. **Since myne-md/myne#505 the Android client then hands the file to the Storage Access Framework picker (`ACTION_CREATE_DOCUMENT`), where the user chooses the destination up front and the bytes are written there**, so on that platform the destination IS chosen by the user, in the platform's own dialog, for the flows that produce a file (a directory export arrives as one `.zip`, because a document picker saves one document; the note PDF has no Android renderer yet, so that flow reaches no picker). iOS keeps the shape it had: the `UIActivityViewController` share sheet, whose "Save to Files" destination is chosen by the user at the moment they tap it. The app's own copy stays on both platforms and is the fallback for a dismissed chooser, because deleting it would lose a recovery phrase that `recovery-flow.md` §2.4 never shows again, and sending the file to a cloud drive removes no exposure that deleting the local copy would (see `decisions/clients/0792`). (2) The **operating system's automatic cloud backup is a second egress channel across this boundary, and the user authorizes it separately or not at all**: iOS includes `Documents/` in iCloud Backup and Android includes `getExternalFilesDir(DIRECTORY_DOCUMENTS)` in Auto Backup, both on by default. A plaintext export carried there leaves the device entirely, which the phrasing above ("outside Myne's protection from the moment it is written") permits but does not describe. Myne therefore **excludes the mobile export directory from platform backup on both platforms**: `NSURLIsExcludedFromBackupKey` on the directory (iOS), `android:dataExtractionRules` plus `android:fullBackupContent` excluding it from cloud backup **and** device transfer (Android). What that closes is the platform's *automatic backup*, and only that. **Since myne-md/myne#517 the iOS half is a chokepoint rather than a startup hook alone**, which matters to this row in one specific way: the destination the app picks for the user is now resolved by a single host command (`plugin:export-dir|export_path`) that resolves the directory, creates it, applies the exclusion and sanitizes the filename in the same call, and returns a path only when all four succeeded. So the export flows cannot compose a destination whose exclusion did not take: the command returns an error rather than a path. **That is narrower than containment and the difference is worth stating, because the first draft of this sentence read as containment.** The three export COMMANDS still take a caller-supplied path string and a literal absolute path needs no `plugin:path` grant, so dropping the `core:path:` grants does not close that route; and the note PDF does not go through a command at all, reaching the host as a `core:event` payload that `pdf_export::present` writes with no containment check. The writers remain the enforcement point, which is where **this row's** plaintext-egress exception already puts them (the composing process is TB2; the write crosses TB3). This sentence cited **TB6** for two review rounds, which is the sync server's Postgres and blob store and says nothing about export writers, local paths or the WebView-to-host boundary; §3 defines no row for that boundary at all, so the citation named one that does not exist rather than merely the wrong number. Resolving each destination inside its own export command is recorded as separate, larger work rather than done here. The startup call stays, because it also repairs a container restored from an older backup. **This narrows the exception and does not widen it**: the mobile main window's whole `core:path:` namespace was dropped in the same change, so the WebView can no longer ASK TAURI to resolve a directory or join a path, and the two hand-rolled TypeScript path sanitizers it used to depend on are one Rust function with a post-condition. "No longer resolve a directory or join a path at all" is what this sentence said for two review rounds and it is the same over-claim the paragraph above retracts: a literal absolute path needs no `plugin:path` grant, and string concatenation in the WebView needs nothing at all. `decisions/security/0595`. **The mechanism is in place; its effect on a device has not been observed** (myne-md/myne#491, and myne-md/myne#517 changed the mechanism's shape without changing that fact). What *is* established: the shipped Foundation call sets the flag and the flag reads back, run automatically against macOS on every `cargo test` (`backup_exclude::tests::macos`); and the Android attributes survive the Gradle manifest merger into the app that ships, run once during review. What is **not**: that iOS's backup honours the flag, and that Android's backup service honours the rules. `mobile/docs/dev/device-matrix.md` §8 rows 8.9 and 8.10 own those and neither has been run.
