A green build and a green `scripts/check-mobile-export-backup.mjs` establish that the four files carrying this property agree with each other, which is a smaller claim again. Read the unobserved half the way TB7's absent updater and TB11's unimplemented service worker are read. Two further limits, stated rather than implied: iOS has **no** device-migration equivalent of the Android `<device-transfer>` exclusion, so a direct device-to-device migration on that platform is uncovered and untested; and on iOS the share sheet is the only route the user has to the file, whose default "Save to Files" destination is iCloud Drive, which is a copy the user authorizes rather than one Myne makes. **The encrypted vault is deliberately NOT excluded**: it remains in the platform backup on both mobile and desktop, which discloses nothing (it is AEAD ciphertext whose key is derived from a password the backup does not hold) and is a real recovery path for a user who does not sync. **Adversary mapping, because this channel has none of its own:** no §1 row covers the platform vendor holding a backup. The nearest are A2 (compulsion, but scoped to Myne's own sync server) and A10 (a state actor), and neither describes Apple or Google reading a file the OS copied for the user. That is an acknowledged gap in §1, not a mitigated row. **A third shape of the same exception (myne-md/myne#166).** `myne-decrypt` is an app-independent CLI that exports the vault to plain markdown without the app installed. The egress is user-initiated and one-shot like the app's export, but there is **no OS file dialog and no dialog authority**: the destination is a raw path argument, so what the user authorizes is the command line they typed. Three properties bound it. (1) The output directory is created owner-only (`0700` on Unix) and every run ends with an unconditional closing line naming the plaintext directory, so a silent export is not possible. (2) A named-provider cloud-sync warning plus a `--yes` confirmation guards known cloud roots (iCloud Drive including macOS `~/Documents` and `~/Desktop` by home-relative match, Dropbox, Google Drive, OneDrive); detection is best-effort and a miss reads as no clearance, which the closing line is the guarantee for. (3) A `.myne-backup` source is materialized into a randomized, create-new temp directory, owner-only on Unix, holding `vault.meta` (the complete offline password-cracking target, §7) plus member ciphertext; it is removed on drop, and a crash or SIGKILL between creation and removal leaves that copy behind, which is the residue window this tool adds. **A fourth shape: nothing above covers `<app_config_dir>`, and the person token lives there (myne-md/myne#911).** Every exclusion this row describes applies to the mobile export directory, not to the app's config directory. `<app_config_dir>/person-auth.json` is ordinary app-container storage, so on iOS it is inside iCloud Backup and on Android inside Auto Backup **by default**, and no exclusion is applied to it and none is checked for. That file holds the person token in plaintext, and the token authorizes every vault the person account is bound to, reads included (`server-protocol.md` §3.1a), so a platform backup carries a credential for the whole set off the device. What bounds it is revocation: the backed-up copy is the same token, so revoking that device at the server makes a restored copy useless, and revocation is the only thing that does. **The exclusion now ships (2026-10-23).** It was recorded as an open exposure for one round and is applied in this change, on both platforms and at DIRECTORY level rather than per file: iOS sets `NSURLIsExcludedFromBackupKey` on `<app_config_dir>` at the same startup point the export directory uses (Apple QA1719 makes a directory-level exclusion cover files created inside it later, which is what a forget-then-create cycle needs), and Android excludes `<exclude domain="file" path="person-auth.json"/>` under `<cloud-backup>`, `<device-transfer>` and in the API-30 file. `scripts/check-mobile-export-backup.mjs` holds the two halves together and both directions were proven by injection. **What the exclusion does NOT do**, stated so it is not read as more: the token is still plaintext on disk, so a jailbroken or rooted device, or a forensic image of the running app's sandbox, still yields it; and neither the iOS flag nor the Android rules have been observed on a real device (`mobile/docs/dev/device-matrix.md` §8 rows 8.9 and 8.10 remain unrun). A keychain or Keystore wrap is the at-rest half and is filed on its own, because the Android arm is a dependency decision rather than a code change. |
| TB4 | Network (transport) | Ciphertext bytes only | TLS with chain and server-name verification. Native sync clients use bundled Mozilla public CA roots; web uses the browser trust store (§1 A4 amendment). No certificate pin or accept-any-certificate first handshake. The transport carries ciphertext-in-ciphertext. | **Exception, the sync server's operator panel (`myne-md/myne#787`):** it is a plain-HTTP listener that crosses this boundary carrying per-account operator metadata in the clear (account hash, stored bytes, device count, freeze state) whenever it is bound to a non-loopback address. It terminates no TLS, so the row's transport guarantee does not hold for it, and the loopback bind is the documented default for that reason. A party that both reaches that listener and the public sync API holds the account-hash roster, which is the selector set for the pre-auth `GET /vault.meta` route and therefore a set of credentials for it (see the operator-console row). |
| TB5 | Sync server (hosted **and** self-hosted treated identically) | Opaque ciphertext blobs, hashed account-number identifiers (plus, for a person session, the 16-byte vault namespace selector it sends as `?account=`), version counters, current-connection IPs (transient, not logged), aggregate quota counters, and (when a server gates registration with an invite code) the presented code itself, which is compared and discarded with no code-to-account record | The server is **explicitly outside the trust boundary** regardless of operator. Self-hosting changes the operator, not the protocol's security model. The protocol assumes a curious or compelled server. |
> **A discovery answer may materialise local vault frames (myne-md/myne#911).** `GET /b2b/vaults` plus the pre-auth namespace fetch lets a listed binding become a local container: one `vault.meta` and one namespace record per vault, capped at 50 per call, with no key, corpus, password or cursor installed. That is a new capability for a party this section treats as untrusted (it can make the client WRITE), and it is bounded and non-destructive rather than accepted silently: the frame is validated for namespace and KDF policy before the write, and the first unlock of each materialised vault still asks for that vault's own password.

| TB6 | Server persistent storage (Postgres + blob store) | Same set as TB5 minus transient connection state | Storage is treated as if directly readable by an adversary; nothing of value to an attacker is stored unencrypted. |
| TB7 | Code distribution channel (`downloads.myne.md`, a first-party static origin the project operates; package managers fetching from it; private GitHub releases behind it) | Released artifacts authentic to the team's **release-signing** key — which signs the hash list, not the installer | **An in-app auto-update now exists, and this row said it did not until 2026-10-07** (`decisions/clients/0649`). `tauri-plugin-updater` is configured and registered on desktop; the client fetches a signed per-channel manifest from `downloads.myne.md`, verifies each artifact against the offline release key (its signature check cannot be disabled), refuses on signature or hash failure, and never installs a version that is not strictly newer (its default version comparator; `allowDowngrades` stays false and is unreachable from the WebView). The check is host-side: the WebView reaches only the `update` plugin's commands, never the updater's own API. This is the crossing condition this row named: refuse on signature or hash failure, never silently downgrade. **The two other things this row used to claim were also false, and are corrected here rather than left standing** (`decisions.md` 2026-08-15, entry 0496): releases were **not** code-signed until 2026-10-07; the macOS build is now signed with an Apple Developer ID and notarized, so Gatekeeper no longer warns there, while no Windows Authenticode certificate exists (so SmartScreen still warns on a Windows install), and desktop builds are **not** reproducible, nor could a third party check if they were, because every `myne-md` repository is private and there is no public source to rebuild from. What actually holds this boundary today is narrower and worth stating exactly: each release's `SHA256SUMS` is **signed with an offline minisign key** and the user verifies it **manually**, the same hashes are **published on a second origin** (`myne.md`, a different box, repository and deploy chain, so a compromise of one cannot make its lie agree with the other), and, since 2026-10-07, **a signed update channel exists**: an auto-update is offered only when its artifact's signature verifies against the offline key and its version is strictly newer, so a substitution that cannot sign is refused. Note what the signature does *not* cover: it authenticates the hash list, not the installer, and it is not something the operating system checks. The Android `.apk` is the one artifact carrying an OS-checked signature, because Android refuses to install an unsigned package; that key is unknown to Google Play, so sideloading still warns about provenance. **The project now operates the distribution origin itself**, which is the A15/TB11 shape applied to a native artifact — materially weaker as an attack, because the artifact is installed and *persists* so it can be hashed afterwards, which a re-delivered web bundle never permits. That posture changes as of 2026-10-07: the signature-gated updater described in the opening is now the mechanism, and the difference matters to anyone reasoning about A9. It meets the crossing condition this row named: refuse on signature or hash failure, never silently downgrade. Marked the way TB11 marks its own unshipped service worker, because the document already knew how to say this. `myne-decrypt` ships through this same channel (myne-md/myne#166): four native binaries (macOS universal, Linux x64, Linux arm64, Windows x64) named in the signed `SHA256SUMS` and verified manually like every other artifact here. The source is not yet public, so no independent rebuild verifies them; the help text says so rather than claiming otherwise. |
| TB8 | Build pipeline & signing keys (CI runners, the offline minisign release-signing key, the Android release keystore, release-tag credentials) | Authority to produce official releases | Internal team controls. Compromise here is the realistic upper-bound attack against A9. **This row claimed "hardware-backed signing keys" until 2026-08-25 and there are none** (entry 0534, completing the sweep 0496 began): on macOS a code-signing key now exists (the Developer ID, used since 2026-10-07) while on Windows there is none, and the Android release keystore is an ordinary file. What holds: the minisign key that signs each release's `SHA256SUMS` is held offline and outside CI, the same hashes are cross-published on a second origin, and the one thing a compromised pipeline COULD push into, since 2026-10-07, is the auto-update channel (TB7), so its blast radius is now the installed base rather than only whoever downloads in the window. Verification of any of it is manual and opt-in. |
| TB9 | Third-party dependencies (Rust crates, Go modules, npm packages) | Source code that runs inside TB2 and TB5 | Hash-pinned lockfiles checked into the repository, minimal dependency tree, periodic dependency review. A compromised transitive dependency can defeat client-side encryption (A8). |
| TB10 | Browser ↔ desktop clip channel (the keyless browser extension + the native-messaging bridge into the Tauri core) | Nothing secret. The extension is a keyless producer that hands captured plaintext markdown to the core; the bridge carries a scoped, revocable pairing token (create-clip + is-unlocked-status only — never a key, never read/modify/delete). | A user-initiated pairing handshake (in-app Accept/Reject + a short numeric challenge) binding the exact extension ID; the core treats every handed-off byte as hostile input (size caps — 16 MiB note body / 64 MiB per image, UTF-8 validation, no path traversal, re-sniffed MIME, rate limits). The extension runs OUTSIDE TB2 in the hostile browser runtime; root_key/item_key never cross this boundary. Clips save to the vault root with no folder/tag selection, so no vault structure (AS3/AS5) transits the browser; the extension holds no plaintext at rest (capture requires the vault unlocked — no pending-queue). |
| TB11 | Web bundle delivery channel (the origin serving the web client's HTML, JavaScript and wasm to the browser, on every page load) | Nothing. The bundle is *not* trusted the way a TB7 artifact is — but it is nonetheless the code that handles the master password and the decrypted vault, so a breach here is total for that session. | Nothing the browser checks. **This is why TB11 exists rather than being folded into TB7.** TB7's crossing condition is "a cross-published hash the user can compare against a file that persists" — a served bundle satisfies *none* of the three: browsers verify no signature over page resources, the user retains no artifact to hash, and the copy is replaced on every reload so any comparison is of something already gone. The compensating controls are after-the-fact, and one of them is not yet reachable by the person it is for: reproducible builds, per-release hashes written to an append-only record that is **not public today** (entry 0534), a strict content-security policy with zero third-party origins, and — once it is implemented — a service worker pinning the code after first load (which will bound *later* substitution, not first delivery; policy fixed in `decisions.md` 2026-07-26, **not yet shipped**). The adversary is A15. Self-hosting collapses this boundary for the self-hoster's own use — they operate it — and leaves it in place for anyone else using their instance. |
| TB12 | Local model runtime process (a user-installed daemon on the same machine — Ollama today — reached over loopback) | Decrypted note plaintext (AS1) for the duration of one request: whatever the invoked feature sends, which is a note body, a selection, or retrieved chunks. **Amended 2026-08-16 (myne-md/myne#529):** an agent run additionally sends the note-id, folder-path and title ROSTER of its approved scope, in the run's OPENING message and before it has acted at all (the `list_scope` tool exists, but the untouched-tool-call form is the wait the model never has to make). That is AS1 plus AS2-class titles plus the ids, and a `note_id` is a UUIDv7, so it encodes its note's creation time at millisecond resolution (AS6). Naming it here because the sentence above says "nothing else" and a bulk enumeration of notes the model has not read is not covered by it. **Amended 2026-08-30 (myne-md/myne#617):** an agent run additionally sends the visible text of recent chat turns, so that a follow-up message can refer to the answer before it. Named here because the sentence below says "nothing else", and because the seed is arm-independent: `build_request` prepends it before the engine is chosen, so a loopback runtime receives exactly what a remote provider does. It is bounded by a host-side character budget (3000 by default, clamped at 12000) and carries no tool results, note bodies, anchors or replacements. The two bounds it does NOT have are stated in the TB13 row and hold identically here: it is not narrowed by the scope of the run it is seeded into, and ~~it is cut rather than filtered when `ai_excluded_folders` changes, because a turn carries text and no note provenance~~ **(superseded 2026-09-04, myne-md/myne#675, `decisions/ai/0582`: the cut-not-filtered seed-block is gone. Exclusion is now enforced at every point where note content ENTERS the pipeline (scope resolution, reads, search, grounding), and historical transcript text is never re-policed; the seed is bounded by the character budget alone. The rule's premise died with the ephemeral transcript: with conversations persisted and synced, "the turns on screen" is no longer a session-scoped population.) **Code-state note (myne-md/myne#675 review): no conversation text seeds any run in this tree today (defect myne-md/myne#648, documented at `agent_start` in `src-tauri/src/plugins/agent/commands.rs`); the bounds above describe the seed #648 restores.** **Amended 2026-09-10 (myne-md/myne#697, agent memory):** the agent system turn now ALSO carries the agent memory (§9.25/§9.26) and the personality directives (§9.27) when those families exist, as their own `Content::UntrustedNote` span inside the system message, never concatenated into the trusted instructions (and, since 2026-09-17, outside any prompt-cache marker: the agent arm no longer asks for one), budget-bounded at injection (truncated, §9.25.3), with quarantined rows never injected. The memory is model-distilled prose the agent wrote in earlier runs, bounded per entry at 2000 characters and in count at 100 live entries (`protocol.md` \u00a79.25.3), schema-enforced and injection-scanned at both write time and injection time (`decisions/ai/0590`); the personality is user-authored text (§9.27). Neither carries note bodies verbatim (the `remember` schema carries distilled text, not provenance), but both are derived from vault content a run read, so they are the same payload class this row already carries. Both cross every arm: a loopback runtime and a remote provider receive the same system turn; a device with `ai_enabled` off seeds neither. Two writes are new at this boundary's client side and bounded rather than barred: the `remember` verb lets the model write one memory entry per run into vault content (§9.26, the 0515 approved-diff discipline deliberately not extended to memory, `decisions/ai/0591`), and every entry the model writes is scan-gated, which is the transparency control rather than an approval. A scan-tripping user write or update is dropped there, never persisted, never injected; a scan-tripping MODEL create persists as `quarantined` (listed in the Memory panel, never injected into any run) until a user edit clears it. Each memory line rides with its host-minted entry id (a UUIDv7, so creation time at millisecond resolution, AS6): the id is what lets a follow-up `remember` name the entry to overwrite, and a provider that retains requests can link the same id across runs. Nothing else — `root_key` and `item_key` never cross, and Myne sends no vault structure it was not asked to act on. | The user naming a provider. Default is unset, and unset means the path is unreachable rather than disabled (`decisions.md` 2026-08-09). The endpoint is validated loopback-only as a **literal** address, never a resolved one — a name that resolves to `127.0.0.1` today is a name someone else's DNS answers tomorrow. **Why this is not TB2.** TB2's CLI-sidecar bullet earns "everything stays inside TB2" because that sidecar ships inside the desktop bundle itself: it is the same artifact the user already chose to install, built from the same workspace, present in the same `Cargo.lock`, and covered by whatever check they made of that download. (Not by a signature. The bundle carries none that any OS checks, and this sentence said it did until 2026-08-25, entry 0534.) This daemon is separately distributed, absent from `Cargo.lock`, and unaudited by anyone here, so none of that rationale transfers. **Why this is not TB10.** TB10's trust cell reads "Nothing secret… `root_key`/`item_key` never cross this boundary" — true there because the clipper is a keyless *producer*; here AS1 plaintext is exactly what crosses. Residual worth naming: the runtime's listener is unauthenticated loopback, so any local process can reach it (§1 A16). **Amended 2026-10-07 (meeting-ai):** the opt-in meeting AI pass sends the meeting note's transcript, which is other people's speech, here on the on-device path, and to `TB13` when a remote provider is configured (`decisions/ai/0648`). **Amended 2026-10-12 (myne-md/myne#848):** two more facts about this payload, both from the same change, and both named here because the sentences above describe the older shape. First, the DEFAULT scope is the whole working set rather than the open note or nothing at all: a conversation started with no note attached enumerates every live note (the exclusion clamp subtracted, nothing else) before it has read any of them, so the roster a run may send is the vault's rather than one note's until the user narrows it. Second, a roster entry now carries the note's FOLDER PATH as well as its id and title (`<id> | <folder> | <title>`), which is AS3-class vault structure crossing a boundary the sentences above say no structure crosses. Both are surface the controls this row already names: the scope is what the user approved, a widening is its own approval, and `ai_excluded_folders` is subtracted host-side. **Amended 2026-10-22 (myne-md/myne#913, attached documents):** a run's payload now also carries the extracted text of up to four files the user attached to THAT message. The COMPOSER reads it from the device-local `MYE1` extracted-text cache (§9.15) when the message is sent and hands the host the file's name and that text; the host caps each document at 20,000 unicode scalar values and the run at four (the surplus is DROPPED, never silently truncated into a different file), and carries them as `Content::UntrustedNote` spans behind one trusted header exactly like note text. **The read itself is not host-enforced, and saying so is part of the claim:** the host receives text, so a page that can call the agent command can also invent a document, which is the same reach it already has over the task string. What bounds this payload is therefore the A8/A15 page context, not a check on the host side. The refusal of a chosen document with no cache entry is the composer's act, and it names the file rather than sending an empty one. This is a payload class the sentences above did not name: the bytes are a FILE's content rather than the vault's note plaintext, and the user may not have authored them. What bounds it: the attachment is chosen for one message by an explicit act (a pasted file or a pick from the library), the choice belongs to that draft and is cleared with it, nothing is read until the message is sent, and the file is already in the vault's attachment store, encrypted and synced like any other attachment, so what crosses is text the user already holds. `note_chars` counts the document spans in the run's off-device figure because they ride as content spans, and a chosen document whose cache entry is missing refuses the send by name rather than entering the run as an empty document. The attachment's bytes are never sent: only the extracted text, truncated at the cap. **Amended 2026-10-22 (myne-md/myne#915, reviewed vault seed):** the same boundary now carries a one-shot structural projection of the vault (note titles, folder paths, tag names and counts) and, only when the model asks for up to ten folders, those folders' note bodies under a 60,000-character budget. The tree is `AS3`-class vault structure crossing a boundary the paragraphs above say no structure crosses; `ai_excluded_folders` removes an excluded folder's NAME as well as its titles, because the name can be the sensitive part, and the scope screen shows the real payload rather than a summary. A remote provider needs its own per-run confirmation naming the provider and the size, checked at the command boundary rather than trusted from the wizard. No note id, attachment, property, task or heading is in the projection. The seed is also a prompt-injection amplifier: one hostile note can become standing context for every future run once it is distilled into memory, which is why the per-entry review screen, the provenance line in the injected block and the rule that a seed row is user-authored (so the model cannot rewrite it) are the controls rather than the scan alone. The write side is a second bulk path outside the run loop; its bounds are recorded in `decisions/ai/0742` and `protocol.md` §9.25. |
| TB13 | Remote AI provider (Ollama Cloud, OpenRouter, OpenCode Zen and OpenCode Go, all services Myne does not operate) | Decrypted note plaintext (AS1), which **leaves the device**. No key material and no account number. **Amended 2026-08-16 (myne-md/myne#529):** "no vault structure beyond the text of the thing the user asked about" is no longer true for an agent run, and is corrected rather than left standing. A run sends the note-id, folder-path and title roster of its approved scope, so a folder-scoped run ships that subtree's titles, identifiers and structure off-device in the opening message, before any tool call, and a `note_id` is a UUIDv7 carrying its note's creation time at millisecond resolution. Those ids are stable across runs and sessions, so a provider that retains requests can link them. The payload class is one this model already carries (AS1, AS2, AS6); what changed is which boundary it reaches, and the honest statement is that the roster crosses TB13, not that nothing structural does. **Amended 2026-08-30 (myne-md/myne#617):** what crosses grows by one further item, the visible text of recent chat turns. A run is now seeded with the conversation the user can see (their own messages and the model's own answers), so a follow-up can refer to the previous one. Three things bound it and one does not. It is bounded by a character budget applied host-side, 3000 by default and clamped at 12000, which is the only cap on THIS component and exists because there was none; the run's total volume stays unbounded, as §7 says, since note bodies read across up to 24 iterations are not covered by it. It carries no tool results, note bodies, anchors or replacements. And it is text the user has already seen and that already crossed this boundary once. What it is NOT bounded by is the scope of the run it is seeded into: a turn taken under a folder-wide scope keeps its summary when the next run is scoped to a single note, so narrowing the scope does not narrow the history, and `decisions/ai/0553` records that as a deliberate loosening rather than an oversight. `charsOffDevice` counts it, because that figure is derived from what the provider was actually sent. ~~**What the seed IS bounded by, beyond the budget:** a change to `ai_excluded_folders` stops every turn then on screen from being seeded into any later run. SYNC-14 carries no in-flight carve-out and this is the same rule one door over, since an agent summary is model prose derived from the notes it read. The cut is by time rather than per note because a turn carries text and no provenance, which is the conservative reading and is stated here rather than left to be inferred from the code.~~ **(That clause is superseded 2026-09-04, myne-md/myne#675, `decisions/ai/0582`: the seed-block machinery is deleted. `ai_excluded_folders` remains the content-scoped control and is now enforced at one more site: scope resolution refuses a scope whose resolved set is empty after the exclusion clamp (the CLI's refused-before-written shape), but historical transcript text is never re-policed, and the seed is bounded by the character budget alone. The rule above existed for an ephemeral transcript whose "turns on screen" was a session-scoped population; with conversations persisted and synced it no longer is, and a per-exclusion-change cut would either vandalize the visible history or quietly under-deliver the seed.) **Code-state note (myne-md/myne#675 review): no conversation text seeds any run in this tree today (defect myne-md/myne#648, documented at `agent_start` in `src-tauri/src/plugins/agent/commands.rs`); the bounds above describe the seed #648 restores.** **Amended the same day (myne-md/myne#620):** an AGENT run's request carried a prompt-cache marker on its system turn, which asked the provider to retain that prefix rather than merely allowing it to. The bound was content class rather than turn role: the marked span was `prompt::system_instructions`, host-written and carrying no note content, and ordinary chat carried no marker at all because its system message is built from retrieved note excerpts. **Superseded 2026-09-17 (myne-md/myne#697): the agent marker is gone.** The system turn now carries note-derived memory, so the marked span stopped being note-free and the marker with it; the agent keeps its output ceiling, which never marked anything. The cost is a full-price system turn on every iteration. That distinction is the correction `decisions/ai/0555` records, since the marker first shipped unconditional and therefore asked the provider to retain note plaintext. Same bytes, same recipient, and A17 already places the provider's retention outside anything this model constrains; `decisions/ai/0555` records the posture change because it is the first place Myne makes a request about retention at all. **The same request also began carrying a routing preference** (`provider.sort = throughput`), on ordinary chat as well as on agent runs. That is Myne influencing which upstream vendor the broker forwards to, which this row already describes as "a set Myne can bound only at its first hop"; the criterion expressed is speed, and no data-handling term is expressed. The broker's object does carry one (`data_collection`), and declining it is an open product decision recorded in `decisions/ai/0555` rather than an absence. Named here because an account of what crosses this boundary that omits a field present on every request is incomplete. **Amended 2026-09-10 (myne-md/myne#697, agent memory):** the agent system turn now ALSO carries the agent memory (§9.25/§9.26) and the personality directives (§9.27) when those families exist, as their own `Content::UntrustedNote` span inside the system message, never concatenated into the trusted instructions (the 2026-08-30 seed amendment's trust class, applied to model-distilled prose), budget-bounded at injection (truncated, §9.25.3), with quarantined rows never injected. The agent arm carries NO prompt-cache marker since this change: the system turn is no longer note-free, so asking the provider to retain it is the request `decisions/ai/0555` forbids, and ordinary chat never carried one either. The memory is model-distilled prose the agent wrote in earlier runs (bounded per entry at 2000 characters, bounded in count at 100 live entries (`protocol.md` \u00a79.25.3), schema-enforced and injection-scanned at both write time and injection time (see `decisions/ai/0590`)); the personality is user-authored text (§9.27). Neither carries note bodies verbatim (the `remember` schema carries distilled text, not provenance), but both are derived from vault content the run read, so they are the same payload class this row already carries. Each memory line rides with its host-minted entry id (a UUIDv7, creation time at millisecond resolution, AS6, linkable across runs by a retaining provider). Both cross every arm: a loopback runtime and a remote provider receive the same system turn. A device with `ai_enabled` off seeds neither, and the memory the agent CAN write (`remember` verb) is bounded to one write per run. The controls are three: the write-time gate (schema, cap, scan) refuses hostile updates and user writes before they are ever stored, and persists a hostile MODEL create as `quarantined` (listed, never injected) instead of dropping it; the injection-time re-scan drops anything that slipped through, never shipped into the system turn; and the `UntrustedNote` span keeps the block out of the trusted instructions, sanitized host-side on every request. Unlike the scope roster, the memory seed is whole-roster: it is never narrowed by the run's approved scope, never re-policed against later exclusion changes, and reaches every synced device by Class-1 sync. The scope clamp bounds what a steered run can DO, not what it is seeded WITH. **Amended 2026-10-07 (meeting-ai):** a meeting transcript is another `AS1` payload that crosses this boundary. The opt-in meeting AI pass (`decisions/ai/0648`) sends the meeting note's transcript, which is other people's speech, to the named remote provider under the same per-provider consent, and sends nothing anywhere when no provider is configured (the on-device path crosses only `TB12`). No rating change: the payload class is `AS1` and `A17` already covers the provider's retention. **Amended 2026-11-01 (myne-md/myne#970, two OpenCode providers):** the recipient set grows by two products of one operator, and every request to the second of them carries **one datum this boundary did not carry before**. OpenCode Zen is the pay-as-you-go gateway and OpenCode Go the subscription plan; both live on `opencode.ai` and are separated by path, not by host (`decisions/ai/0773`). Go refuses a request that carries no `x-opencode-session` header, so Myne sends one: a random 128-bit hex string minted once per process and prefixed `myne` (an entropy failure falls back to the pid, so the value stays unique per process rather than shared by every install). It carries nothing about the device, the vault, the account or the user, and it is never persisted. Its lifetime is one process: two runs in that process carry the same id, which is the stability the field exists for, and two processes carry unrelated ones. That stability is why it is named here instead of being left to the decision entry, since a provider that retains requests can group one process's requests by it, and this row's controls bound what is sent rather than what a retaining provider can correlate (A17). The same request also carries a `user-agent`, and what it says is the product name and nothing further: the literal `myne` on every client, desktop and mobile alike, with no platform, no version and no per-install value, so it distinguishes Myne from something that is not Myne and does not distinguish one Myne install from another. Neither header reaches a provider that does not need it: the other three arms send only `authorization` and `content-type`. Two further facts about the far side are recorded rather than constrained: Zen's free tier answers `403` to any client that is not OpenCode itself, so only its paid models are reachable from Myne, and Go's monthly allowance refuses non-retryably when spent. | Explicit, one-time consent per provider on a screen that states what leaves the device — recorded **per device since myne-md/myne#456** and per account before it. **The device scope holds by the VALUE rather than by the merge class, and that distinction is what makes the sentence true of existing installs too.** A consent stores `<provider>@device`; a bare provider name is a legacy value the gate refuses, so the two populations that hold one, a desktop install that turned Developer mode off and a device enrolled before the reclassification, are re-asked rather than silently honoured. Without that the class alone would have been a claim about newly enrolled devices only: the field-level merge keeps the local value always, so nothing would ever revise an inherited approval, and dropping `developer_mode` from the request-time gate would have moved an already-configured desktop user from sending nothing to sending note plaintext with no act and no notice (`protocol.md` §9.5.2 carries the value grammar). Plus a named provider, default unset and unreachable while unset, and TLS anchored on an embedded root bundle. **Amended 2026-10-07 (myne-md/myne#607):** which network path the request takes is now stated rather than left unstated. By default the device-local Tor routing option is off, and the provider transport dials the provider directly, as every other request does. With Tor routing on, the request goes through the same loopback SOCKS5 proxy the sync transport uses, and the provider hostname is resolved inside the Tor circuit rather than by this device's resolver; with Tor on and the proxy not yet `Ready` (still bootstrapping, failed, or stopped) the request is **refused without opening a socket** rather than sent directly; there is no fallback, because a fallback would leak the device's real source IP at exactly the moment the user believes they are protected. The mobile client stays on the direct path until myne-md/myne#450 ships a mobile sidecar, and its consent copy says so. **What Myne cannot promise here, and does not:** the far side's retention, logging and onward routing are unverifiable — OpenRouter is a broker that forwards to model vendors, so "who receives this" is a set Myne can bound only at its first hop. Two structural properties bound the blast radius instead: surfaces that fire without the user asking (ghost-text, auto-tag) are barred from this boundary at the seam rather than by a setting, and only user-initiated work crosses it. The ChatML special-token defense degrades from structural to behavioural across this boundary — see §7. **Amended 2026-10-12 (myne-md/myne#848):** two more facts about this payload, both from the same change, and both named here because the sentences above describe the older shape. First, the DEFAULT scope is the whole working set rather than the open note or nothing at all: a conversation started with no note attached enumerates every live note (the exclusion clamp subtracted, nothing else) before it has read any of them, so the roster a run may send is the vault's rather than one note's until the user narrows it. Second, a roster entry now carries the note's FOLDER PATH as well as its id and title (`<id> | <folder> | <title>`), which is AS3-class vault structure crossing a boundary the sentences above say no structure crosses. Both are surface the controls this row already names: the scope is what the user approved, a widening is its own approval, and `ai_excluded_folders` is subtracted host-side. **Amended 2026-10-22 (myne-md/myne#913, attached documents):** the same four-document, 20,000-scalar-per-document attached-document payload named in the TB12 amendment crosses THIS boundary too, to the named remote provider under the same per-provider consent: a run sends the extracted text (not the bytes) of the files the user attached to that message, which the composer read from the device-local `MYE1` cache, as `Content::UntrustedNote` spans, and `note_chars` counts it. The consent is per provider and per device as above, and the loopback arm and this one receive exactly the same spans, so a user who has named a remote provider sends what a local runtime would have been given. No key material, no account number and no attachment blob crosses; the catalog row carries a `scope` marker (`chat` vs the absent library value, §9.10.2) that stays on the device and in the synced catalog rather than in the request. **Amended 2026-10-22 (myne-md/myne#915, reviewed vault seed):** on the remote arm the seed's phase one sends the vault's structural projection (titles, folder paths, tags, counts) and phase two sends only the bodies of the folders the model asked for, under a character budget. That is `AS3`-class structure and `AS1` text crossing this boundary in one reviewed act rather than one run at a time. The controls are the per-run confirmation that names the provider and the size (host-checked), `ai_excluded_folders` applied to the folder NAME as well as its titles, and the review screen, which is the human gate before any entry is written. The projection carries no note id, so it does not add a stable correlation handle to this boundary; the bodies carry no id either. The write that follows is local vault content (`protocol.md` §9.25), and a retaining provider can link the resulting memory lines across runs only through their host-minted entry ids, exactly as it already can for a `remember` write. |
| TB14 | Host ↔ Myne's own artefact origin (`downloads.myne.md`, a static-origin service Myne operates) | No vault data. The request carries no account number, no key and no note content; the only thing it reveals is a client IP and which model was chosen, that is, that this device turned AI on. | An explicit user action per download, and the fetched bytes are verified against a SHA-256 compiled into the client binary before the model is registered, so the origin's own `index.json` and `SHA256SUMS` are never the trust anchor and a tampered origin cannot serve matching bytes. The origin keeps no IP logs and no analytics (`decisions/ops/0494`), and that promise is now user-facing copy (`decisions/ai/0628`). Distinct from TB13: this is an inbound fetch with no provider named, not outbound note text. Adversary: A9 (compromised distribution), the same class as the release-binary origin of `decisions/ops/0496`. |

## 4. Protected Against — Specific Mechanisms

For each adversary classified as Strong or Mitigated in Section 1, the specific defense mechanism. This is the cross-product of Sections 1, 2, and 3.

| Adversary | Mechanism |
|---|---|
| **A1. Passive cloud provider** | Client-side encryption before any byte leaves the device (TB2 → TB4). Server (TB5/TB6) stores only ciphertext blobs (AS1, AS2, AS7), opaque version counters (AS6), **per-note, per-conversation and per-memory-entry blob ids** (UUIDv7s, each carrying its creation time; AS6), hashed account identifiers (AS9), and aggregate quota counters (AS17). Root key (AS12), master password (AS10), and recovery phrase (AS11) never leave the client. |
| **A2. Compelled lawful access** | Same architecture as A1. The server cannot disclose what it does not possess. A subpoena forces disclosure of: ciphertext blobs (opaque), hashed account number, current connection IP (transient, no logs), version counter values, **per-note, per-conversation and per-memory-entry blob ids and therefore each item's creation time** (AS6), aggregate storage size. It cannot force disclosure of: note plaintext, titles, structure, links, tags, `updated_at`, master password, recovery phrase. |
| **A3. Passive network attacker** | TLS in transit, **1.3 or 1.2** (TB4: rustls ships with the `tls12` feature enabled and builds with `with_safe_default_protocol_versions()`, so this is a range and not a floor) plus client-side E2EE before transit. Two layers of confidentiality; defeating one still leaves the other. The base text of this row said "TLS 1.3" and cited TB4 for it, and TB4's own rewrite dropped the version while leaving this half standing. |
| **A4. Active network attacker (MitM)** | Public-CA and server-name verification on native clients (including first connection and Tor), browser trust-store verification on web. A compromised public CA can defeat native server authentication; the former TOFU pin would have detected a changed certificate only after a clean first connect (§1 A4 amendment). E2EE still prevents a transport MitM from reading notes. A client now fetches an automatic update (TB7, since 2026-10-07); it is refused unless the artifact's signature verifies against the offline release key. The web code-delivery attack is A15/TB11, a separate boundary. |
| **A5. Lost / stolen device (locked)** | At-rest encryption (AS1, AS3–AS7 inside the encrypted blobs in TB3). The root key is derived via Argon2id from the master password, which is not stored. Device disk in isolation is opaque ciphertext, with the one exception the user creates: a recovery-phrase or account-number file saved from the creation flow (TB3) onto that same disk is plaintext, and this row's guarantee does not extend to it. Owner-only permissions bound which local account can read such a file; they are no defence against whoever holds the disk. On mobile the export directory is now declared excluded from iCloud Backup and Android Auto Backup (TB3, myne-md/myne#491), so that file is not *meant* to leave the device with the platform's automatic backup; TB3 states what has and has not been observed about that exclusion, and this row is about the disk regardless, where the file is plaintext exactly as stated. **Exception, PIN quick-unlock:** the PIN provider places a third wrapped copy of `root_key` (`vault.quickunlock`) on that same disk, so the disk is not opaque in isolation: it is guarded by PIN entropy × the vault's Argon2id cost and is offline-brute-forceable; see §1 A5 and §7. The biometric provider does not have this property: its wrap key is held non-exportably by the OS. **Exception, member vault password:** a B2B member's device password (`vault.member-unlock`, `decisions/protocol/0653`), which `decisions/ui/0668` requires at accept, places that vault's group and items keys, and their person keypair, on that same disk under a key derived from the password, so the vault's disk is offline-brute-forceable at password entropy; see §1 A5 and §7. **It is the only member store since 2026-10-30** (`decisions/protocol/0763`): the phrase-sealed `vault.member-key` beside it is deleted, which removed one wrapped copy of `group_key` and `itemsKey` from every member device. |
| **A9. Compromised build / distribution** | **This row claimed reproducible builds and code-signed releases; it has neither on desktop** (corrected 2026-08-15, entry 0496). Desktop artifacts are not reproducible and the source they would be reproduced from is private. The macOS `.dmg` is now signed with an Apple Developer ID and notarized (since 2026-10-07), so macOS checks it; Windows has no Authenticode certificate and the Linux packages carry no signature any OS checks. **The Android `.apk` is the one exception and it is worth naming rather than rounding away:** it is signed with Myne's own release key because Android refuses to install an unsigned package, so on that platform the OS *does* verify integrity at install and enforces the same key on every later update. That key is unknown to Google Play, which is why sideloading still warns — the warning is about provenance, not about an absent signature. What is actually in place: a **minisign-signed `SHA256SUMS`** per release, whose key is held offline and on neither server, so whoever holds the distribution origin cannot forge a hash list to match a substituted binary; the **same hashes published on a second origin** with its own repository and deploy chain, so a single-host compromise is visible as a disagreement rather than invisible; and, since 2026-10-07, a **signature-gated auto-update** channel: an update installs only when its artifact's signature verifies against the offline key and its version is strictly newer, so there is an update path to attack but a substitution that cannot sign is refused (TB7). The remaining gap is that verification is **manual and opt-in**: a user who downloads and runs without checking is protected by none of it. |
| **A11. Insider threat (Myne team)** | The architecture grants the team no privileged access. Insiders see what an external attacker on the server (A1) sees — opaque ciphertext. The single privileged asset is the release-signing key (TB8), meaning the offline minisign key that signs each release's hash list, together with the Android release keystore; compromise reduces to A9. There is no desktop code-signing key to hold, which is itself a gap rather than a reassurance (§1 A9). **Native clients only.** On the web client the team additionally operates the code-delivery channel (TB11), which is privileged access by construction — see A15 for what is and is not done about it. |
| **A15. Hostile delivery of the client code (web)** | Nothing prevents targeted first delivery; the mechanisms are detection and containment. Reproducible bundle builds so a third party can rebuild and compare, **scoped to a reference build environment** (`linux/amd64`, `web/container/Dockerfile`) rather than to every host, because Cargo hashes the build host's target triple into every crate's `-C metadata` and no stable-toolchain flag removes it: any host can enter that environment through emulation, and a rebuild from outside it differs for reasons that are not evidence of anything (`web-bundle-hashes.md`, entry 0498); per-release bundle hashes written to an append-only record so a substitution is detectable after the fact, **except that the record is not public today** (it lives in a private repository, so the reader this control addresses cannot open it; §5 says the same and entry 0534 records it as a gap rather than a control); a service worker that **will** pin the loaded code so a substitution shipped later cannot reach a running session (policy fixed in `decisions.md` 2026-07-26; **not yet implemented**, so today nothing bounds a later substitution either); a strict content-security policy with zero third-party origins so the bundle is the entire attack surface; and cross-origin isolation, which the client needs anyway. Self-hosting removes the third party entirely for the self-hoster's own use. |
| **A18. Recovery-phrase phishing (web)** | Disclosure only, and weaker than everything it replaced. **The identity phrase joined the surface on 2026-10-22 (myne-md/myne#911 task 13):** a browser now creates and unlocks a person identity, so the words a lookalike asks for are the account's rather than one vault's. Nothing about containment changed, and one cost is recorded at the row: the browser keeps no person credential at rest (session-only, re-entered after every reload), which removes an offline cracking target from origin storage and adds a phrase prompt per load. The phrase is asked for on the web client at creation, in a recovery the user started themselves, and, since `decisions.md` 2026-10-18 entry 0704, by phrase-credential device enrolment, whose grid ships rather than being substituted out; `scripts/check-web-no-phrase.mjs` and the build-time substitutions it enforced are deleted with that entry. **What a phished phrase opens narrowed on 2026-10-30 (`decisions/protocol/0758`, myne-md/myne#938):** the recovery the user can start on the web is now **identity** recovery, and an `NTV8` vault has no phrase path to recover into, so the words buy the person rather than the notes. The surfaces that ask are unchanged, the containment is unchanged (there is none), and the row's rating is unchanged; the §1 row carries the reasoning. The Settings recovery drill that used to be a third place was withdrawn on 2026-10-28 (`decisions/security/0747`, myne-md/myne#935), and the only sentence the product keeps about the phrase now sits on the backup-success screen, with no control beside it. There is therefore no situation a user can name as the one Myne asks in, and no routine flow is kept from training a user that typing 24 words into a page is normal. What is left is the disclosure each surface carries plus the origin's HSTS preload, which binds the HONEST origin to TLS and says nothing about a lookalike, so it belongs with the controls listed above as irrelevant to this adversary rather than with anything that bears on it. **Nothing here prevents** a user from typing the phrase into a lookalike origin, and no delivery-channel control touches this adversary. Native clients are structurally unaffected. **Amended 2026-11-05 (myne-md/myne#1029): a phone can ask from a Settings row.** A mobile client whose sync cycle cannot sign the vault head (the vault is open, the person identity is closed) shows a Settings → Sync row that names opening the identity, and pressing it opens the phrase grid. The ask is app-initiated from a state the client measured itself, it is the same phrase entry the first-run and scanned-code doors already reach, and no rating moves here: this row's subject is a page fetched from an origin, and a native client has no address bar to read and no origin to impersonate, which is the sentence above. |
| **A14. Compromised extension build** | Keyless capability-handoff (the extension never receives root_key/item_key — TB10), an append-only scoped pairing token (create-clip only — never read/modify/delete), render-safe-by-construction ingest (DOMPurify allowlist, no script execution, serve-time re-sniff), and no core-side network fetch (all bytes originate from in-page capture). Save-to-root means no vault structure transits the browser. Damage is bounded to writing visible, deletable clip notes. |

## 5. NOT Protected Against

Written in plain language for users. If any of these scenarios apply to your threat model, Myne alone is not sufficient.

- **You forget your master password and lose your recovery phrase.** Your data is unrecoverable. We cannot help. There is no "forgot password" email, no admin reset, no backdoor. This is the deliberate cost of zero-knowledge.
- **Someone is physically watching your screen.** Open notes are visible to anyone looking at your unlocked device. Myne does not auto-hide content.
- **Someone is watching your screen while the device is *locked*, and you keep more than one vault.** The picker you choose a vault from appears before you unlock anything, and it shows the name you gave each vault this device has opened at least once, grouped by the account that owns it. Anyone who can see that screen reads those names without knowing your password, and without touching the device. Settings → Privacy → **hide vault names** takes them off that screen (vault names and the grouping alike, with grouping suppressed entirely) and destroys the keys the cached names were sealed under, so they cannot come back; what it cannot do is unsee a screen somebody already read, and it can only keep a name from ever being cached if you turn it on before that vault's first unlock here. With names hidden, the picker still shows one row per vault, still numbers them, and still tells that observer how many vaults you keep, when each was last opened, and the order in which you created them. (A5)
- **Someone takes your laptop or phone while it is unlocked and signed in, *during* the active window.** Auto-lock on idle zeroizes your keys and clears the open note after a period of inactivity (A6 is now 🔒 Mitigated), but if an attacker grabs the device while you are actively using it — before the idle timer fires — your notes are decrypted in memory and exposed. Auto-lock narrows this window; it does not eliminate it. Forensic RAM capture, swap, and hibernation are not scrubbed and stay out of scope (A7/A10). (A6)
- **You turned on PIN quick-unlock and someone takes your locked device.** With PIN unlock off — the default — a stolen locked device is guarded by your master password, which the device never stores anywhere. Turning it on puts a copy of your vault key on the same disk, wrapped by your PIN, so whoever holds that disk can guess PINs offline, on their own hardware, with no attempt limit (the limit Myne enforces applies only to guesses made through the app). Six digits plus Myne's deliberately slow key derivation makes that expensive, not impossible. If a stolen device is in your threat model, leave PIN unlock off, or use the biometric option where your device offers it — that key stays in hardware and cannot be copied off. (A5)
- **You set a fast unlock on a shared (member) vault, and someone takes that device.** A shared vault you joined by identity has no vault password of its own; its portable credential is your recovery phrase. Setting a device-local credential (`vault.member-unlock`) writes a copy of that vault's group and items keys, wrapped by your password or PIN, to the same disk, so a stolen locked device can be attacked offline against the credential, exactly as the PIN case above (the biometric method is exempt, as its key is hardware-bound). Worse in one respect: the same seal carries your person keypair, so that copy also opens your identity in your *other* vaults (the trade recorded in `decisions/protocol/0622` and extended to members in `decisions/protocol/0653`). If a stolen device is in your threat model, clear that credential afterwards (`sync_person_clear_member_unlock` in Settings) and unlock with your recovery phrase, which stores nothing new on disk; since `decisions/ui/0668` the accept flow requires the password rather than offering it, so declining it at accept is no longer a way to avoid this. (A5)
- **Malware is running on your device with your user permissions.** A keylogger reads your master password. A screen scraper reads your decrypted notes. Active malware defeats any user-mode encryption — no notes app can defend against this. (A7)
- **Someone forces you to enter your password.** Coercion, "rubber-hose cryptanalysis", or legal compulsion to disclose. Myne does not implement plausibly-deniable encryption. The data is exposed. (A12)
- **A nation-state-class adversary targets you specifically.** Zero-day exploits against your operating system, hardware implants, supply-chain attacks against the release-signing key. We use boring well-vetted primitives and publish the spec, but we do not claim protection at this level. (A10)
- **Traffic analysis reveals usage patterns.** A network observer or the sync server can see *that* you synced and *roughly how much* (blob count and size), even though they cannot see *what*. Myne does not pad or randomize sync timing in v1. (A13) **And a vault created under an identity is uploaded without you pressing anything:** once the app is pointed at a server and your identity is registered, the sync cycle does the publish itself, so "I connected a server" is also "I published this vault". There is no per-vault publish switch to leave off in this version; stopping publication is tracked separately (myne-md/myne#975).
- **Someone learns your account number.** Your account number is not a password, but it is not nothing either. Anyone who has it can ask the sync server for your account's encrypted key file from anywhere in the world, with no device of yours and no login, that is how a new device of *yours* joins, and the server cannot tell the two apart. The file does not reveal anything on its own; what it gives someone is the material to start guessing your master password on their own hardware, for as long as they like, with nothing to slow them down but the strength of that password. So treat the number like cash rather than like a username: it is fine to type into your own devices, and it is not something to post in a screenshot or a support thread. (This applies only if you use sync. A local-only vault has nothing on a server.) **Amended 2026-10-22 (myne-md/myne#911):** this holds for a vault that has its own account number. A vault born under your person identity has none, and is not addressed by one: its sync namespace is a random 16-byte id (`protocol.md` §9.3.5), so knowing a person's number does not locate any of their vaults' metadata. The person number itself is derived from the phrase, so there is nothing to lose and nothing to guess at.
- **One phrase gets you back to being you, and opens no vault (myne-md/myne#938, `decisions/protocol/0758`; the previous reading, "one phrase now opens every vault you own", was true of `NTV7` from 2026-10-22 to 2026-10-30 and is not true of the generation this version writes).** A vault born under your person identity seals its `root_key` under the password wrap alone (`protocol.md` §9.3.6), so the identity phrase is not a vault credential at all: it derives the person keypair and the account number, which is what a new device needs to be *you*, and it decrypts no note on any vault that person owns. Every one of those vaults still asks for its own password. The phrase does not rotate and there is no way to revoke it, so its blast radius is the identity: whoever holds the words can be you, list your vaults by name, receive what you are shared into and sign as you, and still cannot read a single note. Two things narrow the exposure and neither bounds the damage. The phrase is never stored in the clear (the sealed frame is AEAD-encrypted, one atomic write), and a vault born under the identity shows no **recovery kit** at all, so the identity ceremony is the one place the words appear. **A device holding the phrase is not held for approval.** A device that proved the identity phrase declares the recovery path and **self-approves** (`server-protocol.md` §3.1a, §3.1), so a stolen phrase alone is enough for an attacker to attach a device that can read and write every vault the identity owns, silently, with no existing device asked. The only bound is revocation from one of the other devices (`/auth/devices/revoke`), which is an availability control: it stops that device syncing, and it cannot un-read what the device already pulled. The vault password still works independently, and on this generation it is the only thing that opens the notes, so losing the phrase alone does not lock you out and losing the password alone is not survivable. **What it does not bound:** anyone who has the words and one approved device, now or in ten years, is you. **The server also learns something it did not before.** Vaults that used to be unrelated accounts, each with its own number, are now bindings under one person account, so the operator can group them and read "this person owns these N vaults", and reads that mapping per request rather than once at signup. That is the collaboration social graph already accepted in `decisions.md` 0459, extended from who shares a vault to who owns which vaults; it is linkability and not access, since knowing two vaults belong to one account opens neither. On the web the same phrase is reachable from served code, which A18 rates. (A18, A1, A2)
- **Someone in your household reads your recovery phrase.** The phrase is bearer-secret. Anyone with the phrase becomes you. Store it like cash, not like a password. **And a phrase you already wrote down can stop being the way in:** a vault that still carries its own recovery phrase keeps it until that vault is upgraded, and the upgrade retires it for good. Two steps got here. The explicit upgrade (`myne-md/myne#911`) zeroed a `vault.meta` recovery wrap and left the identity phrase as the second door; since myne-md/myne#938 (`decisions/protocol/0758`) that door is gone too, so an upgraded vault opens with its password and nothing else, and the upgrade screen says exactly that before it runs. A phrase you wrote down for a vault you upgraded is a phrase that opens nothing, which is worth knowing before you go looking for it in a drawer.
- **Files you export are not protected.** When you export a note or your vault as plain markdown, export a PDF, or export attachments, the resulting files are ordinary unencrypted files. Myne encrypts what it stores; it does not protect what you deliberately take out. Anyone who can reach where they landed can read them. **The phrase and account number you can save to a file from the creation screen work the same way**: the result is an ordinary plain-text file, and anyone who reads it holds your identity (myne-md/myne#938: they can become you on a new device and list your vaults; they still need each vault's password to read one, and that password is never in the file). Where your operating system supports it, Myne writes that file readable only by your own user account, and never touches it again. After that, protecting it is yours.
- **On a phone or tablet, every export first lands in the app's own `Documents` folder, and you then choose where a copy goes.** On a computer and in the browser, Myne asks you where to save and writes there. A phone app cannot write straight to a folder you pick from its own code, so on Android Myne opens the system's own save dialog and writes the file wherever you choose there, and on iPhone it opens the share sheet, whose "Save to Files" is how you choose. In both cases Myne also keeps the file in its private folder, so a dismissed dialog does not lose it. That folder is not one you can browse into on either system, so the dialog is your only route to the file. Two consequences worth knowing. Your phone's automatic cloud backup normally copies that folder to iCloud or Google Drive, which would carry a saved recovery phrase off your device; Myne tells both systems to leave that folder out of the backup, though we have not yet been able to confirm on a real device that they obey. And if you saved a recovery phrase on a phone before that change, a copy of it is already in an existing backup and nothing in the app can reach in and remove it. If that applies to you, treat the phrase as exposed and create a new vault.
- **You use the web client, and whoever serves it decides to target you.** The desktop and mobile apps are downloaded once and stay installed, so a substituted copy reaches only that one download and, if you kept the file you downloaded, it is still on your disk to compare against the hash we publish for the current release. Nothing does that comparison for you: it is manual and opt-in, and a user who downloads and runs without checking is protected by none of it. There is no Windows build and no installable iOS build to check. Two things this bullet used to claim are not true and are corrected here rather than left standing (entry 0534, extending 0496's correction of TB7 and A9 into this section, which that sweep missed): the desktop installers carry **no signature your operating system checks**, and nothing verifies a signature at launch. Only the Android package is OS-verified, because Android refuses to install an unsigned one. The web client is different in a way none of that repairs: it is re-sent to your browser every time you open the page, and your browser checks nothing on it. That means the operator of the web address (whoever runs the copy you opened) is able to send *you specifically* a modified version while everyone else gets the normal one, and the modified version would see your password as you type it. We record a hash of every released bundle before it is deployed, and the origin republishes it, so a substitution is detectable to anyone who has the record. That record lives in a private repository today, so it is not something you can check independently, which is a gap and not a control. Catching a substitution afterwards would not stop it from reaching you either, and the app does not warn you in the moment. If this is in your threat model, use the desktop or mobile app, which do not have this property, or run your own copy of the web client. (A15)
- **A fake site asks you for your recovery phrase, and you give it.** Anyone who gets those 24 words plus your account number becomes you from anywhere: they can register a device, see every vault your account owns by name, read what you are shared into and keep doing it forever, because the phrase does not change when you change your password and there is no way to revoke it. **What those words do not do is open a note** (myne-md/myne#938, `decisions/protocol/0758`): every vault asks for its own password, which the phrase does not derive and the server does not hold. Before that change the same theft read every vault you owned, so the loss is smaller and it is still a total loss of the identity. What has changed is how much help Myne can give you in avoiding it. Until 2026-08-15 (entry 0514) the web version could not create an account or recover one, so it had no reason to ever ask for the phrase, and "no Myne page asks for your 24 words" was a fact you could rely on without checking anything. It can ask now. It asks at creation, in an **identity** recovery you started after losing a device, to register a person identity (from the identity home, or from Settings → Sync on a device that already holds a vault), and to add a device. It no longer asks in a vault recovery, because an `NTV8` vault has no phrase path to recover into (`decisions/protocol/0758`; §9.3.6 forbids an unlock surface from offering one). **There is no longer a short rule that tells you when a prompt is legitimate**, so the check is yours: read the address before you type those words, and if anything reached you as a link in a message or an email, do not type them at all. Desktop and mobile do not have this problem: an app on your machine cannot be impersonated by a web address. (A18)
- **A browser extension you installed can read what is on the page.** Extensions you grant access to a site can read everything that site displays and everything you type into it — your recovery phrase while it is on screen, the words you type when recovering, and your master password every time you unlock. This is how browser extensions work; no setting in Myne changes it, and the page cannot detect it. If you are not certain about every extension in your browser profile, create your account and do any recovery in the desktop or mobile app, or use a clean browser profile for those steps. (A19)
- **You install a compromised plugin** (when plugin support eventually ships). Plugins run inside the trust boundary of the client and can read decrypted notes. v1 has no plugin API for this reason; future plugin support will be capability-scoped from the first design.
- **The sync-server operator** (whoever runs the server your client is pointed at) **acts maliciously.** The protocol is symmetric — the server cannot decrypt your notes. But a malicious operator can refuse service, retain ciphertext after deletion, or correlate metadata. The architecture limits damage; it does not prevent operator misbehavior of these kinds.
- **A browser extension you installed is compromised by a malicious update.** Myne's clipper extension is keyless — even fully compromised, it cannot read, decrypt, or delete your notes (it can only *create* clips, and only while your desktop vault is unlocked). The worst it can do is write misleading or spam notes into your vault; those render safely and you can see and delete them. It cannot exfiltrate your vault. (A14)

## 6. Compensating Controls (no audit budget)

Myne has no budget for a paid third-party cryptographic audit. The substitute is a layered set of public, free, verifiable controls. Each control is a real engineering commitment, not a marketing line.

- **Public protocol specification** — `spec/protocol.md`. KDF parameters, AEAD construction, key hierarchy, recovery derivation, account-number scheme, sync wire format. Detailed enough that an academic cryptographer can review it without source-code archaeology.
- **Public threat model** — this document. Updated in the same change as any architectural shift that affects guarantees.
- **Public statement of limits** — §5 (*NOT Protected Against*) and §7 (*Known Limitations & Open Issues*) of this document. There is no separate `limits.md`; these two sections are the limits of record, and §5 is written in plain language for exactly this purpose. Marketing and in-product copy is reviewed against them, and copy that claims more than they allow is rejected. The order is normative (this document's preamble): downgrade the claim to match, or change the threat model first — with a `decisions.md` entry justifying the change — and only then the copy. Never ship the copy and fix the threat model afterwards.
- **Reproducible builds** — **shipped for the web bundle and the server image; not for desktop.** The web bundle rebuilds byte-identically **in a reference build environment** (`linux/amd64`, built from `web/container/Dockerfile`, reachable from any host through emulation), and every released hash goes to an append-only record (`web-bundle-hashes.md`) that is **not public today**, so the outsider this control is for cannot read it (entry 0534). The qualifier is load-bearing rather than cosmetic: a rebuild on a different architecture differs, because Cargo hashes the build host's target triple into every crate's `-C metadata` and that reaches the emitted wasm, so a third party verifies by entering the reference environment rather than by building natively (entry 0498). Desktop artifacts are built locally, are not reproducible, and — with every `myne-md` repository private — have no public source to be reproduced *from*. This bullet described all three as done; it is corrected here rather than left as a compensating control the project does not have (entry 0496).
- **Signed release hashes, not code-signed releases** — each release's `SHA256SUMS` is signed with an offline minisign key and cross-published on a second origin; the desktop installers themselves are **unsigned**, so the OS warns on install (the Android `.apk` is signed, because Android requires it). The auto-update payloads are signed with the same offline key (TB7, since 2026-10-07), so the key now protects one more thing. Windows Authenticode remains unpurchased; the Apple Developer ID is owned and used for the notarized macOS build since 2026-10-07 (this bullet claimed both were in use until 2026-08-25).
- **Hash-pinned dependencies** — lockfiles checked into the repository for npm, Go modules, and any WASM artifacts. Dependency updates require explicit review.
- **`SECURITY.md` + `.well-known/security.txt`** — vulnerability disclosure policy with a clear contact channel and acknowledged response window.
- **Public bug bounty (acknowledgment-only acceptable in v1)** — a documented page listing past reports and the researchers credited. Cash bounties may follow once the project sustains revenue.
- **Spec-then-bounty cadence** — protocol changes ship as drafts with a public review period before they reach production clients. The privacy-research community is invited to comment.
- **Engagement with the privacy-research community before HN launch** — informal review by at least one external cryptographer or security researcher, with their feedback acknowledged in release notes. Not equivalent to a paid audit, but materially better than silence.
- **Native screen-capture exclusion (Windows 10 build 19041+ only; the macOS call is retained but nothing is claimed for it).** `tauri::WebviewWindow::set_content_protected(true)` is applied at app startup, wrapping `WDA_EXCLUDEFROMCAPTURE` on Windows and `NSWindowSharingNone` on macOS. Best-effort defense-in-depth layer within A7's existing classification — does not change A7's best-effort status. **This layer ONLY affects OS-level capture paths; it does NOT defend against active in-process malware (keyloggers, framebuffer scrapers, V8 string-heap inspection).** The per-platform reality is asymmetric and is stated per platform rather than as one sentence, because a single sentence is what made this item overclaim for a year (`decisions.md` 2026-08-15, which supersedes the macOS clause of 2026-05-27 "Screen-capture protection: per-platform best-effort discipline"):
  - **Windows 10 build 19041 and later: effective, and this is the one platform where the layer earns its place.** The main window, and every open detached (pop-out) note window (myne#680; see the §7 detached-window bullet for how each pop-out is protected and torn down), are excluded from every capture path the Desktop Window Manager composes: OS screenshot APIs, `Windows.Graphics.Capture`, DXGI Desktop Duplication, Microsoft Recall, and the third-party remote-control tools that ride those pipelines. It does **not** cover three classes. (1) Modes where DWM is not composing — Microsoft's own reference states the feature "works only when the Desktop Window Manager(DWM) is composing the desktop", and legacy mirror-driver capture modes disable composition to work. (2) Capture below the compositor, at the GPU or kernel level. (3) Any process running as the user, which can reset the affinity to `WDA_NONE`; that is already inside A7's assumption and is the reason this is a layer and not a boundary. Microsoft additionally disclaims the API as a security or DRM guarantee in the reference itself. Windows builds below 19041 silently degrade `WDA_EXCLUDEFROMCAPTURE` to `WDA_MONITOR` (a black box, not exclusion) rather than failing.
  - **macOS — the call is made, no protection is claimed.** Apple's own API reference documents `NSWindowSharingNone` as "a legacy constant that macOS no longer uses" and instructs developers not to use it to hide content from capture; Apple Developer Technical Support states there are no public APIs for preventing screen capture. The call is retained because it still has an observable effect on some capture paths and costs nothing to keep, but **Myne claims no macOS coverage, including against remote-control screen sharing.** The symbol is not formally deprecated, so no compiler warning, lint or repo guard signals this — the claim can only be kept honest by this document.
  - **Linux — no protection applied, on any display server.** Neither X11 nor Wayland lets an application request exclusion (§7).
  - **iOS and Android are both APPLIED and both UNVERIFIED, by two mechanisms with two different confidence levels (added 2026-10-16; the list above had no mobile entry, and a reader filled the gap with "not covered").** The mobile client has none of `set_content_protected`'s platforms, so its protection is a host-side hook (`apply_screen_capture`, `screen_guard.rs`, tracing-parity with the desktop `screen_capture.rs`) driven by the vault's own `screen_capture_protection` preference. **That control is a post-unlock Settings toggle, and mobile has NO device-local override**: the `<app_config_dir>/screen-capture.json` escape hatch and its process flag are desktop-only, so a phone cannot disable protection before unlocking a vault and cannot disable it afterwards without unlocking one. **Android** sets `FLAG_SECURE` on the window from the first frame through the in-repo Kotlin plugin (`mobile/src-tauri/tauri-plugin-screen-guard/`). That is a documented platform flag, and what IS verified about the pre-unlock screens is the refusal rather than the effect (`set_screen_capture_protection_rejects_when_slot_empty`): the setter will not turn protection off while no vault is unlocked, so nothing can switch it off before the app has a vault. Whether the flag is ON from the first frame is the same §7 walk as the iOS arm above, at rows 7.8 to 7.11, of which 7.11 is the cold-start capture before unlock; **so both mobile arms are applied in code and unobserved on hardware**, and the difference between them is the strength of the documentation behind the technique, not the state of the evidence. **iOS** splices a secure `UITextField` layer into the window at app STARTUP (the post-unlock variant crashed on device), `objc2::exception::catch`-guarded, with the iOS-17 sublayer branch. **The iOS arm is applied in code and UNVERIFIED on real hardware**, and that is where the two platforms genuinely differ: Android's arm rests on a documented flag, while the iOS one is the undocumented-subview technique with an OS-version branch and no Apple guarantee, so its evidence is the device walk (`mobile/docs/dev/device-matrix.md` §7, rows 7.1 to 7.7 and soak row 7.6), which has not been run and whose failure reverts the iOS apply to a no-op. Neither platform exposes a capture exemption to other apps, and neither protects against a capture taken on ANOTHER device pointed at the screen, which is a limit no client here can close rather than something shared with the macOS and Linux rows above.
