  **The pre-unlock surface is protected regardless of any saved *vault* preference, with one consented device-local exception (myne-md/myne#482).** `decisions.md` 0135 named the property without that qualifier, and three shipped decisions then composed into a lockout: protection is always ON before unlock, the toggle that could clear it is a field of the AEAD-sealed `vault.prefs`, and the control that writes it is post-unlock. On Windows that left a user on remote-control software or a screen magnifier looking at a black rectangle with the account number, the 24-word recovery-phrase ceremony and the device-join QR behind it, an accessibility failure and a data-loss path at once. The exception is a **device-local** override (`<app_config_dir>/screen-capture.json`, plus a `--disable-screen-capture-protection` process flag, which is honoured both at startup and on a RELAUNCH, since the documented recovery is a user reaching for a terminal while the black window is already up. The relaunch path is the only one that reaches an already-unlocked session, and it shares its argv vector with the `myne://` deep-link ingress §7 rates as website-reachable, so an argv carrying a deep link refuses the flag outright and that ingress gains no authority here), reachable from a keyboard-focusable control on every pre-unlock screen and set through an explicit consent step that states the cost. **That consent step guards the PRE-UNLOCK control, and it is a UI gate rather than a host-side one.** The post-unlock control is a plain toggle beside its helper text, with no confirmation: the user there is authenticated, deliberate and reading the effect, and a second dialog over an authenticated settings toggle is friction rather than consent. **It is ONE row now, and it writes BOTH stores** (`decisions/security/0705`, narrowing `decisions/security/0562`), device file first: the setting used to render as two peer rows, which read as one setting duplicated and left the user to work out which of two helper sentences described the lock screen. The consequence is stated rather than left to be inferred: turning protection off from Settings now turns it off before unlock **on that device** as well, so the property this paragraph opens with reads "protected before unlock unless a device-local override says otherwise, and an authenticated user may set that override from the single Settings row". The preference half still syncs, so another device inherits the post-unlock choice and keeps its own pre-unlock state, and a replay of `vault.prefs` alone does NOT reach pre-unlock protection (§7 states why). The pre-unlock escape still writes the file ALONE, because a sealed preference is unreachable before unlock, which is also why the Settings row reads the EFFECTIVE state rather than the preference. What follows is about the gate the pre-unlock control does have:  `screen-capture:default` is granted to the main window unconditionally and the setter takes its boolean from page context, so code executing in the WebView can persist the override without it. **The row that empowers is A8, not A7**, and naming A7 here would discharge the new capability against the one adversary that never needed it: A7 runs natively with the user's privileges and can call `SetWindowDisplayAffinity(WDA_NONE)` itself, exactly as this section records above, so it gains nothing. A compromised bundled dependency (A8) executes in page context only and could not reach that call. **What the slot gate bought before this change was narrower than "nothing", and saying "nothing" would send the next reader to the wrong baseline**: `prefs:set_screen_capture_protection` is in the `prefs:default` bundle granted to the main window and takes its boolean from page context, so A8 with an **unlocked** vault could already clear the affinity, for the remainder of that unlocked session only, since the next lock's `apply(app, true, "lock_reset")` re-forced it. What A8 gains now is new in four ways, and is stated rather than implied: a write that is reachable **pre-unlock**, is device-wide, survives relaunch, and is not undone by the lock-time re-apply. **What it is worth to A8 is nonetheless small, and that is the reason a host-side gate was rejected rather than merely skipped**: code running in this WebView already reads the unlock password field, the 24-word phrase and the account number straight out of the DOM, so suppressing the capture affinity adds nothing it does not already hold. It helps only a second, separate observer who can watch the screen and cannot execute in the page. Against that, the only host-side gate that page context could not satisfy is a native modal, this codebase has no such gate anywhere (not on vault deletion, not on a password change), and it would stand in front of the one control whose users are the people who cannot see the window. `decisions.md` 0562 records that trade as a sub-decision, and the position is reversible: a native confirmation becomes the right answer the moment this surface acquires an asset the page cannot already read. **A second, smaller capability change rides with it and is rated here rather than left implied.** The re-show that idle auto-lock needs is now one host command page context invokes (`screen-capture:allow-screen-capture-show-protected`), and it replaced `core:window:allow-show`. That is a trade, not a narrowing, and the code that used to call it a narrowing has been corrected: `core:window:default` is 28 grants and every one is a read, so `unminimize` and `set_focus` were never available to the page and now are, while the one mutator it did have is withdrawn. What the page gains is **nuisance-class and not a data one**: a forced un-minimise and a focus steal, with no argument it can choose and no state it can name, on a window whose contents it can already read through the DOM. It moves no rating and opens no boundary; it is written down because a capability that widens while its own comments say it narrows is the shape a reviewer stops reading. It is device-wide rather than pre-unlock-only, because an override that lapsed at unlock would put the black window straight back in front of the person it exists for, and while it is set the per-vault preference is superseded rather than silently ignored. What it discloses at rest is in §7. (`decisions.md` 0562.)

  Users in environments where untrusted screen recording or remote control is active should not rely on this layer, and on macOS and Linux should not expect it at all.

## 7. Known Limitations & Open Issues

Items that are acknowledged as imperfect today and will be revisited. Captured here to prevent silent drift.

- **A click on model-authored text may open an in-vault note, and can do nothing else (myne-md/myne#867; `decisions/ai/0692`).** The chat transcript renders a note name the model wrote as a link, which adds a click surface to a surface this document already treats as prompt-injection reachable: the notes a run reads are attacker-influenceable content, and the answer quotes them. The click is bounded on four axes, and each is a property of the implementation rather than a policy. **(1) An exact match only.** The candidate set is the strings that name a note the vault already holds: a vault-relative path, that path without its trailing `.md`, or a bare title, taken from the resolver's own `notePath` projection, which the wikilink picker, the resolve chip and the conversion command already share. **One shape is deliberately NOT a candidate**: the path-without-extension form of a note that has no title, because that form is a bare FOLDER (`Projects/`) rather than a note name, and admitting it would let a mention of a folder in prose, or a folder wikilink whose label equals its target, open an untitled note the reader never named. There is no fuzzy matching, and a text that is only a prefix of a real title (`Pixel 10`, on a vault holding `Pixel 10 Graphene OS`) stays plain text. **(2) Nothing outside the vault.** The candidate set is the vault's own note set, so a mention can name no path the vault does not contain and no file outside it. **(3) It opens a tab and does nothing else, with ONE coupling stated rather than left out.** No request is sent, no setting changes, no write occurs, and on the sidebar chat specifically the press also makes the opened note the composer's DEFAULT SCOPE (`defaultGrantFor(openNoteId)`), so the user's next send in that pane is grounded on the note whose name the model produced. That is the shape a prompt-injection reader needs: the bullet admits a mention can name any note the vault holds, so a hostile note that steers the model into naming a note the run was never granted can become the next run's scope with one press and the user's next send. It is not a boundary violation: the press itself sends nothing, and the `ai_excluded_folders` clamp still refuses an excluded single-note scope. and neither renderer (`ChatMarkdown` on desktop and web, `mobile/src/components/MentionText.tsx` on the phone, both on the one shared matcher `src/notes/ai/noteMentions.ts`) performs navigation or a host call of its own: the surface passes it a callback, and a caller that supplies none renders the pre-change plain text. **(4) It does not widen what the model may reach, and the candidate set is the VAULT's rather than the run's.** The click tells the model nothing: no request is sent, no state the model can observe changes, and the candidate set is never given to it in any form. **The set is bound (2)'s set, not the run's scope, and this bullet said "in the run's scope" until the review of myne-md/myne#867 measured it:** it is the vault's own note set, so a mention can be offered for a note the run was never granted, including one inside an `ai_excluded_folders` folder. That is deliberate rather than an omission, and the reason is what the exclusion control does: it governs what AI reads and sends, and this link sends nothing, reads nothing and grounds nothing. The model's answer is what it already wrote, and a name inside it becomes pressable whether or not the name's note was in scope. What the user gets is therefore a suggestion they must still press, in the same class as the model proposing an edit the user must approve, and a folder excluded from AI is not revealed by it: the candidate set is never transmitted, and the text that produced the mention came from a note the run WAS granted. **No adversary rating changes**, no boundary moves, and nothing on this path reaches **TB5**.

- **The automatic update check is a new egress, and it is default-on (2026-10-07, entry 0649).** The desktop fetches a static signed manifest from `downloads.myne.md` carrying the running version and platform, ten seconds after the editor mounts and then every 24 hours while it stays open, and the user can turn it off in Settings to About. What it reveals is a version string, a platform and the client IP to a first-party origin that keeps no IP logs (`decisions/ops/0494`); it carries no account number, no device id and no vault id. This is the one egress the user does not name a provider for (sync is opt-in; the AI remote arm needs a named provider), so it is recorded here rather than folded into those. The dependency it admits (`reqwest`/`hyper`) is `decisions/security/0650`.

- **A failed web account creation can leave a registered account nobody can reach (myne-md/myne#174).** Where sync is required, creation runs `commit` -> `sync_set_endpoint` -> `sync_enroll` and only then displays the phrase, so a failure never leaves a user holding words for an account that does not exist. The rollback on that failure path is **local only**: it deletes the OPFS container and cancels the session. `sync_enroll` registers the account server-side before it seeds `vault.meta`, so a failure after registration leaves durable state across **TB5** — the Argon2id SCRAM verifier (**AS13**), and in the late case the seeded `vault.meta`, which §7's pre-auth `GET /vault.meta` bullet describes as the complete offline password-cracking target — for an account whose number the client has just discarded. No user path can reach it to delete it. Both payloads are ones TB5 is already trusted with, so this is an at-rest residue rather than a boundary violation, and it is recorded rather than mitigated: the compensating server-side abandon call, or retaining the account number so a retry re-enrols into the same registration instead of orphaning it, is owed. The E2E covers only total failure (a dead sync origin, so registration never lands); the partial case is untested.

- **The agent may write preferences, which is a new write surface with its own two bounds (myne-md/myne#850).** The in-app agent may now change a closed set of twelve settings (the note-placement family and the two display formats) instead of only proposing note edits. The surface is bounded twice, and the two bounds are deliberately different in kind. **First, by the offer**: a setting change is never shown to the user where the write cannot happen, so a build without the preference-write capability and a detached note window both offer nothing at all. **The mechanism on the detached-window arm is the client's own window-role check, and this paragraph named an ACL until the 2026-10-13 review corrected it** (`src/notes/agent/settingCatalogue.ts` answers with an empty offer when `detachedNoteId()` is set). The ACL is not on this path: the agent's setting write is an in-process call into the prefs plugin from the agent plugin's own command, not a `plugin:prefs|set-*` invocation, so no per-window grant is consulted. That makes the arm a **consistency bound rather than an enforcement**, and the difference is stated rather than glossed: a page inside a detached window already holds the vault plaintext, the `note-*` capability does grant `agent:default`, so such a page can start a run and call the apply. **And neither of the two lists involved is host-derived**: `agent_start` takes the offered ids and `agent_apply` takes the approved settings, both from page context, so what this arm bounds in practice is the app's own UI. What constrains a page writing an ARBITRARY setting is the second bound below together with `retain_proposed_settings`, which refuses a change the run never proposed and is the settings sibling of the note path's `retain_proposed_edits`; what it does not constrain is a page that supplies its own offer list first. Moving the window check host-side, into the agent's own apply and undo commands where the window label is reachable, is the change that would close that; it is owed and not done, and the bound is written as it is rather than as it was claimed to be. A change offered and then refused after approval would be a promise made by us at the moment the user was deciding, and that is what this bound protects. **Second, by derivation at the write**: the egress-control sets and every `DeviceLocal` field are refused whatever the client claims, and `myne-core` re-derives that and re-validates the value rather than trusting the proposal. Devices here means the device-local class, which is also why three settings the plan named for the display family (the theme, the editor text size and the show-edit-times toggle) are **not** offered: they are stored per machine and sync nothing, so a change would land on the machine that ran the agent and leave every other device showing the old value with nothing explaining why. They carry that reason on their index entry rather than being silently absent. **What is new in the at-rest and RAM posture, stated because it is the part that is genuinely new**: the run's applied record now holds previous **preference values** in memory, not only note identifiers, so an undo can restore the value a setting change replaced. That record is the same class of RAM-only, lock-scoped state as the note restore points: it dies with the session, is never persisted, and is never synced; the durable copy of a preference remains `vault.prefs`, which is the blob whose size the bullet below already discloses. **No adversary rating changes.** The write reaches no boundary a note write did not already reach, the agent's instructions carry setting IDS rather than any value the vault holds, and the model is told nothing about what a setting currently is. `design-notes/ai/0676`.
- **An approved device names itself, which is a new UNATTENDED write into `vault.prefs` with three bounds (myne-md/myne#973).** A device that has never had a name now writes one without a user in the loop: the row it labels is the one that otherwise prints its own hex code twice, because the title is derived from the same six characters. The value is not user content in the shipped client: it is the platform word the enrollment field is already pre-filled with ("Mac", "iPhone", "Linux PC", …). **It is not bounded by the backend, and saying it was would overstate a guarantee.** `set_device_name` accepts any non-blank string on both write paths; the automatic one adds the three bounds below and nothing about the value's content, exactly as the manual rename does. What holds the automatic entry to a platform word is the client that sends it. What lands is one entry in `device_names` (**AS27**), once per device, inside the `MYP1` envelope every other preference rides, on the same Class-1 sync and backup path. Nothing crosses **TB5**: no name field exists in the enrollment request or the roster response. **Three bounds, and each one is a loss the user would not connect to the name.** (1) **The vault plane only.** The map is a set-union field of the ACTIVE vault's prefs while a member session's roster is that person's own account, so on a member vault the id would label no row of the vault's roster and the name would replicate to every co-member of a group-shared map with no deletion. (2) **`vault.prefs` must already exist.** The §10.2.1 wholesale seed is keyed on that file being ABSENT and carries the account's device-local fields (`custom_theme`, `auto_lock_seconds`, the `editor_*` family), which the merge path can never adopt, so a write that created the blob would silently cost a joining device all of them. This bound is taken on `PrefsOrigin::Absent`, the write path's own signal, which matters because the first cycle persists the pulled head EARLY and seeds the preferences at its end: "this device has adopted a head" is true for minutes on a large account while "this device has prefs" is still false. **It is the bound this write enforces, not a claim about the seed's own read**: the seed branches on its own `io.read(..).ok()` being `None`, so an unreadable `vault.prefs` still reads as absent there and the wholesale write can replace a real blob. That gap is pre-existing, is not widened by this change, and is recorded as owed rather than described as closed. (3) **Insert only when absent, under the write mutex, and stamped at the floor.** The mutex serialises this process, not two devices, so the insert-only check alone cannot stop the write that matters: a device offline since somebody renamed it holds no entry, the check passes, and a `now` clock would beat the human write on every device under §10.2's per-key rule, with no history to recover it from. So the automatic entry carries **no clock**, which §10.2 reads as the weakest claim there is: any rename from any client outranks it permanently, and all this path can ever do is fill a key nobody has claimed. Absent rather than a literal zero, because the merge drops a zero clock as it carries the value forward, so a literal one would leave this device's shared-subset hash disagreeing with every peer's and cost a redundant push round. The value it puts there is a platform word, so losing that race costs nothing, and `device_names` has no unclocked writer (`0348` supplied the first caller on 2026-08-14, field clocks shipped 2026-07-13), so a clockless entry for the same key is not a state that exists. **What the observer learns** is the same class AS27 already records, plus two coarse increments: the blob's plaintext length steps by one name (XChaCha20 preserves length) and the write re-pushes the blob once, so A5 at rest and A13 on the wire read a per-device step and a prefs-edit event, both dominated by the `.myn`-count signal accepted above and bounded by §9.5.4's byte budget. **No adversary or asset rating changes**, and the fallback is unchanged: a device that is refused, or whose write fails, keeps its id-derived label and the row stays legible. (`design-notes/sync/0770`, which supersedes the "a nameless device keeps the fallback until renamed" posture of `decisions/sync/0348` clause 2.)
- **Recovery-phrase-only model has a known UX cost.** Bitwarden, 1Password, and Standard Notes all bleed users at this exact step. Myne holds the line in v1; if support volume becomes unmanageable, the response will be better UX (forced phrase confirmation, optional encrypted recovery file written to user-controlled storage) — *not* email recovery. Any change here triggers a threat-model amendment.
- **On-device OCR widens the untrusted-input parsing surface (MYND-441; TB9).** Image OCR adds an ML runtime (`rten` plus its gemm/SIMD/tensor/model-file/shape-inference crates and `flatbuffers`), three image decoders, and a PDF XObject/`FlateDecode` path — all of which parse **attacker-influenceable bytes** in the same process that holds `root_key`, in **every** build including the `--no-default-features` auditable one, and on mobile as well as desktop. The ingress is real, and **the routes are not the ones this sentence used to name** (corrected 2026-08-29, myne-md/myne#510; `decisions/ai/0542`). On every client an attachment the user **attaches** (file picker, paste or drop) carries bytes they did not author, and so does one that arrives by **sync** from a compromised second device; the shared sniffer (`core/src/plugins/attachments/sniff.rs`) admits anything on the `ALLOWLISTED_CONTENT_TYPES` list by magic bytes, and the parsing rows are gated on the sniffed type. On mobile the **OS share sheet** (**AS19**) is a third. The **browser clipper** (**TB10**) is *not* one today on either client: its per-image `attachment_import` seam is unimplemented, and `resniff_image`, which reads like the filter that would bound it, has no production caller and delegates to that same allowlist sniffer, so it narrows nothing. It becomes an ingress when that seam lands. This sentence previously named only the share sheet and the clipper, which **understated** the surface on desktop, where neither exists: a reader could conclude desktop parsing takes no attacker-influenceable input at all, which would license removing one of the limits below. Understating a guarantee is the same defect as overstating one. This is the same class as the Mermaid/embedded-HTML render surface already recorded here, and it is mitigated the same way rather than by trusting the input: everything is **pure Rust with no C/C++ decoder** (chosen over an `onnxruntime` binding and a PDF rasterizer precisely for this reason), the decoder runs under explicit pixel-dimension limits so a compression bomb is a clean `Failed` rather than an allocation abort, and both the decode and the model passes are wrapped in `catch_unwind` so one hostile attachment cannot abort an index build. Adversary impact is **A7**-class (an attacker who can already place a file on the device), which is best-effort in this model; no new adversary gains a capability, and no key material or plaintext crosses a boundary it did not already cross. Recorded because the dependency surface — not the data flow — is what changed.

  **The audio-decode path is the same shape and is now bounded on both clients (myne-md/myne#485 for mobile, myne-md/myne#510 for desktop).** A voice attachment reaching transcription is attacker-influenceable by the routes named above, and its WAV header declares the sample rate the decoder resamples from, so a declared rate far below 16 kHz expands the decoded buffer by the ratio. Both clients clamp the declared rate to `4_000..=384_000`, and both refuse a clip whose projected 16 kHz length passes a ceiling **derived from the § 9.9 attachment cap** (`protocol::MAX_ATTACHMENT_LEN / 2` output samples) rather than picked. The refusal is taken inside the parser, before the buffers are materialised rather than inside the resampler after them, and above the identity fast-path rather than below it, so a clip already at 16 kHz is measured too. The outcome is a clean typed refusal instead of an allocation abort or an OOM kill. Desktop reached this second: until myne-md/myne#510 its ceiling was derived from an 8-hour duration, which the attachment cap and the 4x clamp together put permanently out of reach, so the check was dead code and the worst case was roughly 768 MiB of live allocation across the four buffers a decode holds. It is now **320 MiB**, measured, and the number needs its shape stated: the peak falls at the RESAMPLE, at a declared rate just above 16 kHz, where the identity fast-path no longer applies and a second output buffer is allocated beside the mono one. At exactly 16 kHz the resampler returns its input by value and the peak is 256 MiB at the downmix instead. A first draft of this paragraph recorded that lower figure by measuring only the identity path. **Two residuals remain, and neither is closed by the above.** The recorder's own per-clip boundary counts samples rather than seconds, so on a capture device below 16 kHz it is clamped to the transcribable frame count as well, and the recorder additionally refuses to start at all outside the same `4_000..=384_000` window the decoder clamps to. The two together keep the invariant that no clip a Myne client records is refused by a Myne decoder: the first holds the length axis and the second the rate axis, and the length clamp alone leaves the invariant false for a device reporting below 4 kHz. That pair is a construction rather than a guarantee the format carries, and a WAV produced anywhere else is still refused on its merits. The rate refusal is not a defence against an adversary and is not claimed as one: the recorder's rate comes from the OS rather than from attacker-controlled bytes, unlike the decoder's copy of the same window. And **the wall clock is bounded by nothing**: an accepted clip at the ceiling is about 34 min 57 s of audio transcribed inside one `spawn_blocking`. Desktop's `transcribe_streaming` yields a `ControlFlow` per segment, so Stop and the vault-lock teardown reach the loop; mobile registers no cancel command, so there the attacker's best move changes axis rather than disappearing, from an allocation abort to pinning a core for many minutes on one tap. A7's rating does not move: this is availability of the app on the user's own device, not confidentiality, and an attacker who can place a file already has the ingress the row assumes.
- **Blob size leaks information.** Even with all content encrypted, the size of each ciphertext blob and the count of blobs per account reveal something about note volume and usage. v1 does not pad. A future revision may introduce blob-size bucketing or padding to a granularity (e.g., 4 KiB). **The encrypted search-index blob `vault.search` (MYND-29; `spec/protocol.md` §9.6) is in the same class:** to an at-rest disk observer (A5) / a future sync observer (A13), its size leaks a coarse signal about the vault's indexed vocabulary and total note volume — strictly less than, and dominated by, the `.myn`-count + per-blob-size signal already accepted above (an index built from N notes is bounded by the same N notes). Accepted for v0.1 under the same no-padding posture; the same future bucketing/padding revision would cover it. `vault.search` adds no *new* class of leak — it is a derived cache of content whose size signal is already exposed by the `.myn` files themselves. **The encrypted editor workspace-state blob `vault.workspace` (MYND-166; `spec/protocol.md` §9.7) is likewise in this class:** its size leaks only a coarse open-tab-count signal, far below the `.myn`-count signal already accepted. More importantly, persisting *which notes are open* is **activity metadata (AS3)** that v0.1 deliberately keeps inside the AEAD envelope (under `session_state_key`) rather than as plaintext local app state — so a disk/filesystem adversary (A4/A5) learns neither the open-note set nor (because the blob is written only on graceful lock/quit, never per tab-switch — on mobile, lock plus app-background, see the dedicated bullet below) the *timing* of note switches from its mtime. It adds no new plaintext metadata surface. **The per-attachment extracted-text cache `attachments-text/<id>` (`MYE1`, MYND-328; `spec/protocol.md` §9.15) is in the same derived-cache class:** it holds text extracted on-device from an attachment (PDF/Word/text) — as of MYND-270 the **on-device ASR transcript of an audio attachment** (speech-to-text of an `audio/wav` `MYA1` blob), and as of MYND-441 the **on-device OCR of an image attachment or a scanned, text-layer-less PDF**, both of which ride this **same `MYE1` derived-cache class** (no new asset) — sealed under the `root_key`-derived `attachment_text_key`, so a disk observer (A5) learns only a coarse per-attachment size signal — already bounded by the `MYA1` source size beside it — and never the text. Extraction, audio transcription and OCR are **on-device only** (no cloud OCR/STT/extraction service is ever contacted — a standing anti-feature; the OCR weights are bundled and the runtime has no network surface, asserted by `src-tauri/scripts/ai-no-network-check.sh`), so none of them crosses a new trust boundary. OCR is **not** gated on `ai_enabled` — it is search correctness rather than an AI feature, so it is written in every build, including `--no-default-features`. It is the same "decrypted-at-rest derivative of user content" already accepted for `vault.search`: a rebuildable cache, purged on attachment deletion for cryptographic-erase parity (§9.15.4). It adds no new class of leak.
- **The personal dictionary is synced state now, and its size steps with the user's own vocabulary (myne-md/myne#640).** `vault.dictionary` (`MYD1`/`MYD5`) was written and read on one device only; it is now a synced Class-1 singleton under a derived `personal-dictionary-sync-id`, so the plaintext (the user's added words, their vault-wide ignore set, and a per-word removal clock map retained indefinitely) leaves the device for the configured sync server, sealed under the family KEK. Two consequences are stated rather than implied. **First, the observer learns a size series**: XChaCha20 preserves plaintext length, so A5 at rest and A13 on the wire read a roughly per-word step that grows with the words ever added or removed and never shrinks while a tombstone is retained, bounded by §9.13.4's 4 MiB cap and dominated by the note-count signal already accepted above; each dictionary write is also a re-push, so an increment reads as a dictionary-edit event. **Second, a shared vault has ONE dictionary and every member may read and rewrite it** (`GroupRole::PersonalDictSyncId`, `sync-protocol.md` §10.2.1): a member's added or ignored word is visible to the co-members, inside the group tier the family's KEK already used. The words themselves stay inside the AEAD envelope, so neither the server nor a disk observer reads a word; what is new is *who can open them* on a shared vault, and that is the same boundary the group tier already sets for notes. **No adversary rating changes.** (`design-notes/protocol/0710`.)
- **`vault.prefs` size now steps with the folder count, and with folders long deleted (myne-md/myne#613).** Until this change the preferences blob was near-constant: its size tracked how many FEATURES had settings, so it carried no user-content signal worth stating. At-fill registration puts every folder a note lands in into `deliberate_folders` (`spec/protocol.md` §9.5.2), and the per-entry removal clocks that make a folder delete propagate (`spec/sync-protocol.md` §10.2) are retained indefinitely, so the plaintext length is now a function of two user-content quantities: how many folders the vault has, and how many it has ever had. XChaCha20 preserves plaintext length exactly, so an at-rest disk observer (A5) and a sync observer (A13) read a roughly 68-byte step per registered folder, and because the blob is re-encrypted and re-pushed on every preferences write, they read the increments as folder-creation events. **AS3** covers folder and hierarchy STRUCTURE, which stays inside the AEAD envelope: the observer learns neither a folder's name nor its place in the tree, only a count and a coarse change signal. Two things bound it. The count is bounded by the byte budget of §9.5.4, which is what stops the signal growing without limit. And it is strictly coarser than, and dominated by, the `.myn`-count signal already accepted in the bullet above, since a vault with N folders has at least N notes unless the user made empty ones. Accepted for v0.1 under the same no-padding posture as every blob above; the same future bucketing or padding revision would cover it. Unlike the derived caches in that bullet, this one is NOT rebuildable from the notes and is not a cache, which is why it is stated separately rather than folded in.
- **The opt-in diagnostic log is a plaintext usage record at rest, bounded by a number, and erased when the toggle goes off (myne-md/myne#860).** The desktop client can write a diagnostic log while the user has it switched on. **Logging is OFF by default and nothing is written until it is turned on**, and the flag that controls it is a one-boolean plaintext file in the device-local plaintext family (`<app_config_dir>/diagnostic-log.json`), which is also the shape the file takes: **turning it off writes `false` rather than removing the file**, and the write path always writes and never unlinks, so what presence discloses is that logging was turned on here at least once, not never. That residual survives the user switching it back off, which leaves `{"enabled": false}` on disk rather than nothing, and it is stated rather than mitigated the way `screen-capture.json` (§7, further down) states its own. **Reach: the switch takes its boolean from page context** (`plugin:diagnostics|diagnostic_log_enabled_set`), on the same main window every pre-unlock screen renders in, with no unlock and no consent step, so code executing in the WebView (A8) can turn the log on without the user. That is the shape `screen-capture.json`'s bullet already records for its own override, and it is stated here for the same reason: the switch is a user-facing control, not a cryptographic boundary, and this bullet is where a reader learns which of the two it is. **What a reader gets (A5 lost or stolen, locked; A7 malware) is a bounded usage record, never content.** The content claim is enforced at TWO layers, and both are needed. **The file layer admits only the app's own `tracing` targets** (`FILE_TARGETS` in `src-tauri/src/logging`), so a dependency crate's records are not written at all: the sink is process-global and `try_init` also installs `LogTracer`, so without that filter `hyper`, `automerge` or `tauri`'s own crates would be writing into a file the user is invited to publish. And `scripts/check-tracing-discipline.mjs` fails the build if a `%` Display formatter reaches `info` or above, which are the levels the file layer records (it carries its own fixed filter and deliberately ignores `RUST_LOG`, so a `debug!` value reaches disk only where the filter names its target and never by an environment variable), or if a value is interpolated into a message, which is the shape no field-name rule can inspect, or if a field VALUE builds an unbounded string (`format!`, `concat!`, `String::from`, `to_string`, `to_owned`), which no field-name rule can bound either. **One target is recorded below that floor, and it is named rather than implied** (`design-notes/ops/0768`): `myne_core::agent` is admitted at `debug`, because a run that stopped is the report this log exists for and the run loop's own trail (`iteration`, the six `tool` verbs with entry/hit/body counts, and `reply`'s shape counts) is all `debug`-level, so a fixed `info` floor left a failure window holding two search-index heartbeats. The widening is ONE TARGET, not a level: `myne_core`'s own floor stays at `info` for every other module, the host's `myne::agent` target stays at `info`, the loop's own caps bound the volume (24 iterations, 8 live runs, inside the same three-file footprint), the ten sites carry integers, byte and character counts, static verb names and one enum shape, and the guard's `%` rule is extended to `debug` for exactly that module and no other, so the claim is machine-checked where the level fell. A `debug!` under any other admitted target still cannot reach disk. The guard reads both macro spellings, the qualified `tracing::info!` and the bare `info!` in a file that imports from `tracing`, because a qualified-only scan reads nothing at all for the idiomatic form. **What it does not cover, stated rather than left to be discovered**: it does not hold field NAMES to an allowlist, it does not redden a future violation of the enumeration below (that enumeration is a one-time audit over the same sites plus the closed-enum rule), it does not read `println!`/`eprintln!`/`dbg!`, and its roots are the six source directories it scans (`core/src`, `src-tauri/src`, `format/src`, `protocol/src`, `cli/src` and `tor-sidecar/src`) rather than the file layer's own `FILE_TARGETS` list. That list carries target names matched by PREFIX with no separator boundary, so `"myne::"` admits `myne::ai` and `myne::agent`, and each crate-name entry admits any target that begins with it, from any tree. Admission is by target NAME, so the file layer's set is not a superset of the guard's by construction. What is measured today is that the guard's coverage contains what the file layer admits: every producer of an admitted target sits inside one of the six scanned roots, and the one linked first-party tree outside them (`theme-validator/src`) is outside the admitted set too: `cli/` and `tor-sidecar/` are scanned and their crates' targets are not admitted, which is the safe direction, while a listed name admits a target by NAME rather than by where the record was emitted. The audit behind that guard measured **zero** sites interpolating a message and no site emitting a path, a note id, an account hash, a title, a filename, plaintext, key material, a salt or a nonce as a value. **The bound is a number**: at most `RETAINED_FILES` (3) files, with `MAX_FILE_BYTES` (1 MiB) as the **rotation threshold** rather than a hard per-file cap (the check is taken before a record is written, so a single record larger than the threshold lands whole and that file exceeds it), oldest dropped first, so the footprint is a constant and not a function of how long the toggle stays on; the threshold is held to a floor derived from a MEASURED record size by the module's own test. **Turning the toggle off deletes the active log and every rotated file in the same action**, because withdrawing consent withdraws the data and a deferred erase loses to a kill. Files are created `0600` at create time inside a `0700` directory under the platform log directory, with the create-time detail stated because `0600` carries no group or other bits for umask to strip and a chmod afterwards would leave a window. **Both modes are Unix-only**: the create-time mode is `#[cfg(unix)]` and the non-Unix arm of `prepare_dir` is a bare `create_dir_all`, so on Windows neither mode exists and the file and its directory inherit the ACL of `%LOCALAPPDATA%`, under which the directory sits. **Two things this bullet does NOT claim.** The directory is `app_log_dir()` rather than the vault tree or `<app_data_dir>` on macOS and Windows, but on **Linux** it resolves INSIDE the data directory, so the separation from an OS backup is a macOS and Windows property. And **one artefact can exist while the toggle is off**: the desktop panic hook writes a single fixed-size crash record (compiled-in `file:line`, version, platform, no message and no payload, per `decisions/ops/0366`), bounded to one file that the next crash overwrites, shown on the surface and deleted either by an explicit action or by a report that includes it. That exception is what the feature's own rule admits and nothing else does: the hook is **host code rather than a `tracing` call site**, so no emitted event can reach it, and its payload is a closed set of scalars. **No adversary or asset rating moves**, and this is a §7 limitation rather than a new boundary or asset row because no boundary did. The one primitive A8 gains is named here rather than left in the code: `save_report` takes a destination from page context, so code executing in the WebView can write a plaintext bundle to any path outside the app data directory, without an unlock and **without the toggle ever being on**. The host refuses a destination whose canonical parent resolves inside any of the three stores this feature owns, which are the app data directory (which holds `vaults/`), the log directory and the config directory, and it refuses outright when a store cannot be resolved rather than skipping the comparison. That is the containment check `export` and `secret_export` make, and the residual is what is left after it: a destination outside all three is still page-chosen, so this is a second instance of a primitive the WebView already had rather than a new class. What makes it worth stating is that it does not require the user to have enabled anything. (`design-notes/ops/0690`.)
- **The copy-diagnostics block carries five bounded tokens about the vault, and the vault generation in it is a targeting fact rather than a value (myne-md/myne#1003).** A user who presses Copy diagnostics, on the settings panel, on the fail-closed screen, or on the phone's Settings → About, gets a flat `key: value` block they can paste into an issue, and since this change it carries `vaultGeneration`, `vaultAuthority`, `vaultBinding`, `vaultKeyAtRest` and `vaultSession` alongside the build identity it already carried. Every one of them is a closed enum token. `src/lib/diagnostics.ts` assembles the block field by field from a literal allowlist, so a field cannot arrive by accident, and a reading the client could not take is omitted rather than printed as a placeholder. **What the block deliberately does not carry**: no identifier (no note id, no account number, no account hash, no device id), no filesystem or OPFS path, no key material, no note content, no error message, no user-agent string and no timestamp. The one disclosure worth naming is the generation, because it is the discriminator §1 A18's radius turns on: `MYV1`, `NTV5` and `NTV6` carry a recovery wrap an offline phrase attack reaches and `NTV8` carries neither, so a paste says which radius that user faces. `vaultSession` also says whether an identity was open and whether a device key was at rest at the moment of failure. **Nothing is transmitted**: the only consumer is a clipboard the user operates, and there is no endpoint, no beacon and no automatic send. A5 and A7 read the same facts from the vault directory on the device, so no adversary gains a capability, and a diagnostics read that hangs or refuses costs those five lines rather than the whole block, because the block is what a user hands over when the app has already failed closed. No adversary or asset rating moves.

  **Two more readings joined the block on desktop (myne-md/myne#991), and they are measurements rather than enum tokens.** `storageUsedMb` used to print `navigator.storage.estimate()`, which measures the WebView's own origin store and therefore none of the vault, and `bundleHash` was absent because a bundle cannot contain the digest of its own bytes. Both are now answered by the host: `storageUsedMb` and `storageEntries` describe the **active vault directory** (total bytes of the files and links, and how many entries it is spread over), and `bundleHash` carries the SHA-256 of the **running executable**. Three consequences, stated rather than left to be found. The size is rounded to whole megabytes, which bounds the first; the entry count is exact, and the pair is a stable description of one vault, so two pastes from the same vault correlate and two pastes in one public thread distinguish one vault from another. That is coarser than the five closed-enum tokens beside it and still short of an identifier: no note id, no account hash, no device id, no path, no per-file size, no file name and no content, which is the line the block's "deliberately does not carry" list draws. The digest names the executable, not the installer, so it matches no published `SHA256SUMS` and is not comparable with the web client's served bundle hash under the same key. **A8 gains two page-reachable host reads, and they are named here for the reason `save_report` is.** `diagnostics:build_digest` and `diagnostics:vault_usage` are granted to the main window, take no argument, need no unlock and no auth permit, and return a digest and two numbers. `vault_usage` therefore reads while the vault is locked, which is deliberate: the fail-closed screen builds the same block, and both reads are `Metadata`-class: they are the same facts A5 and A7 read off the directory, they open no ciphertext, and TB3 and TB5 are unchanged. A webview-embedded script can poll the pair and watch the size grow, which is an activity signal of the coarsest kind and no finer than the blob mtimes beside it. **No adversary or asset rating changes.** Nothing here is transmitted: the only consumer is still the clipboard the user operates.

- **Named-workspace switch timing is coarsely observable at rest (bounded relaxation of §9.7.4).** Named, switchable workspaces (MYND-308; `spec/protocol.md` §9.7 `vault.workspace` `0x02`) add a hybrid save model whose active-slot pointer (`vault.prefs` `active_workspace`, §9.5.2) and layout descriptor must survive a crash. To avoid a per-switch file write — which would turn the `vault.workspace` / `vault.prefs` mtime into a high-resolution editor-activity log — the client **coalesces** these flushes to **at most once per ~5 minutes of active use, plus graceful exit / auto-lock** (the same time-coalescing pattern as snapshots below). The consequence is a deliberate, **bounded** relaxation of the prior graceful-exit-only cadence (§9.7.4): a filesystem adversary (A4/A7) can now infer that the user switched or rearranged workspaces *within* a ~5-minute window from the blob mtime — a coarse activity-timing signal, never the *content* of the switch. Workspace **names and layouts** remain inside the AEAD envelope under `session_state_key` (**AS3**) — the adversary learns neither which workspaces exist, nor their names, nor which notes any of them hold; only that *some* workspace activity occurred in a ~5-minute bucket. Accepted for v0.1 under the same at-rest-metadata posture as blob size + snapshot cadence; it is strictly coarser than the per-switch leak it replaces, and the crash-loss window is bounded to the same ~5 minutes. (`decisions.md` 2026-06-17 "Named, switchable workspaces"; SYNC-6.)
- **Mobile `vault.workspace` writes at backgrounding, not only at lock (bounded relaxation of §9.7.4).** The mobile client (MYND-561; `spec/protocol.md` §9.7.4 "Mobile exit analogs") persists the sidebar folder-expand set into `vault.workspace`, and resolves the desktop rule's "app quit" to **app-background** (`visibilitychange` → hidden / `pagehide`) alongside vault lock — because a phone app's normal death is an OS kill of a backgrounded process, which delivers no quit signal. A phone is backgrounded many times a day without locking, so to a filesystem adversary (A4/A5/A7) the blob's mtime is a **backgrounding-granularity** activity signal rather than the lock/quit-granularity one the desktop rule yields. Bounded on three axes: the write is **dirty-gated** (a session that never expanded or collapsed a folder writes nothing, so the mtime does not advance at all), it is **never per-interaction** (a folder toggle alone writes nothing — only an exit seam does), and the *content* — which folders are open — stays inside the AEAD envelope under `session_state_key` (**AS3**), so the adversary learns that *some* persisted view state changed before a backgrounding, never what. Accepted for v0.1 under the same at-rest-metadata posture as blob size, the MYND-308 coalesced workspace cadence above, and snapshot cadence below; the rejected alternative — writing only on lock — silently loses the state on the platform's normal termination path. (`decisions.md` 2026-07-25.)
- **Mobile `vault.indices` writes at its exit seams, and its mtime is a backgrounding-granularity signal (bounded relaxation, myne-md/myne#722).** The mobile client now writes the structured-index cache (`spec/protocol.md` §9.8, `MYI1`), which no mobile build had ever read or written, on the exit seams `vault.workspace` uses under the bullet above, with one subtraction: **manual lock, vault switch and app-background**, never the edit. **Idle auto-lock deliberately skips the flush** (§9.8.5's fail-closed arm: the key drop must not sit behind an AEAD encode of a large index), and the next unlock rebuilds the missing delta. Bounded on four axes. It is **dirty-gated**: the index is flushed at a seam only when it moved since the last write, so a session whose index never moved writes nothing and the mtime does not advance. It is **never per-interaction**: there is no settle timer and no per-sweep write, so editing for an hour produces at most one write, at the next seam. The **content** (titles, folders, tags, wikilink targets, typed property values, task records) stays inside the AEAD envelope under the `root_key`-derived `vault-indices-key` (**AS3**/**AS4**), so a filesystem adversary (A4/A5) or local malware (A7) learns that *some index activity preceded a backgrounding* in a session that changed something, never what changed and never which note. And the **size** is a derived-cache signal already in the blob-size class above: XChaCha20 preserves plaintext length, the size steps with indexed metadata volume, it is bounded by §9.8.5's 256 MiB cap, and it is dominated by the `.myn` count and per-note sizes beside it. The write is also ordered so the cache cannot become durably wrong: the client runs its pending index delta to completion before writing and skips the write when that pass fails, which is what keeps the next unlock's reconcile equal to a full rebuild (§9.8.4). The rejected alternative, writing only on lock, silently loses the index on the platform's normal termination path; the rejected alternative on the other side, a settle timer, makes the mtime an editing-activity log. **No adversary or asset rating changes**, and nothing here crosses **TB5**: the cache is never synced. (`design-notes/clients/0711-*.md`.)
- **Encrypted snapshots leak edit cadence at rest.** The per-note version-history feature (MYND-96; `spec/protocol.md` §9.1 `snapshots/`) writes an encrypted `.myn`-format blob per captured revision. To an at-rest disk observer (A5), the *count* of a note's snapshots and their on-disk *ordering* (UUIDv7-named) reveal a coarse signal about how often and how recently that note was edited — a signal the single-current-`.myn` model did not expose. This is accepted for v0.1 under the same no-padding posture as blob size above, and is bounded on several axes: snapshots are **local-only** (single-device; the sync server never receives them, so **AS6** — encrypted user-meaningful timestamps — stays intact), capture is **time-coalesced** (~5 min) rather than per-keystroke, retention is a **bounded window**, and a **flat** `snapshots/` directory (rather than per-note subdirectories) limits how legibly per-note counts are exposed. Snapshots add no *new* class of secret: their plaintext is the same note content already protected by the `.myn` files, sealed under the same `item_key`. Cryptographic erase is preserved — permanently deleting a note purges its snapshots (`decisions.md` 2026-06-08).
- **Encrypted CRDT notes (`NC05`) sync and back up their full edit-history — an AS6 exposure the `MY01` model kept local-only (automerge-sync-migration, `decisions.md` 2026-08-09).** The next-major CRDT note-type (`spec/protocol.md` §10.5) stores each note as one encrypted Automerge document whose payload is the note's **full change history**, not just its current text. Two consequences change the posture the `MY01` model + the local-only snapshots bullet above held: **(1)** unlike snapshots — which are deliberately kept off the server so **AS6** (user-meaningful edit timing) stays server-invisible — a CRDT note's history rides *inside* the synced blob and the `.myne-backup` container, so anyone who ever obtains that note's `item_key` (a `root_key` compromise, a leaked backup, a retained server ciphertext) can reconstruct **every past revision and deleted draft**, not merely the current content; and **(2)** the blob's *size* now grows with **edit count**, not content length, so blob size leaks a coarse per-note **edit-volume** signal beyond the existing size-leak class. This is **not** a forward-secrecy walk-back — v0.1 already declares no forward secrecy (§7 (4), `spec/protocol.md` §11) — it is a **widening of the retained-history exposure surface** the threat model states before the format ships. It is scoped to notes the user has explicitly migrated to `NC05` (migration is explicit + one-way, §10.4); a `MY01` note is unchanged. On the wire it is bounded exactly as every synced blob is (**TB5 intact** — the server sees only opaque ciphertext plus the existing size/timing/count metadata; the Automerge change-DAG, actor-ids, and change-hashes **never cross TB5 / the wire** — they are computed and compared only client-side. The device-local head frontier the sync loop compares (`doc_heads_hex`, persisted in `sync-state.json`) does sit **at rest in device-local plaintext**, under the same posture as the vault-head baseline (§9.16) — never synced, never backed up, visible only to an at-rest device / local-malware adversary (A5/A7) that already sees far more; it is not an AEAD-envelope contents leak on the wire, `decisions.md` 2026-08-09). The retained-history **depth** is bounded by a compaction/retention policy tracked separately (myne-md/myne#423, deferred); **until that lands, history is retained unbounded, and this bullet is the disclosure of that.** Accepted as a deliberate, recorded escalation for v0.1's next-major successor; marketing/limits copy must not claim a migrated note's history stays on-device. (automerge-sync-migration, myne-md/myne#421.)
- **Multi-vault registry leaks vault count + last-unlock timing at rest, and, since myne-md/myne#411, the *name* of every vault this device has unlocked.** Multi-vault (MYND-165) stores per-vault directories at `vaults/<VaultId>/` plus a best-effort plaintext `vaults/registry.json` cache holding each vault's opaque `VaultId` + last-unlock timestamp. To an at-rest disk observer (A5) / local malware (A7), this reveals **how many vaults exist** and **per-vault last-activity timing**; the pre-unlock vault **picker** (shown only at ≥2 vaults) surfaces the same count + last-unlock to anyone at the unlock screen (shoulder-surfer), and, for any row carrying no cached name, the `Vault N` fallback whose `N` ranks vaults by ascending `VaultId` (myne-md/myne#411). A `VaultId` is a UUIDv7 and its leading bits are its creation time, so that same observer also reads each vault's **creation rank on this device**. That is **bounded to relative ordering**: a position is rendered, never a timestamp. It is still a widening, because creation ordering was previously visible only to an adversary who could read `vaults/<UUIDv7>/` on disk, and because the fallback it replaced numbered rows by their position in the last-unlock ordering, which disclosed nothing that ordering did not already show. It is also what the hide-vault-names option below leaves on screen, so no key destruction removes it. It does **not** reveal note content, or account identity (`VaultId` is a random UUIDv7, decoupled from `account_number_hash`; what its leading bits encode is a creation time, never anything about the account). The vault-names half of that sentence is superseded by the paragraphs below; the rest of it stands. Accepted for v0.1 under the same no-padding / at-rest-metadata posture as blob size + snapshot cadence above, and bounded: the `vaults/<id>/` directory count already exposes the vault count regardless of the cache, so the registry adds no *new* leak class; it is non-authoritative (rebuildable from the disk-scan), so a future revision could encrypt it under an app-level key, but that adds key-management surface for marginal benefit. (`decisions.md` 2026-06-09 "Vault disk layout"; SYNC-7.)

  **The vault name is a separate exposure with its own bounds, and the sentence above does not cover it.** A vault's human-readable name is cached device-locally so the locked picker can show it after a relaunch, in a per-vault `MYL1` blob at the vault root sealed under a per-vault OS-keychain `wrap_key`. Only a vault this device has **unlocked at least once** is ever named: there is no key with which to read the label of one it has not, so the cache cannot imply knowledge the device does not have. `registry.json` itself is unchanged and still holds no name. Nothing here crosses **TB5**: the store is device-local, never synced, and structurally excluded from the `§9.11.3` backup allowlist, so no name reaches the server or a portable artefact.

  **The count's bounding argument above does NOT extend to names, and must not be reused for them.** That argument works because the `vaults/<id>/` directory listing already exposes the count. Directory names are random UUIDv7s and `vault.meta` carries no label field, so before this change a vault's name appeared **nowhere on disk outside its own AEAD envelope**. A name is also **user-authored text**, which the count and the timestamps never were. This is a new category of disclosure rather than a larger quantity of an old one, and it is recorded as one.

  **The user can take it back, and what that is worth has four limits worth stating.** The device-wide [hide vault names](glossary.md#hide-vault-names) option destroys every per-vault key and removes every `MYL1` file, which is cryptographic erase, the same posture `decisions.md` 2026-05-07 fixed for note deletion when it rejected platform-specific secure-delete. **First limit:** that protects against someone who reads the medium *afterwards*. A full-system snapshot taken while names were enabled carries both the ciphertext and the keychain, and destroying the key later does not reach that copy. **Second limit:** the cache is device-local and does not sync, so renaming a vault on one device leaves the old name on every other device's lock screen until that vault is unlocked there, and a locked device cannot learn otherwise. **Third limit:** the picker is a **pre-unlock** surface, so anyone who can see a locked device's screen reads the name of every vault this device has unlocked, and no key destruction reaches that. Erase acts on the medium; it does not act on a screen somebody has already read. Only enabling the option **before** a name is ever cached prevents that observer from seeing one at all, which is why the option is not developer-gated (`decisions.md` 2026-08-15). This is the exposure the feature deliberately creates, and it is the same observer the count and last-unlock sentence above already names. **Fourth limit:** the erase is durable, the suppression is not. The option's own state is a device-local **plaintext** JSON file (`<app_config_dir>/vault-names.json`, `{"hidden": bool}`) of the same class as `tor-routing.json`, outside any AEAD envelope, so the "Local prefs replay" bullet below does **not** bound it: that bullet's bound rests on AEAD verification and the `account_number_hash` AAD binding, and this file carries no MAC and no vault binding. An adversary with disk write can set it back to `false`; the write path re-reads the flag on every unlock and every rename, so the next unlock resumes caching names and mints a fresh keychain key for a user who asked that none ever be written. What was already erased stays erased, because that was a key destruction rather than a setting. Detection parity with that bullet does hold: the Privacy row reads the same file, so a reverted flag is visible on the next view. All four limits are stated to the user rather than left to be discovered. **No adversary rating changes.** (`decisions.md` 2026-06-09 "Vault disk layout" and 2026-06-09 "Multi-vault in-app switcher + label management", both superseded on the label clause; myne-md/myne#411.)
- **Vault co-membership groups under the same disclosure as vault names (myne-md/myne#684).** One account can own multiple vaults, and a picker groups them by account. That relation (which vaults belong together) lives in one sealed device-local file, `<app_config_dir>/account.json`, encrypted under a device-local OS-keychain wrap key, readable while every vault is locked. Since `decisions/ui/0728` the record carries **no user-authored text**: an account has no name, and the heading a surface draws for a group is built from the names of the vaults inside it, which the rows beneath already show. To an at-rest disk observer (A5) / local malware (A7) this reveals the **co-membership relation**, under the same OS-credential carve-out as the cached vault names above: whoever can satisfy the OS account credential reads it; the vaults' master passwords do not guard it. It reveals no note content, no password, no phrase, no name, and no key, and it opens no ciphertext. The pre-unlock picker shows the grouping to the same shoulder-surfer §5 names, and the hide-vault-names option takes it off that screen too: enabling it destroys the account record and its key alongside the `MYL1` sweep, list calls answer empty while it is on, and mutations are refused while locked or hidden rather than applied invisibly. A platform backup carries the record's **ciphertext only**, never the grouping in the clear. The server sees one coarse consequence: `vault.meta` grows 345 to 417 bytes on first stamp and carries a per-device-varying sealed field afterwards, plus push churn on the upgrade: opaque bytes and timing, the blob-size class already accepted above, and no cross-vault linkability (each device seals its own account id under its own vault's key). **No adversary rating changes.**
- **Every per-vault device-local secret is destroyed with the vault it belongs to, and the device-wide ones deliberately are not (myne-md/myne#761).** Vault deletion's post-delete teardown destroys each secret scoped to the erased vault that this device keeps **outside** the vault directory, and the rule is a class rather than a call: a per-vault device-local secret is either destroyed on erase or declared independent of the vault's lifetime with the reason, while a secret shared by every vault on the device must NOT be destroyed by erasing one of them. The members today are the sealed-label wrap key (`md.myne.vaultlabel`, destroyed since myne-md/myne#411), the device-local person-identity carrier (`<app_config_dir>/person-vault-carriers/`, destroyed by its own delete since myne-md/myne#792), and the item the **owner quick-unlock biometric wrap key** and the **member-vault biometric wrap key** share (`md.myne.quickunlock`, account `hex(account_number_hash):vault_id`, destroyed since this change; nothing removed it before, which is audit run-1 lead 10). The erase runs **after** the vault directory is gone and only when the delete proceeded, so a refused delete leaves a surviving vault's credential intact (on a full delete the blob that consumes the key went with it; on a `PartialDelete` some files resisted the unlink and the erase still runs, because the user asked for the vault to be destroyed, the password path is untouched, and a retry finishes the sweep); the keychain delete is best-effort but not silent (a failure is logged with a bounded reason and does **not** turn a completed erase into an error a retry would answer `VaultNotFound` to), and what survives a failed delete on the ordinary `Ok(())` arm is one random 32-byte secret whose ciphertext was removed with the vault. **Out of the class, each for the same structural reason:** `md.myne.captures` and `md.myne.shareimport` mint one device-wide account (`device-wrap-key`) because an item is enqueued before anyone knows which vault will drain it, so erasing one vault must not make another's queued items unreadable; the account-label record (AS20, `md.myne.vaultlabel`'s reserved `account-record`) names co-membership ACROSS vaults and is erased only by the hide-vault-names sweep; and the person keypair's phrase-sealed file is vault-independent by design. **Three residues are declared rather than destroyed, and the decision entry names all three.** The **pre-vault-scoping account-only keychain item** is never deleted by an erase path, because on a twin install (a `KeepBoth` restore, or joining an account this device already holds) it may be the SIBLING vault's live credential; it is left in place: the read-side migration re-stores the key under the vault-scoped name but deliberately leaves this one alone (it cannot tell a twin's live credential from a stale one), so it survives as a second copy of the same wrap key. And **`vault_repair`**, which finishes an interrupted delete, runs only when `vault.meta` is byte-absent, so the `account_number_hash` half of the address cannot be derived there and the item cannot be addressed. Third, a vault whose `vault.meta` is **byte-present but unparseable** (a state the delete gate admits, because it tests byte-presence rather than parseability) yields no `account_number_hash`, so the item is not addressed and the only trace is the bounded `account_number_hash_unavailable` log line. None is an exemption from the class rule; all three are stated so "every per-vault secret" does not read as exhaustive. **On a target with no keychain the erase is a no-op and says so** rather than implying a cleanup that cannot happen: every **per-vault** keychain-backed device-local secret in this crate is Apple-only (the device-wide share-import key is the one Android-Keystore-held exception, and it is deliberately outside this class; the device signing key is the second device-wide secret and is outside it for the same reason, with its own four-rung storage ladder recorded in the per-device-token bullet below, myne-md/myne#938), the Settings copy says where Myne stores such a key and that it asks the keychain to delete it rather than promising the key is gone, and the user guide is updated in the companion change (`myne-website#96`) rather than already repeating it. Three non-erase paths destroy the same item because they remove its only consumer: disabling the member's biometric fast unlock (and the attempt-cap wipe), replacing a biometric credential with a PIN or password, and the root-replacement migration and its peer-side promotion (myne-md/myne#761). **No adversary rating changes**: A5's existing quick-unlock and label carve-outs are unchanged, and this closes a place where the erase path's own post-delete contract was incomplete rather than a place where ciphertext was readable. (`design-notes/security/0709`.)

- **Scheduled task reminders hand pending times and their count to the operating system (MYND-618).** Delivering a reminder while Myne is closed requires the **operating system** to hold the registration, which means the pending reminder set leaves the AEAD envelope and sits in OS-owned storage in plaintext. **Scope as shipped (`decisions.md` 2026-07-30, narrowed 2026-08-29):** everything in this entry describes the **OS-registration tier**, and **no client ships it today**. The desktop delivers reminders only while it is running, so it hands no registration to any operating system. Mobile has the plugin and the ACL grant, but the pass that would reconcile the vault's tasks against what the OS holds has **no production caller** (`scripts/check-unwired.mjs`), so its registration set is empty too and the client no longer says otherwise (`decisions.md` 2026-08-29, myne-md/myne#508). **None of the exposure below currently applies to any build.** It is recorded rather than deleted because both clients are one wiring change away from it: the desktop's native adapters stay gated behind the blocked delivery measurement, mobile's pass stays gated on acquiring a caller, and the entry applies unchanged, with no re-rating, on whichever change lands first. Three observers see it: the **at-rest device adversary (A5)**, **local malware (A7)**, and — because a notification can surface on a locked screen — a **shoulder-surfer at the lock screen**. What leaks is the **times** of pending reminders, their **count**, and therefore the length of the scheduling horizon. What does **not** leak is task text or note content: the notification body is **content-free by default**, carrying no task description, and the registration payload carries no note identifier a reader could resolve. The leak is bounded on four axes: the horizon is **short** (only reminders within it are ever registered), and since myne-md/myne#508 it is user-adjustable only **downward** from the seven-day default, which is also the clamp ceiling, so every reachable horizon is no longer than the one this entry accepts, the registration set is **never synced** and **never backed up** (it is device-local OS state, rebuilt from the vault on unlock), the **server boundary is untouched** — the sync server sees nothing of this and **AS6** (encrypted user-meaningful timestamps) stays intact — and the whole surface exists only while the feature is enabled. The nearest precedent is the **multi-vault registry** bullet above: also plaintext at rest, also a count plus timing, also naming the shoulder-surfer, and likewise **not** a downgrade of the headline at-rest rating — qualifying that rating once per feature would, after a few features, make it meaningless. **No adversary rating changes.** The **Linux gap** — no equivalent OS pre-registration surface, so reminders fire only while Myne is running — is classified as a **usability limitation, not a security one**: a platform that holds no registration leaks nothing, so the gap is strictly the safer side of this trade. **Mobile (MYND-619) inherits this posture unchanged and adds two asymmetries, both on the *acting* side rather than the *leaking* side.** The registration surface is identical — the same content-free body, the same short horizon, the same `{identifier, local instant}` payload and nothing else — and the identifier handed to the platform is **16 random bytes minted per registration**, so nothing in the OS store resolves to a note even for a reader holding it; there is **no push service and no push token anywhere in the mobile path**, so **TB5 stays untouched** and no per-device liveness signal is created. **(1) On Android, part of the pending set is held in a file Myne itself writes.** The platform exposes no way to enumerate an app's registered alarms, so the client keeps its own `{identifier → instant}` record in app-private storage — both to answer "what is pending" and to re-register after a restart, since Android drops every pending alarm on reboot and on app update. The *content* is exactly what this entry already records (pending times, their count, the horizon length) and it carries no note identifier or task text; what differs is the *holder* — the app rather than the operating system — so on Android the exposure does not end when the OS notification store is cleared. iOS holds the whole set itself and needs no such record. **(2) Acting on a tap is heavier than on the desktop, and heaviest on Android.** A tap on a locked device cold-launches the app; the identifier is latched **in memory only**, never written to disk, and consumed exactly once after unlock — the deep-link ingress pattern already recorded for the widgets, and a tap that arrives with nothing listening is dropped rather than queued, because the only alternative to dropping is a file. On desktop and iOS a quick-unlock provider can stand between the tap and the task; **Android has no quick-unlock provider at all** (the shared provider is Apple-Keychain-backed and Android answers `HardwareUnavailable` until a Keystore provider lands), so a cold-start tap on an Android reminder lands on a **full master-password Argon2id unlock**. That is a **usability** asymmetry rather than a security downgrade — the heavier path is the one demanding *more* credential, not less — but it is the difference between "reminded me and I acted" and "reminded me and I could not". **No adversary rating changes.**
- **Sync timing leaks usage patterns.** The version counter changes when a client pushes; an observer can correlate counter increments with real-time activity. v1 does not implement timing randomization or cover traffic.
- **Note creation time is server-visible, durably, via the UUIDv7 blob id (AS6).** A note's id is a UUIDv7, its `.myn` filename is that id (`protocol.md` §9.1), and that same id is the note's sync wire key (`sync-protocol.md` §4.2). UUIDv7 embeds a millisecond Unix timestamp in its leading 48 bits, so the sync server's per-vault index *is* a per-note creation-time series: when the vault was started, how it grew, when the user wrote in bursts and when they stopped. This is **stored state, not a transient observation** — unlike the sync-timing item above, it survives in the server's index and is therefore compellable under A2. It is not a new leak class at rest (`protocol.md` §9.4 already accepts the filename as plaintext, so A5/A7 have always seen it); what §7 records here is that it reaches **TB5**, which the AS6 row previously said it did not. Accepted for v0.1. **Both code-side alternatives are major-version changes, not doc patches or near-term options:** switching note ids to UUIDv4 would *delete* `created_at` outright — the id is its only carrier, since `markdown-dialect.md` deliberately keeps a `created:` field out of frontmatter — and the `.myn` filename format is normative in `protocol.md` §9.1, so changing it is a §10.1 event; deriving an opaque per-note sync-id would need a keyed HMAC over the note UUID (the `attachments-id-hmac` shape, not the `catalog-sync-id` singleton shape), which contradicts `sync-protocol.md`'s normative "a note's key is its 16-byte UUID" and touches the §4.2 manifest key — and it would close only the TB5 half, leaving the on-disk filename leaking to A5/A7 exactly as before. **Memory entries are a third UUIDv7 series with the identical property and the identical acceptance** (`memories/<id-hex>`, `sync-protocol.md` §10.2.6): per-entry creation time at millisecond resolution, durably, in the server's per-vault index (AS6 row).
- **The release-signing key is a single point of compromise.** A leaked or coerced key lets an attacker publish a hash list that authenticates a malicious artifact, which defeats client-side encryption for anyone who downloads it during the window. **Two things this bullet claimed until 2026-08-25 were false** (entry 0534): the signing is not hardware-backed, and until 2026-10-07 there was no auto-update to push such a build through (entry 0481, TB7). **That second clause changed on 2026-10-07**: the update channel exists and signs with the SAME offline key (entry 0649), so a leaked or coerced release-signing key can now push a malicious update to the whole installed base, not only to whoever downloads inside the window. That is a real increase in the key's blast radius, and it is why its custody matters more than it did. What holds: the key is held offline and outside CI, build and signing are separate steps, and the hashes are cross-published on a second origin so a single compromised origin does not settle the question. Verification is manual and opt-in, so none of it protects a user who downloads and runs without checking. Cannot eliminate the risk.
- **No formal third-party audit in v1.** Compensating controls (Section 6) are a credible substitute, not equivalent. If the project's funding can one day carry a formal audit, that becomes a priority.
- **No mobile in v1.** Users on iOS/Android remain on substitute stacks (Obsidian + Cryptomator + Möbius). The audience that wants E2EE most acutely on mobile is unserved by Myne during Phase 1–2.
- **No plausibly-deniable encryption.** Coercion (A12) reveals the data. v1 makes no attempt at deniability; consider in v2+ only with caution — partial deniability schemes are easy to misimplement and easy to claim falsely.
- **Self-host operator log policy is out of our control.** The protocol prevents the server from seeing plaintext, but a malicious self-host operator can still log connection IPs, retain old ciphertexts after deletion, or correlate metadata. Self-host documentation will explicitly state which operator behaviors are *protocol-level* (cannot be done) versus *policy-level* (must be enforced by the operator).
- **Argon2id parameters on low-end mobile.** The shipped v0.1 parameters are `m=256 MiB, t=3, p=2` (`spec/protocol.md` §4.1) — four times the memory of the `m=64 MiB, t=5` draft figure this bullet used to quote, so the problem is heavier than it read, not lighter. 256 MiB is a lot to ask of a low-end Android device. Either accept slow logins on low-end phones or ship per-device parameter profiles encoded in the encrypted blob. Decision deferred until mobile work begins — bounded, though, not open-ended: `spec/protocol.md` §4.2.1 fixes the accepted cost range any per-device profile must land inside, and a client rejects a vault outside it before Argon2id runs.
- **Embedded-HTML render surface (MYND-209).** Rendering a curated safe subset of note-authored HTML in the WebView (TB2) is a deliberate, bounded XSS-surface addition that supersedes the prior "raw HTML → plain monospace, no XSS surface" posture (`decisions.md` 2026-06-08; `markdown-dialect.md` §8). The control is an ultra-tight DOMPurify allowlist (8 elements, `title`-only attribute) with rendering via `innerHTML` of **sanitized output only** — never raw note bytes; off-allowlist tags (`<script>`/`<iframe>`/`<img>`) and HTML inside code are not rendered. CSP (`script-src 'self'`, no `'unsafe-inline'`; `object-src 'none'`) is defense-in-depth, not the primary control. The threat is a crafted/imported note attacking the user on render; the residual risk is a DOMPurify bypass (mitigated by its large install base, single purpose, and exact-pin). **What a bypass can send outward, stated at the right fidelity (myne#383).** No remote *fetch* is possible (no `src`/`href` survives, and `connect-src` allows no off-origin destination). Remote *navigation* is a separate channel that CSP structurally cannot govern: `navigate-to` shipped in no engine, and `default-src` falls back only for fetch directives, so no policy written in `tauri.conf.json` could refuse `location = "https://…/?d=" + noteBody`. This clause previously read "so a bypass could not exfiltrate", which overstated the guarantee — the fetch half was closed and the navigation half was simply unexamined.
