also supersedes the Tor-specific pin claims in the A4 row and in the glossary.

- **A member's local unsynced content survives a group rotation only because the member re-keys it, and the epoch it commits is bounded by its pinned owner, not by the server** (`collab-b2b-sessions`, `decisions/protocol/0661` + `0708`). Two honest limits, stated rather than mitigated. **(1)** When an owner removes a member, the group key and the vault `itemsKey` rotate. The owner re-keys every blob it holds, but a member's device may hold notes, attachments and snapshots the server has never seen; those per-entry wraps are sealed under the retired `itemsKey` and there is no server copy to re-pull. The member therefore re-keys its own local content, crash-safely and idempotently, at the next credential unlock, which is the first moment the key that sealed those blobs is in hand (the adoption installs the tier first and defers the re-key; the session cannot read pre-rotation blobs until that unlock, and nothing is lost): an interrupted re-key is rolled forward from a device-local journal on the next unlock, never silently dropped, and a device-local `vault.content-epoch` marker distinguishes "keys installed, blobs still retired" from "both landed" so a retry cannot double-apply. What this does NOT cover is a member that never runs the re-key at all (an old client, or a device whose content was authored and then abandoned before the rotation): its local unsynced blob is unrecoverable, and nothing here can reconstruct it, because the retired key is gone with the epoch. **(2)** A member holds no `root_key`, so it cannot authenticate a rotated `vault.meta` itself. It commits a new epoch ONLY after its own delivered rotation package opens at exactly that epoch against the owner public half PINNED at accept (`decisions/protocol/0675`); a strictly-greater epoch the server mints without a matching, verifying package is HELD, not written. That is a real refusal rather than a delay precisely because the pin is out-of-band, and the bound it carries is the same one the accept ceremony already rests on: a member who never pinned a genuine owner code has nothing to verify against, and a server that substitutes the owner material is refused rather than trusted. The epoch write is irreversible (a genuine lower epoch is then refused as a rollback forever), which is why the hold exists. The delivery outbox is device-local and never synced, and its PACKAGES are opaque, recipient-sealed frames: no party without the recipient's key can open one. **It is not opaque as a file, and saying so would understate it.** It is plain JSON at the vault root holding the account hash, both epochs, the retired epoch's sync-ids, the removed member's `person_id` and one `person_id` per remaining recipient, in the clear. A `person_id` is a vault-independent pseudonym (`SHA-256("myne.person-id|" || phrase)[:16]`), not a name, so what an at-rest observer learns is the membership set and which pseudonym was removed, plus the epoch timeline; no word of content and no key. Before the first successful POST it is also the ONLY copy of those packages, so the reason it is safe to keep is the per-recipient seal, not the server already holding them. The vault root is inside the platform backup the encrypted vault is deliberately part of, so this metadata rides that backup in the clear; the packages themselves remain sealed. No adversary rating changes: the AEAD-sealed roster in `vault.meta` already records the same membership to any party holding the vault key, and this adds a pseudonymous, plaintext copy on the device that already holds `sync-state.json`'s `device_id`. No adversary rating, asset, key, magic or AAD role changes.

- **The app-store listing is a publication surface, and nothing published there can be retracted (myne-md/myne#896; `decisions/ui/0714`).** The store listing images and their caption copy are authored here and uploaded to three third-party stores. What is depicted is a render of a shipped mobile screen inside a device frame, so the frame makes the same claims the app makes and is bound by the same rules: no frame may depict a flow a mobile user cannot reach (`scripts/check-store-publishable.mjs`), and a frame whose claim the phone cannot deliver is a defect of the listing rather than a marketing choice. **No vault-derived byte crosses this surface**: the account number and the recovery-phrase specimen a frame draws are pinned synthetic literals in `mobile/src/components/misc.tsx`, held by `scripts/check-store-frame-identifiers.mjs` over the story screens, their import closure and the caption data, so a second value reaching a frame fails the build. That guard exists because the account number is a bearer identifier for account possession (§5) and a published image is the one place it could land that cannot be withdrawn. **The asymmetry is the row's content**: every other surface in this document is assessed for what an observer learns, and this one is assessed for what Myne states, permanently, under its own name, before a user installs anything. Two consequences are recorded rather than mitigated. A mistake is not correctable by a later release, only by a store edit of a live listing; and the credited claim is a product claim, so copy that exceeds §4 or §5 is a false statement about the product rather than an over-wide security claim. A9 (compromised distribution) covers the store origin's integrity, not the listing's accuracy. No adversary or asset rating changes, no key material leaves the device, and no trust boundary moves.
- **An erased attachment's display metadata survives in the conversation that sent it (myne-md/myne#913).** A message that carried files persists `{id, name, contentType}` on its own turn so the transcript can name what a question was about after the composer's chips are gone (`protocol.md` §9.24.2). Deleting the attachment rewrites the CATALOG row into a scrubbed tombstone and crypto-erases the blob, and it does not reach into conversation bodies: the filename and MIME hint stay inside that conversation's own sealed `MYJ1` body, which is synced and backed up. This does not contradict §11.4 rule 1 as that rule is scoped (it governs the catalog and the blob plane, and it is the sentence that matters for a synced artefact ABOUT the file), but the difference is real and is stated rather than left to be discovered: the name of a deleted file remains readable to anyone who can open the conversation, until the conversation is deleted. A chip is the MESSAGE's record of what it carried, not a claim that the file is still there: pressing one opens a reader that refuses an id the store no longer has, and the chip itself does not query the catalog (there is no per-id existence command, and `catalog_list` deliberately omits chat-scoped rows). The exposure class is the filename the user themselves sent, not the bytes, and the control is conversation deletion, which already exists.

- **On a shared (`NTV6`) vault, `scope: "chat"` is a LISTING fact, not a confidentiality boundary (myne-md/myne#913).** An attachment pasted into a conversation is stored exactly like any other: the blob is sealed under the vault's `itemsKey` and its catalog row under the group-derived catalog key, so **every member can decrypt the file's bytes and read its filename, content type and size**, while the conversation that carried it is person-private (a member's in their own `MYY5` profile). The marker keeps the row out of the library listing; it does not keep the file from a co-member. This is the group tier's stated posture (`decisions/protocol/0593`: a member can read, copy, write and tamper with everything the keys open) and it is named here because the UI signal points the other way: a file absent from the library reads as a file that is as private as the conversation it was sent in, and it is not. Against the SERVER nothing changes (AS7: ciphertext only), so no trust-boundary rating moves; the reader newly enabled is a co-member already inside TB5. **The cleanup sweep takes this seriously in the other direction**: because no session can enumerate every participant's conversations, a membership-bearing or `NTV6` vault refuses both the unused list and the unused sweep outright (`ConversationsNotProvable` / `ConversationScanFailed`) rather than offering a file another person may still be using.

- **A view manifest crosses no trust boundary, and deception is the residual it leaves (myne-md/myne#922; `decisions/security/0727`).** A `.myneview` file is **data**: one canonical JSON object holding a saved view's definition plus the version that names it, exported by the app and imported through a file dialog the user opened. It carries no code, no formula, no remote reference and no wildcard, and it can only ask the host for operations the host already performs for the user on that device. That is why there is **no new TB row and no new adversary row**: the file enters inside TB2/TB3 through a path the user chose, it is parsed by the host that already holds the vault plaintext, and unlike the clipper (TB10) or the web client's code delivery (TB11) it is not a separately distributed artifact that executes. **The residual is deception**, and it is a real one: an author can name a view one thing and write a definition that does another, and nothing in an unsigned file can prove otherwise. Three properties bound it rather than close it. The **capability summary** is computed from the definition itself (its columns, its cover property, its grouping, its sort key, every property its filter references, and its declared `write_fields`) instead of being declared alongside it, so an author cannot understate what their view reads and there is no wildcard to write. Approval is **per device** and keyed on the version, so a view that arrives by sync or by import is visible and refuses to run until somebody at that device has seen that summary and agreed, and an update asks again. Writes are **host-owned and closed**: only a property named in `write_fields` can be written, the three markers the host manages (`archived`, `trashed`, `deleted`) are refused outright so no view can hide notes, and each write re-checks the approval and the field's expected value before it touches the disk. What the summary cannot do is state intent: it says what THIS definition reads and may write, and a view that selects the whole vault says exactly that, so a user can still approve something whose purpose they misunderstood. A view also **never receives or displays a note's body**. It reads properties through the same derived index search uses, and the renderer treats every value as a text node, with a cover image resolved through `attachment://` only. The one body-derived signal a definition can carry is a full-text `search` condition in its filter, which answers only whether a note matches: that is disclosed in the summary as part of what the view selects, and it is the shape a reader should recognise, because it is the closest this surface comes to reading note text. **The attack class this design turns into a no-op is worth naming rather than assuming**: a shared vault whose configuration directory executes payloads on open, where handing somebody a folder is handing them code. There is no analogue here, because the artefact is data, it is validated strictly (an unknown top-level key is refused by its name, a reserved marker is refused, a wrong type or an absent version is refused), it runs nothing, and approval cannot be inherited from another device. **No adversary rating changes**, and the one asymmetry worth recording is that the guarantee is about **capability, not intent**: this is a bound on what a hostile definition can do, not a claim that a definition is what it says it is.

- **An external MCP client can read everything in an OPEN vault except the folders the user left out, through the running desktop app, and the approval is DEVICE state rather than vault state (myne-md/myne#923).** `myne-mcp` is a sidecar that speaks MCP over stdio to a client the user installed (Claude Desktop and its kin) and holds no key: every tool call is a request to the running desktop over its local command socket, which answers `vault_locked` while the vault is locked and `app_not_running` when the app is closed, so no call reaches a sealed vault and none reaches one that is open behind another account. **The new party is the client process, not a new key path**, and it sits outside this document's trust boundary in a way a co-member does not: whatever it reads it may keep, log, or forward to its own vendor, so a tool result is egress the user consented to rather than data Myne controls. Four bounds hold. **The scope is a DENY list**: the folders the user leaves out on the approval screen, everything else readable, re-resolved host-side on every call from the stored grant, so a connection never names its own scope and a caller cannot widen it. That exclusion set is the union of the connection's own list and `ai_excluded_folders` plus `cli_excluded_folders`, because a folder the user already kept out of Myne's own AI and out of the terminal client is one they have excluded twice about the same vault, and a third list that starts empty would hand it over without asking again (`decisions/security/0753`; `scope::quiet_folders` is the one home for the union). The union only ever narrows, and it is counted into the approval screen's own numbers, so the count a person approves is the count a call is served under. **The write scope is an ALLOW list beside that deny list, and the asymmetry is the decision** (`decisions/ai/0752`): the folders named at approval, nothing else, **exact match with no subtree**, re-resolved host-side on every call, and an empty list means write nowhere, the inverse of an empty exclusion list, which means the whole vault. A folder created after the approval is therefore not writable until the user names it, because a subtree grant would be a permission nobody clicked. The attachment between vault and client is per connection, one row per declared client name, and revocable from the same screen. And no key material crosses: the sidecar never holds `root_key`, and the tools return note text and metadata rather than ciphertext or keys. **The direction of the deny list is a consent decision with a cost on each side, and both are stated rather than left to be inferred.** A folder created after the approval is COVERED, because the user said "everything except this"; the allow-list model this feature first shipped with froze a folder set instead, so a new folder started outside it and the section offered a one-click "add what you made since" row. There is no such row now, because there is nothing left out: the reach of an approval grows with the vault, and the approval screen says so in words at the moment the decision is made. What the deny list does not do is widen by accident: an exclusion naming a folder that was renamed or deleted excludes nothing (it was already excluding nothing it could reach), and an exclusion that DOES name an existing folder takes its whole subtree. **An allow-list grant written by the earlier build is REFUSED rather than reinterpreted**, with its own `reapproval_required` reason: the two models are both arrays of folder paths, so reading one as the other turns "only `private/`" into "everything except `private/`", and an upgrade must not be the act that widens a grant. **The asymmetry worth stating is where the grant lives, and this paragraph said the opposite until 2026-10-28.** `mcp_connections` is a `DeviceLocal` field of `vault.prefs` (`sync-protocol.md` §10.2, `core/src/plugins/sync/prefs_classification.rs`): it is encrypted, its VALUE is never adopted from another device and is never inherited, so an approval given on one device is not in force on any other and a second machine is asked again (its bytes still travel inside the encrypted `vault.prefs` blob like every device-local field's, which is why the wording here is "not adopted" rather than "does not leave the device"). That is the `ai_enabled` precedent and the reason is the one this document records twice already: a synced consent record would let a newly enrolled device inherit an approval nobody there gave, and here it would also name folder paths that two devices can disagree about after a merge. The consequence worth stating plainly is the other half of it: revoking on one device does not revoke elsewhere, so a user who approved a connection on a laptop and a desktop has two approvals to take away, and the field is per device precisely so neither can speak for the other. **What the write path adds to that paragraph, and what it costs.** **This build dispatches four of the five write verbs the grant can bound, and the fifth is absent rather than refused** (`decisions/ai/0752` states the split): `note_create`, `note_append`, `note_edit` and `note_move` write, and a `tools/call` naming `note_set_field` is answered as version skew (`invalid_request`) rather than as a permission decision, which is deliberate. Setting one frontmatter field means splicing a value into a YAML block, and the only implementation of that splice in this repository is the dialect's TypeScript; writing a second one in Rust would be a second opinion about quoting, multi-line values and inline comments, so the host declines the verb until the splice has a Rust home rather than growing a divergent copy. The rest of the closed list is absent for the reason it always was: the five verbs the grant can bound are create, append, edit, move and set a field, and nothing else: no verb deletes a note, renames a folder, changes an attachment, or reaches the file system, because an operation whose result the user cannot see, on a container they think of as a place rather than a document, is not something a name-and-scope grant can authorise. **Every write is a host-validated intent, never a request the client composes and sends**: the host re-reads the target from disk, checks the grant against the live blob, checks the note has not changed since the client saw it, applies the change atomically, and records a pre-write snapshot in the same history the user's own edits enter, so the undo is theirs and not the client's. A write grant with no per-write prompt would otherwise be a promise nobody can check. **An out-of-scope write is refused and becomes a review card on this device** (`mcp_write_requests`, §9.5.2), not a silent failure: the row names the connection, the folder, the verb and the client's own sentence, nothing has been applied while it exists, and accepting it extends that one grant by exactly that one folder. A plain refusal would be answered by a client retrying, and a client retrying is indistinguishable from a client probing, so the card is the cheaper answer to the same event. Two further bounds ride this: the queue is `DeviceLocal`, so a proposal is decided by the person at the machine where the attempt happened and is never inherited by another device, and it holds **no authority**: accepting re-reads the grant as it is at that moment, so a replayed blob can at most re-ask a question the user already answered. **The egress bound is unchanged and now cuts both ways**: what the client reads it may keep, and what it writes it may have copied from anywhere, so a note a connection wrote is content the user's own model saw. What remains outside this document's guarantees is the same thing as before, the user's client itself (A7-class software they installed); the scope, the closed verb list and the snapshot are the bounds, and none of the three is enforced by the client.

**No adversary or asset rating changes and no trust boundary moves**: the reader is a process the user started, on the machine that already holds the plaintext, and it reaches the app over the same local command socket the `myne` CLI uses. What this does not bound is the user's own client, which is A7-class software the user installed; the consent screen, the read scope and the named write scope are what bound it, and the closed verb list is the sharpest of the three because no verb outside it exists to be called: a delete, a folder rename or an attachment change has no name a client can send, so the host has nothing to scope-check and refuses at the dispatch table. What the dispatched verbs add is the surface behind those bounds, and three properties hold it: every write re-reads the note from disk before deciding, an edit or a move is refused when the note has changed since the client saw it and when a live co-editing session owns it, and every write **to an existing note** takes its restore point BEFORE the first byte changes and is refused outright if that restore point cannot be taken, so a change a connection made is one the user can always put back. **A note CREATE is the one exception and it is arithmetic rather than an oversight**: its pre-write state is the note's absence, which a snapshot store that copies an existing blob cannot express, and the id does not exist until the write returns. A created note is undone by deleting it, and no verb in this surface deletes, so the only party who can remove one is the person who owns the vault. A grant also carries **no expiry**: an approval is standing until it is revoked, which is recorded here rather than left to be inferred, because a deadline on the write half would turn a client's write into a race that client cannot see (`decisions/security/0753`).

## 8. Comparison vs. Adjacent Products

A side-by-side comparison of how Myne's threat model differs from the closest alternatives. Drawn from each product's published documentation, source code, and public security incidents as of early 2026.

| Axis | Myne (v1) | Notesnook | Standard Notes | Joplin (E2EE on) | Obsidian Sync |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Account identity | Self-generated number, no email | Email | Email | Email | Email |
| Server can read note content | No | No | No | No | No (claimed; closed-source client) |
| Server can read note titles | No | No | No | **Yes** (titles + IDs leak per issue #6456) | Unknown / closed source |
| Server can read folder/structure | No | Partial | Partial | Yes | Unknown / closed source |
| Server can read user-meaningful timestamps | Partial — `updated_at` no; `created_at` yes, via the UUIDv7 blob id (AS6) | Yes | Yes | **Yes** | Unknown / closed source |
| IP address logging | None | Per provider policy | Per provider policy | Per chosen sync target | Per provider policy |
| Open-source client | Yes | Yes | Yes | Yes | **No** |
| Open-source server | Yes | Yes | Yes (server-side) | Yes (Joplin Server) | **No** |
| Self-host option | Yes (first-class) | Yes | Yes | Yes | **No** |
| Independent published audit | Compensating controls only | Planned, none yet | Trail of Bits 2021 | Informal only | None published |
| Recovery model | Master password + **either** a 24-word vault phrase (`MYV1`/`NTV5`/`NTV6`) **or** the identity phrase (`NTV7`, which has no vault phrase at all; myne-md/myne#911) | Email + master password | Master password (no recovery) | Master password (no recovery) | Master password + email |
| Encryption on by default | **Always** (no plaintext mode for the vault) | Always | Always | **Off by default** (must enable) | Yes |
| Plugin model | None in v1; future API capability-scoped | None | Editor swaps | Plugins yes, **broken on mobile** | Yes (full vault read access — privacy hole) |
| Markdown files plaintext on disk after decryption | **Yes** | No (proprietary store) | No | Sort of (SQLite + attachments) | Yes (no E2EE on disk) |

The differentiator is the combination, not any single column: **plain-markdown-on-disk + no email + encrypted titles + an encrypted `updated_at` + no IP logs + open-source full stack + first-class self-host**. No incumbent matches all of these. The timestamp column is stated as `updated_at` rather than "timestamps" because `created_at` rides the UUIDv7 blob id and is server-visible (AS6) — the row is still favourable against every comparator, and it has to be true.

---

## Decisions Log (this document)

A snapshot of choices made during the v0.1 draft. Mirrored to `decisions.md`.

**Resolved**

- **Operator console panel (operator-console row + TB4), added 2026-09-28.** `decisions.md` entry 0621, myne-md/myne#787. A browser cannot dial the operator console's Unix domain socket, so the console gained a **network-bindable, read-only** TCP listener (`MYNE_ADMIN_PANEL_ADDR`, off by default). Two things this record exists to hold: the **access criterion widened** (whoever can reach the bind address, not whoever can run commands as the operator) while the **stored history did not** (it projects state already persisted and keeps nothing), and the account-hash roster it displays is a **set of credentials** for the pre-auth `GET /vault.meta` route rather than a privacy-free identifier. TB4 gains the exception: the listener terminates no TLS, so a non-loopback bind puts the roster on the wire in the clear. The claim is recorded as a widening, not as "no boundary expansion", because the clause that used to ground that phrasing was deleted.
- 13 adversary classes catalogued (A1–A13) with explicit Strong / Mitigated / Best-effort / Out-of-scope status per class. The roster has grown since, and **§1 is the list of record** — read it rather than a count restated here; each extension has its own dated bullet below.
- **Browser-clipper channel (TB10 + A14), added 2026-06-20.** The keyless native-messaging clipper (`browser-extension` epic, MYND-442) extends the original A1–A13 / TB1–TB9 catalogue with **A14** (compromised-extension-build, 🔒 Mitigated) and **TB10** (browser↔desktop clip channel). Save-to-root + no-queue mean no vault structure (AS3/AS5) and no plaintext-at-rest ever reach the browser; the extension is keyless. (`decisions.md` 2026-06-20.)
- **Web bundle delivery channel (TB11 + A15), added 2026-07-26.** The Phase 3 web client (`phase3-web-client`) adds **A15** (hostile delivery of the client code itself, ⚠️ Best-effort, web only) and **TB11** (the origin serving the bundle), and splits two previously absolute rows **per client**: A11 stays 🛡️ Strong on native clients and becomes ⚠️ Best-effort on web, because a served bundle grants the operator exactly the extra access the Strong claim denies; A4 stays 🔒 Mitigated on native and becomes ⚠️ Best-effort on web, because a browser exposes neither TLS-exporter material nor certificate pinning to page JavaScript. A6 is noted weaker on web (page-scoped idle, throttled background timers). Filed as an adversary and a boundary rather than a §7 known limitation on purpose: filing a normative guarantee under "known limitations" downgrades it by bookkeeping instead of by decision. Self-hosting removes A15 for the self-hoster's own use and not for anyone else on their instance. (`decisions.md` 2026-07-26.)
- **Recovery-phrase phishing and extension DOM read (A18 + A19), added 2026-08-15.** `decisions.md` 2026-08-15, entry 0514, reverses rule 1 of the 2026-07-26 web capability set: phrase display (creation) and phrase entry (recovery) ship on the web client, while the phrase as a *device-enrolment credential* stays absent there. **A18** (credential phishing, ⚠️ Best-effort, web only) is filed because the old rule was not a control but a **structural inference** — no served page ever asked for the phrase, so any page asking was self-evidently fake — and shipping phrase entry spends that inference permanently. The attack needs no bundle compromise, so it is neither A15 nor A4, and none of the delivery-channel controls touch it; what remains is containment (one situation only, enforced by keeping enrolment's phrase grid out of the artefact) rather than prevention. **A19** (extension host permissions, ❌ Out of scope, web only) is filed separately because it is a different party with a different mechanism: an extension the user installed reads the DOM directly, which no CSP governs and no page can detect. A19 **predates** this change (the master password has always been typed into that DOM) and is named now because the phrase, the one credential no password change rotates, has joined it. Neither row is folded into A14, whose subject is Myne's own keyless clipper. (`decisions.md` 2026-08-15, entry 0514; issue `myne-md/myne#174`.)
- **AS6 split into `updated_at` and `created_at`, 2026-08-04 (amends the "Threat-model AS6 unchanged" clause of `decisions.md` 2026-05-07).** AS6 read "**Not visible.**" for both halves of the user-meaningful-timestamp asset. That is true of `updated_at`, which is sealed inside the AEAD envelope, and false of `created_at`, which has no encrypted carrier at all: a note's creation time is the timestamp embedded in its UUIDv7, that UUID is its `.myn` filename (`protocol.md` §9.1), and that same UUID is its sync wire key (`sync-protocol.md` §4.2) — so the server's per-vault index is a durable per-note creation-time series, compellable under A2. The at-rest half was never hidden (`protocol.md` §9.4 accepts the filename as plaintext); what was wrong was the claim that it stops at TB5. Amended rather than rewritten, per this log's own discipline: the AS6 row, the A1 and A2 mechanism rows, the §8 comparison row and its differentiator sentence, the "no server-visible timestamps" subsection heading, and a new §7 limitation. No adversary rating changes and no code changes — both alternatives (UUIDv4 ids; a keyed per-note sync-id) are major-version events, and §7 records why. (`decisions.md` 2026-08-04; issue `myne-md/myne#361`.)
- A1 (passive cloud) and A2 (compelled lawful access) are both Strong-guarantee — the architectural commitment is "the server cannot disclose what it does not possess".
- A12 (coerced user) is explicitly Out of scope in v1. A6 (unlocked stolen device) was Out-of-scope in the v0.1 draft and is now **🔒 Mitigated** by auto-lock on idle (`decisions.md` 2026-05-30 "Auto-lock on idle"; MYND-121 + MYND-122) — key-zeroize + best-effort open-note teardown, exposure-window-narrowing, not a Strong guarantee against forensic RAM capture.
- A7 (malware), A10 (state-level), A13 (traffic analysis) are Best-effort, with the limits documented to prevent marketing overreach.
- **AS6 — User-meaningful timestamps are encrypted.** Server uses an opaque per-note version counter (sync-internal monotonic integer) for conflict detection; conflict resolution happens on the client after decryption. This is a sync-protocol commitment.
- **AS14 — No IP logging.** Hard architectural commitment. Operational logs and abuse mitigation must function on per-account counters without IP addresses.
- TB5 — hosted sync and self-hosted sync are the **same** trust boundary. The protocol does not give the operator more access in either case.
- TB7 — since 2026-10-07 there **is** an in-app auto-update, signature-gated against the offline key and never silently downgrading; it was retracted until 2026-08-15 (entry 0481) and reversed by `decisions/clients/0649`. An update is fetched and installed by the app, and the same offline key that signs the hash list signs the update artifacts.
- Compensating controls for the missing audit are codified: public spec, public threat model, public limits doc, reproducible builds (**web and server only** — not desktop, and not verifiable by an outsider while the source is private), minisign-signed and cross-published release hashes (**not** code-signed desktop installers), hash-pinned dependencies, SECURITY.md, security.txt, public bug bounty (acknowledgment-only acceptable in v1), spec-then-bounty cadence, pre-launch external review.
- **Hard line: no email recovery, ever.** If recovery-phrase UX support volume becomes unmanageable, the response is better in-product UX, not email-based recovery.

**Deferred**

- Argon2id parameter tuning on low-end mobile devices — decision blocked on Phase 3 mobile work. The cost *range* it must land inside is not deferred (`spec/protocol.md` §4.2.1); only the profile's position within it is.
- Blob-size padding / bucketing — deferred beyond v1; A13 remains Best-effort meanwhile.
- Sync-timing randomization / cover traffic — deferred beyond v1.
- Plausibly-deniable encryption against coerced-user (A12) — out of scope in v1; v2+ only with caution.
- Decrypted-memory wiping *beyond best-effort* for A6 (forensic-grade freed-page / swap / hibernation scrubbing) — deferred. Auto-lock on idle (`decisions.md` 2026-05-30) now delivers key-zeroize + best-effort open-note teardown (OS window-hide + WebView reload), moving A6 to 🔒 Mitigated; userspace-deterministic RAM scrubbing remains out of scope (A7/A10).

**Open risks**

- Release-signing key compromise (TB8) is the single largest unmitigated risk against A9. Holding the key offline and outside CI, separating build from signing, and cross-publishing the hashes on a second origin reduce it and do not eliminate it. **This line said "hardware-backed keys" until 2026-08-25 and there are none** (entry 0534). The Apple Developer ID is now owned and used for the notarized macOS build (since 2026-10-07); a Windows Authenticode certificate and a hardware token for the minisign key remain unbought. The project is in beta and this is deferred work rather than a decision against it, which is why the row stays ⚠️ Best-effort instead of moving to an accepted-risk list.
- Tauri's Cargo dependency surface plus the React UI layer's npm dependencies together form a non-trivial supply-chain attack surface; transitive compromise (A8) remains feasible despite hash pinning. The Vite + React choice (`decisions.md` 2026-04-26) keeps the npm side as small as practical, but it does not eliminate the surface.
- The "no formal audit" gap will widen if Notesnook publishes one before Myne does. Accept the risk; the compensating controls are the response.
- Self-host operator log policy is outside the protocol — documentation must surface the protocol-level vs. policy-level distinction so users understand which guarantees follow them when they switch operators.
